# OpenXcom3D manual

Version 1.15. OpenXcom3D is **OpenXcom Extended (OXCE)** with a first-person 3D battlescape: an engine add-on (`src/FirstPerson/`, with small hooks in the core) and a data mod (`OpenXcom3D`) that switches it on.

In short, with pictures: the [play guide](https://b0realis.github.io/OpenXcom3D/play.html) has [every key](https://b0realis.github.io/OpenXcom3D/play.html#keys), [every setting](https://b0realis.github.io/OpenXcom3D/play.html#settings) and how each plays, what's on the screen, and every feature. This manual has the whole story.

1. [Installing](#1-installing)
2. [Controls](#2-controls)
3. [The top bar and items](#3-the-top-bar-and-items)
4. [Settings](#4-settings)
5. [Fire modes per weapon (FPS-style shooting)](#5-fire-modes-per-weapon-fps-style-shooting)
6. [Rules for mods](#6-rules-for-mods)
7. [What you'll see](#7-what-youll-see)
8. [Performance](#8-performance)
9. [Known limits and ideas](#9-known-limits-and-ideas)
10. [For developers](#10-for-developers)
11. [Version history](#11-version-history)
12. [Thanks](#12-thanks)

## 1. Installing

You need your own copy of the original game data: *UFO: Enemy Unknown / X-COM: UFO Defense* and/or *X-COM: Terror From The Deep*, e.g. from Steam or GOG.

Step by step, for each system: the [play guide](https://b0realis.github.io/OpenXcom3D/play.html) (the downloads on Linux, Windows, the Steam Deck and macOS, and where your game data goes) and the [build guide](https://b0realis.github.io/OpenXcom3D/) (compiling it).

Add-on builds keep their own user and config folders, so an existing OXCE profile is never touched:

- the release's `user/` folder, when started with `play.sh` (or `play.bat` on Windows);
- `~/.local/share/openxcom3d` for the AppImage;
- `~/Library/Application Support/OpenXcom3D` for the macOS app;
- otherwise `~/.local/share/openxcom3d` and `~/.config/openxcom3d`.

### Option A: the prebuilt release (Linux x86-64)

Download it from the project's [Releases](https://github.com/b0realis/OpenXcom3D/releases) page, in either form:

- **`OpenXcom3D-<version>-x86_64.AppImage`**: a single file. Make it executable (`chmod +x OpenXcom3D-*-x86_64.AppImage`, or *Properties → Permissions → executable* in a file manager) and run it. Its profile is in `~/.local/share/openxcom3d`.
- **`OpenXcom3D-<version>-linux-x86_64.tar.gz`**: unpack it anywhere and run `./play.sh`. Its profile is the `user/` folder inside.

**Nothing needs installing.** The game comes with its own libraries: SDL 1.2 (sdl12-compat on SDL2), SDL_image and SDL_mixer in `lib/`, with SDL_gfx built in. All it takes from the system is glibc 2.34 or newer (2021 on: Ubuntu 22.04 and newer, Debian 12, Fedora 35 and newer, RHEL 9, Mint 21, every current Arch, Manjaro or other rolling system, and SteamOS), plus the display, sound and OpenGL every desktop has. SDL opens X11 or Wayland and PulseAudio, PipeWire or ALSA when the game starts. On a system older than glibc 2.34, build it from source (option B).

The archive holds:

```
bin/openxcom              the engine (OXCE 8.7.1 + OpenXcom3D)
lib/                      the SDL libraries it comes with
data/common, standard     OXCE's own data
user/mods/OpenXcom3D      the mod
user/options.cfg          a starting profile: UFO + OpenXcom3D, 1280x800 window
play.sh                   starts the game (finds the game data, says what's missing)
steamdeck/                the Steam Deck controller layout, and the script that sets it
licenses/                 the licences of the libraries in lib/
install.py, README.md, OpenXcom3D_manual.md, openxcom3d.patch, LICENSE.txt
```

#### The game data

`play.sh` (and the AppImage) finds the original games by itself the first time, and links them into the profile. It never copies or changes them. It looks, in this order, for:

1. a `UFO` or `TFTD` folder already in the profile (`user/UFO`, or `~/.local/share/openxcom3d/UFO` for the AppImage);
2. a `UFO` or `TFTD` folder in the game's folder, next to it, or in your home folder;
3. *X-COM: UFO Defense* and *X-COM: Terror From The Deep* in a Steam library. That covers Steam itself, installed natively, as a Flatpak or as a Snap, and every other library it lists, such as a second disk or the Steam Deck's SD card.

A game folder is the one with `GEODATA`, `GEOGRAPH`, `MAPS` and `UFOGRAPH` in it (Steam's are `XCom UFO Defense/XCOM` and `X-COM Terror from the Deep/TFD`). A new profile switches on the game that's there, UFO if both are. If neither is found, it says where to put them.

#### With the installer

```sh
python3 install.py
```

It asks for:

- the install folder (default `~/Games/OpenXcom3D`);
- the original UFO data folder;
- the TFTD data folder.

Common Steam locations are found on their own.

It then copies the game and its libraries, **links** the game data folders into the profile, writes `options.cfg` and creates `play.sh`. Without asking:

```sh
python3 install.py --target ~/Games/OpenXcom3D --ufo /path/to/UFO --tftd /path/to/TFTD --yes [--desktop]
```

`--desktop` also adds a menu entry.

#### By hand

Unpack the archive anywhere and run `./play.sh`. To use game data it doesn't find, link it into the profile first:

```sh
ln -s /path/to/UFO  OpenXcom3D-*-linux-x86_64/user/UFO
ln -s /path/to/TFTD OpenXcom3D-*-linux-x86_64/user/TFTD
```

#### Steam Deck

1. In **Desktop Mode**, download the archive (or the AppImage) and unpack it, for example into `~/Games`. Run `play.sh` once; double-click it and choose *Execute*, or start it from Konsole. If *X-COM: UFO Defense* or *Terror From The Deep* is installed through Steam, it is found by itself. Otherwise copy the game's folder next to `play.sh` as `UFO` (or `TFTD`).
2. For **Game Mode**: Steam → *Games* → *Add a Non-Steam Game to My Library* → *Browse*, set the file type to *All Files*, pick `play.sh` (or the AppImage) and add it. It's then in your library under *Non-Steam*.
3. The **controller layout**: after adding the shortcut, run `steamdeck/add_controller_layout.sh` once in Desktop Mode (from Konsole, or double-click → *Execute*). It closes Steam for a moment, adds the layout to Steam's templates, sets it for your OpenXcom3D shortcuts (it keeps a copy of Steam's file it changes, `configset_controller_neptune.vdf.before-openxcom3d`), and starts Steam again. Back in Game Mode, OpenXcom3D uses it; it can also be picked by hand under *Edit Layout → Templates → OpenXcom3D*. Run the script again after adding another OpenXcom3D shortcut, or after updating OpenXcom3D (to get the new version's layout). The layout is Valve's *Keyboard (WASD) and Mouse* template with this game's keys:

| Deck | Key | Does |
|---|---|---|
| Left stick, click | W A S D, K | walk and step sideways; kneel |
| Right trackpad, click | mouse, left button | look (or move the cursor); click |
| Right stick, click | mouse, left button | look more finely; click |
| R2 / L2 | left / right mouse button | fire (click) / cancel (with a free cursor: hold to look) |
| L1 | C | mouse look ↔ free cursor |
| R1 | F8 | isometric map ↔ 3D |
| A / B / X / Y | Space / R / J / I | jump / reload / next fire mode / inventory |
| D-pad up / down | Page Up / Page Down | fly up / down, ride a grav lift |
| D-pad right / left | Tab / G | next soldier / switch hand |
| D-pad left / right, held | Y / F | akimbo on/off / the flashlight on/off |
| Left trackpad, clicked: up / right / down / left | Z / X / V / O | snap / aimed / isometric map ↔ 3D (in a new profile; the auto shot where *Auto shot* is bound to V) / open the door |
| Left trackpad, clicked and held: left / right | , / . | snap turn 45° left / right |
| Menu (☰, right) / View (⧉, left) | Escape / H | the game's menu / classic HUD |
| View, held | P | 3D HUD on/off (on the map: the map camera) |
| L4 / R4, held | Left Shift + A / D | lean left / right (with the setting *Lean* on) |
| L5 / R5 | N / T | night vision / tactical flare |
| L5 / R5, held | L / Backspace | the suit light (the game's personal light) / end turn (held, so a press a little short never ends the turn) |

A button with something on a long press (held for 0.4 s) sends its own key when it's let go, a little later than the others. On the isometric map the left stick sends the game's own W, A and S (the special item, abort with a question first, stats) and X sends J (the kneel reserve): move the map with the trackpad there.

On the Deck a new profile plays fullscreen (1280×800, the Deck's own screen). Steam + X brings up the keyboard for anything else. Without the layout, Valve's *Keyboard (WASD) and Mouse* template works too, with F8 mapped to a spare button.

#### If it doesn't start

`play.sh` says what's wrong, in a window when it wasn't started from a terminal:

- *older than glibc 2.34*: build it from source (option B);
- *lacks libGL.so.1*: the system has no OpenGL library, which comes with the graphics drivers (`libgl1` on Debian and Ubuntu, `libglvnd` on Arch, `libglvnd-glx` on Fedora);
- *needs the data of the original game*: see [The game data](#the-game-data).

If the window closes at once with an X error about GLX, the system's OpenGL is broken. It runs without it like this:

```sh
SDL12COMPAT_OPENGL_SCALING=0 SDL_RENDER_DRIVER=software ./play.sh
```

### Option A on Windows: the prebuilt release (64-bit)

Download **`OpenXcom3D-<version>-windows-x86_64.zip`** from the [Releases](https://github.com/b0realis/OpenXcom3D/releases) page and unpack it somewhere of yours: Documents, the Desktop or `C:\Games`. Not *Program Files*: the game writes its saves and options into its `user` folder, and can't there. Then double-click **`play.bat`**.

**Nothing needs installing.** It runs on 64-bit Windows 10 and 11. The game comes with SDL 1.2 (sdl12-compat on SDL2) as `SDL.dll` and `SDL2.dll`, with SDL_image, SDL_mixer, SDL_gfx and the C++ runtime built in, and takes from Windows only what every Windows has. The game isn't signed, so Windows may warn the first time ("Windows protected your PC"): click *More info*, then *Run anyway*.

The zip holds:

```
play.bat                  starts the game (finds the game data, says what's missing)
bin\openxcom.exe          the engine (OXCE 8.7.1 + OpenXcom3D)
bin\SDL.dll, SDL2.dll     SDL 1.2 (sdl12-compat) on SDL2
data\common, standard     OXCE's own data
user\mods\OpenXcom3D      the mod
licenses\                 the licences of the libraries and of the compiler's runtime
README-Windows.txt, README.md, OpenXcom3D_manual.md, openxcom3d.patch, LICENSE.txt
```

`play.bat` looks for the games' data as `play.sh` does:

1. a `UFO` or `TFTD` folder already in `user\`;
2. a `UFO` or `TFTD` folder in the game's folder or next to it;
3. *X-COM: UFO Defense* and *X-COM: Terror From The Deep* in Steam: its own folder (from the registry) and every other library it lists, such as a second disk.

It links what it finds into `user\` with a directory junction, which needs no administrator and leaves the data as it is. On a disk that can't hold a junction (FAT32, exFAT, a network drive) it copies the data instead. The first time, it also writes the starting options: the game that's there (UFO if both), and a 1280×800 window if the screen has room for one (at least 1300×900), else the full screen. Your saves, the options and the log (`openxcom.log`) are in `user\`. Extra arguments go to the game: `play.bat -load mysave.sav`.

The Windows build is made on Linux with llvm-mingw (Clang for Windows), from the same sources as the Linux release, except sdl12-compat: 1.2.74 instead of 1.2.72, which crashes on Windows. **It's played under Wine** (11.17) here: the launcher in every case above, and battles in 3D in UFO and TFTD. On a real Windows (GitHub's), the [automatic builds](#automatic-builds) check every change: the game starts, and `play.bat` finds the games in Steam's libraries (a second library on another disk too), links them and starts the game. Nobody has played a battle on a real Windows machine yet, so reports are welcome.

### Option A on macOS: a first, untested build

The [automatic builds](#automatic-builds) make a macOS app on GitHub's Macs: **`OpenXcom3D-<version>-macos.zip`**, for Apple silicon and Intel, macOS 11 (Big Sur) and newer. Nobody here has a Mac: it has been built, checked and started there (the game says its version, natively and under Rosetta, and the app's starter sets up its profile), but never played. Reports are very welcome.

To get it: from the [latest release](https://github.com/b0realis/OpenXcom3D/releases/latest), take **`OpenXcom3D-<version>-macos.zip`** and unpack it. Between releases the latest run of [Build](https://github.com/b0realis/OpenXcom3D/actions/workflows/build.yml?query=branch%3Amain) has one too: under *Artifacts*, **macos-release** (a GitHub account is needed; each run keeps it for 30 days).

**Opening it the first time:** the app is signed ad hoc, not by Apple, so macOS stops it the first time. Open it once, then *System Settings → Privacy & Security → Open Anyway*; or in Terminal, `xattr -dr com.apple.quarantine /path/to/OpenXcom3D.app`. After that it opens like any other app.

**Nothing needs installing.** The app holds the game (OXCE 8.7.1 + OpenXcom3D), SDL 1.2 (sdl12-compat) and SDL2 in `Contents/Frameworks`, with SDL_image, SDL_mixer and SDL_gfx built in, OXCE's data and the mod; zlib is the system's.

**The game data:** the first time, it looks for the games in a `UFO` or `TFTD` folder next to the app or in your home folder, in your Steam libraries, and in apps named after them in Applications (GOG's keep the DOS game inside), and links what it finds into its profile, changing nothing there. Otherwise copy the game's folder to `~/Library/Application Support/OpenXcom3D/UFO` (or `TFTD`); the app says so in a window if it finds none. Your saves, the options and the log (`openxcom.log`) are in `~/Library/Application Support/OpenXcom3D`.

### Option B: build from source

Every command, step by step, for Linux, for Windows with MinGW (MSYS2), and for the Windows build made on Linux: the **[build guide](https://b0realis.github.io/OpenXcom3D/)**.

#### With the installer

```sh
python3 install.py --from-source [--workdir ~/.cache/openxcom3d-build] [--jobs 8]
```

1. It checks the build tools; if something is missing it prints the install command for Debian/Ubuntu, Arch or Fedora.
2. It clones `oxce-plus` from GitHub and checks out the commit above.
3. It applies `openxcom3d.patch` and builds with CMake (Release).
4. It installs the result like option A.

`--repo` uses another clone, e.g. a local mirror.

#### By hand

The build tools and libraries:

| System | Command |
|---|---|
| Debian, Ubuntu, Mint | `sudo apt install git cmake g++ make pkg-config libsdl1.2-dev libsdl-mixer1.2-dev libsdl-image1.2-dev libsdl-gfx1.2-dev zlib1g-dev libglu1-mesa-dev` |
| Arch, Manjaro | `sudo pacman -S --needed base-devel git cmake sdl12-compat sdl_image sdl_mixer sdl_gfx glu zlib` |
| Fedora | `sudo dnf install git cmake gcc-c++ make pkgconf sdl12-compat-devel SDL_image-devel SDL_mixer-devel SDL_gfx-devel zlib-devel mesa-libGLU-devel` |

Then:

```sh

git clone --branch oxce-plus https://github.com/MeridianOXC/OpenXcom.git
cd OpenXcom
git checkout f0134ef8065799c7723953d64b565f7832bfd782
git apply /path/to/openxcom3d.patch
cmake -S . -B build -DCMAKE_BUILD_TYPE=Release -DDEV_BUILD=OFF -DBUILD_PACKAGE=OFF
cmake --build build -- -j8
```

The binary and OXCE's data are in `build/bin/`, and the mod is in `mod/OpenXcom3D/`. To lay them out:

1. Put the mod in a profile's `mods/` folder (it switches itself on the first time the game finds it).
2. Link `UFO`/`TFTD` into that profile.
3. Start with:
   ```sh
   build/bin/openxcom -data build/bin/ -user <profile>/ -cfg <profile>/
   ```

`install.py --from-source` does the same.

Notes:

- OXCE bundles rapidyaml, so yaml-cpp isn't needed (old forum guides say otherwise).
- On distributions with SDL3, `libsdl1.2-dev` is sdl12-compat over SDL2/3, and it works.

### Playing

- Start `./play.sh` (or the AppImage; `play.bat` on Windows), begin a game (or **New Battle**), and in battle press **F8** or click the **3D** button at the top left.
- **Options → 3D VIEW** has the settings and the key bindings.
- [Controls](#2-controls) below.

#### TFTD

1. Open **Mods**.
2. Choose *Terror From The Deep* as the master.
3. Keep *OpenXcom3D* enabled.

#### Screen size

- The default is a 1280×800 window with the battlescape at half the screen size (640×400), which keeps 60 FPS on modest machines.
- Bigger screens work, but the game's **Battlescape scale** (video options) sets the 3D view's size. At *screen/2* (the default) a 1920×1080 screen gives a 960×540 view, twice the work of 640×400. At *screen* the view stays at 640×360 with its controls three times bigger, but the isometric map gets small.
- **Graphics detail** in 3D VIEW trades detail for speed: **Low** draws blocks of 2 pixels without sun shadows, about twice as fast. (*Pixel size* under Advanced is the setting behind it: 1 by default; **Auto** draws blocks of 2 on a big view.)

### Uninstalling

Delete the install folder (and `~/.local/share/applications/openxcom3d.desktop` if you made a menu entry). Your game data folders were only linked, never changed.

On Windows, delete the folder. The game data in `user\` is only a junction (deleting it removes the link, never the data it points to), or a copy where it had to be copied.

## 2. Controls

All of these can be rebound in **Options → 3D VIEW → Controls**: click a key's row (it turns gold, a blinking _ in place of its key while it waits), then press the new key (Shift, Ctrl and Alt can't be bound; Esc leaves the key as it was); a right click clears it. That page also lists (under a heading) the game's own keys that work in 3D: kneel, reload, inventory, hands, the suit light (the game's personal light), night vision, next/previous soldier, end turn, frame counter, quick save/load and the ten enemy keys.

| Input | Action |
|---|---|
| **F8** or **V** | Isometric ↔ 3D (or the ISO/3D button); back on the map, it's centred on your soldier |
| **Mouse** | Look around, all but straight up and down (89°); the head turns 45° for free, further turns the soldier at the game's cost (setting *Turning costs TU*; off, the soldier turns with you for free) |
| **W A S D** | Walk and step sideways in real time. Each tile costs its normal TUs; walking into a door opens it. With *Movement: Tiles*, a press walks exactly one tile. On a grav lift or a ladder, or flying with flight on, **W** looking up (more than 30°) climbs (flies straight up) and looking down goes down, as on Half-Life's ladders (**S** the other way round); held, it climbs on level by level, each paid like Page Up / Page Down. Arrows left of the compass show where it can go, the one W takes now lit yellow. Looking straight ahead, or at the top or the foot (a ceiling, the ground), W walks off as usual. Being shot at (reaction fire) stops any walk, as on the map: the keys do nothing until let go, and a new press walks on |
| **Caps Lock** | Sprint on/off: each step is the game's own run (Ctrl on the map), faster. By the default rules a run costs three quarters of a walk's TUs and half as much energy again, and only armor that allows running runs. In every game an enemy coming into sight doesn't stop a sprint, as with en passant (ENEMY SPOTTED still shows); being shot at does. **SPRINT** shows under the compass, green while the soldier runs and grey while they can't and walk (their armor, or no breath left for a run) |
| **`** (left of 1) | En passant on/off: an enemy coming into sight doesn't stop the walk, as moving with Shift held doesn't on the map; ENEMY SPOTTED still shows. Being shot at still stops it, as on the map. **EN PASSANT** shows under the compass |
| **Ctrl+Shift + click** | With the auto shot of a spray weapon (`sprayWaypoints`, X-Com Files' miniguns): marks a spray burst's first point, the tile under the crosshair; each click after marks the next, and the last fires the burst along them, as on the map (a Ctrl+Shift+click fires early). The right button takes back the last point. The points show as light green poles with a mini target and their number. |
| **Numpad 8 2 4 6** | One tile ahead, back, left or right, almost at once, like a step on the isometric map; two pressed together (8 and 4) a diagonal tile, as W and A make one (whatever the *Movement* setting; holding the key steps on, but stops at a closed door: press again to go through). **7 / 9**: snap turn 45° left / right |
| **Left button** | Fire at the crosshair in the current fire mode |
| **Wheel / J** | Previous / next fire mode (auto, snap, aimed, launch, melee, psi, use, throw, prime: a gun's shots from quantity to quality, a gun starting on snap, one notch from either) |
| **Z / X** | Snap / aimed shot. The auto shot has no key of its own by default: the wheel or **J** reaches it, or bind one under Controls |
| **G** | Switch hand: the right hand, the left, and the built-in weapon (the classic HUD's SPECIAL, such as an armor's), in turn, each that gives something to use. The weapon moves to that side of the screen; with akimbo on, it picks which gun fires first. An empty hand gives the unit's empty-hand weapon, where a mod has one (fists, a claw: OXCE's `specialUseEmptyHand`); without one, G skips it, as an empty hand has nothing to use |
| **Y** | Akimbo off, and back on to the kind the setting *Akimbo* says (Full or One-hand, see [Settings](#aiming-and-combat)); with the setting Off it does nothing; the warning line says which. It also marks the soldier's akimbo on the map, or clears it, so their map and reaction shots follow (see [Akimbo on the map](#akimbo-on-the-map)). The *Akimbo style* key (unbound unless you set one) switches two guns between taking turns round by round and both at once (*Akimbo volley*) |
| **O** | Open a door without walking through: the one you look at, if it's within reach (the sides of your tile, or a wall meeting one of its corners, as a right-click on the map reaches), else the nearest one the view looks towards. The soldier turns to it first, by the rules (*Turning costs TU*: a paid turn, then the door) |
| **(no key: set one)** | Grab the item underfoot, in 3D and on the map: one item on your tile and a hand free to take it, into that hand (the right one first) for the game's time units for that move (ground to hand); with more items, or no hand free (nor beside a gun that blocks both hands, the game's red 2), the inventory opens instead; with nothing there, NOTHING UNDERFOOT. It has no key until you give it one on the 3D keys page (every letter is the game's or ours) |
| **T** | Throw a tactical flare: free, as often as you like |
| **(no key: set one)** | A tank's turret switch, as the TURRET button: the view turns only the turret |
| **B** | Suit flight on/off (flying armor; SWIM under water); each soldier's switch is kept with the save. A soldier who went up without it (the map's own up button or path) has it switched on when seen in the air, so touching down keeps flight on: Page Down on the ground (or B) switches it off |
| **F** | The flashlight on/off: the 3D view's own light, a spot on the crosshair, separate from the suit light (L) |
| **Space** | Jump: a hop on the spot (1 time unit). Walking forwards or sideways into something low (a fence, table, crate, rock: up to 15 voxels high), it vaults over it for 30 time units (setting *Jump over low obstacles*) |
| *(no key at first)* | **Emergency climb**: for a unit the rules leave no way out (a sunken railway track, a ditch, a fenced yard). For half the unit's time units with nothing carried, rising with its load to all of them at a full load, straight ahead along the grid, where the plain step is refused: up a ledge onto the next tile; over a fence or anything else up to 20 voxels high (a taller one too if sight passes it and nothing is overhead: a wire fence, not a house's window), onto the next tile or, when it stands on that tile, the one beyond; or out of a pit (or onto a low flat roof), onto the next tile a level up, when it has a floor and nothing is over the unit's head. The tile climbed onto must have a floor and be free. The unit is seen and may be shot at as after a step. One tile big units only; bind it under the game's Controls. |
| **N** | Night vision on/off (Scroll Lock, the game's own toggle, works too); at any time, also during explosions, shots and the aliens' turn |
| **Page Up / Page Down** | Fly up/down (flight on), or ride a grav lift or climb a ladder. Standing on one, purple arrows with LIFT PLATFORM show which ways it goes; with a free cursor (C), click them to ride. With flight off, a soldier in flying armor keeps to the lift as anyone else does (not on up into the air from its top: the message says which key switches flight on); one in the air all the same comes down to land, by itself or with Page Down. Off a lift or ladder, Page Up with flight off switches flight on and flies up, and Page Down with flight on, standing on the ground, switches it off |
| **1 … 0** | Turn to visible enemy 1–10 (or click the red enemy buttons) |
| **Right button** | Cancel the current action, or forget a target marked for *Confirm fire*; with nothing to cancel, hold to zoom. With a free cursor: hold to look |
| **U**, held | Zoom: the view glides in to 4 times closer, and back out when you let go. **SCOPING 2.6x** at the right shows how far, and a thin blue cross runs through the crosshair. Aim, shots and hit chances don't change. In 3D, U then isn't the game's UFOpedia key (on the map it is) |
| **Left Alt**, held | Show facing: a ring round every unit in sight, with an arrow the way it faces, in its side's colour (X-COM green, the aliens red, civilians yellow), drawn over everything, for units whose sprite doesn't show it; with *Facing icons* on, an arrow on each unit as well (the unit seen from above with you at the bottom: up, it faces away from you; down, towards you) |
| *Range (hold)*: no key until you give it one | The rangefinder on demand, while held (setting *Rangefinder*: the digital **RANGE 12** left of the crosshair, or the analogue scale if that is chosen). |
| **C** or **middle button** | Mouse look ↔ free cursor (to click the buttons, hover soldiers for their cards). With *Middle click identifies* on, a middle click on a unit shows it instead, as on the map |
| **H** | Classic HUD on/off: every button of it works in 3D ([below](#the-classic-hud-in-3d)) |
| **P** | The 3D HUD in three steps. Once, a clear view: the top bar, the info panel, the compass, the lift arrows and the enemy markers hide (and can't be clicked); the crosshair stays. Twice, a clean view: the crosshair, its lines (mode, hit chance, costs), the accuracy line and the name under it go too. Three times, all back. The weapon in hand and the trajectory keep their own settings, and the messages still show. On the isometric map the top bar shows as ever, and P shows or hides the map camera instead (see *Map camera*) |
| **Arrow keys** | Turn left and right, look up and down (the 3D view's own keys, *Turn left* ... *Look down*; the map's scroll keys stay the map's, so they can be put on W, A, S, D) |
| **, / .** | Snap turn 45° left / right, without stepping (the soldier turns with it) |
| **Left Shift + A / D**, held | Lean out left / right, to peek round a corner, with the setting *Lean* on (otherwise Shift does nothing and A / D strafe, as before): the eye moves to the side, stopping short of walls and of anyone in sight, and with *Tilt* the view tilts. A shot while leaning out starts from that side. Q and E stay the game's hand keys (they don't act while Shift is held, so a lean never opens a menu). The lean key is rebindable (*Lean (hold with strafe)* under Controls, Shift, Ctrl or Alt allowed), and each side can have a key of its own too (*Lean left / right*, unbound) |
| **Insert** | Cheats off, and the same ones back on (Doom mode, Doom IDKFA, Doom IDDQD, Doom IDCLIP: see [Settings](#cheats)) |

The original hotkeys still work, except for W, A, S, D (on the map: the special item, abort and stats), U and the keys above. While you lean out (Shift held) they stay quiet, so Q or E don't open a hand's menu in the middle of a lean; the keys above work then too:

- K kneel, I inventory, R reload (from a clip anywhere the soldier carries one: the belt, pockets, backpack, the other hand; in 3D, when it loads nothing, the warning line says why: already loaded, no spare clip for the weapon, or not enough time units, naming how many: in the big mods a clip deeper in the inventory costs its move to the hand too, so one in the backpack costs more than one on the belt);
- Backspace end turn;
- Q / E use left / right hand;
- L the suit light: the game's personal light, for the whole squad (the flashlight is F);
- Tab next soldier, the 3D view looking through them (*Previous soldier* has no key until you give it one: click ◄ on the top bar);
- M the minimap, on your soldier; Home looks straight ahead, the way the soldier faces;
- F5 / F9 quick save / load; F7 the frame counter.

A key that can't do its job just now says why on the game's warning line: armor that can't fly, a fire mode the weapon doesn't have. With the game's Ctrl or Alt held (Ctrl+S and its other shortcuts) the keys are the game's, and W A S D don't walk. OXCE's **Ctrl+End** (its debug light: the light halved, then everything at full light, then back) lights the 3D view as it lights the map.

### The classic HUD in 3D

**H** (or the HUD button) puts the game's own bottom HUD over the 3D view, with a free cursor. Every button works there. Those that act on the isometric map are turned into what makes sense in 3D, and so are their keys, with or without the HUD shown:

| Button (key) | On the isometric map | In 3D |
|---|---|---|
| Soldier up / down | Up or down a level (a grav lift, flight) | As Page Up / Page Down in 3D: a lift ride, or flight, which must be switched on (SUIT FLY or B; otherwise "Flight off") |
| Map level up / down (Page Up / Page Down) | Shows the level above / below | Looks 30° up / down, towards that level: the 3D view shows every level anyway. The keys stay the soldier's up and down in 3D |
| Minimap (M) | The minimap, at the map's view | The minimap, on your soldier |
| Centre on soldier (Home) | Centres the map on the soldier | Turns the view back to where the soldier faces, level |
| Red enemy buttons (1 … 0) | Centre the map on that enemy; right click: select the soldier who sees it with the most time units left | Turn the view to that enemy; right click: switch to that soldier, looking at it |
| Layers (OXCE links menu) | The links menu, or (with links off) the multi-level view | The links menu works as on the map; the multi-level view only changes the map, and a line says so |
| Kneel (K) | Kneel / stand | The same; the view drops to kneeling height |
| Next soldier (Tab; right click: previous), next and done (`\`) | Select the next soldier (done: not again this turn) | The same; the 3D view looks through them |
| Hands (Q / E) | The action menu; a target is clicked on the map | The action menu; a shot picked becomes the fire mode and a click on the view fires it; other actions are aimed with a click on the view (a guided missile's waypoints and psi on a creature out of sight on the map). Right click: the hand for reaction fire; middle click: the Ufopaedia |
| Reserve TUs (F1 … F4, J), spend all TUs (Delete) | Time units kept for a shot or kneeling | The same (in 3D, J is the next fire mode: the kneel reserve is its classic HUD button); walking in 3D keeps the reserve |
| Inventory, options, end turn, abort, the soldier's name and bars, special, skills | Their screens and menus | The same |

### Akimbo on the map

On the isometric map a soldier with a gun in each hand can fire both too: hold **Ctrl+Shift** and click either hand's weapon. Each click steps the pair on: **both at once** (a mini target in each hand's box), **in turn** (two in each), and off. A shot fired on the map then fires both guns as akimbo does in 3D: pick the mode from the clicked gun's menu as usual, and the other gun fires its mode of the same kind (snap with snap, auto with auto), else its first, both 20% less accurate, for the more expensive gun's shot and a tenth (the other gun fires only if the time units are there for its share). An aimed shot fires alone, as in 3D. It works with akimbo off (the mark is the choice; with akimbo *One-hand* a two-handed gun doesn't pair up, and a gun that blocks both hands, the game's red 2, never does) and is kept with the soldier in the save; it goes once a gun leaves a hand. The soldier's **reaction shots** follow the mark too, in either view: both guns fire. **Y** in 3D sets the selected soldier's mark along with akimbo there (in turn, or both at once with *Akimbo volley*; cleared when akimbo goes off), and the *Akimbo style* key changes it with the style. Shots in 3D keep their own akimbo (the settings and the style key).

## 3. The top bar and items

**Top bar**, from left to right:

- **ISO/3D** · **HUD** · **INV**, at the left edge (on the map HUD hides and INV takes its place);
- **◄ soldiers ►**, in the middle of the screen: 10 shown (on a wide screen, as many as fit at full size), the window follows your soldier, and the mouse wheel over ◄ or ► scrolls; so does a right click on ◄ or ►, one soldier at a time, without changing who's selected (a left click selects the previous or next soldier). Hovering one shows a card with stats and a live helmet camera. A skull marks the dead, a caduceus the unconscious, and an orange alien face a soldier under alien mind control; a unit of the other side under *your* mind control gets a button too, after your soldiers, its number in purple (looking through it, the view's edges are washed purple, breathing gently, as is its helmet camera). A right click on a soldier's button opens their **SOLDIER CAM** (their helmet camera big; see *Soldier camera preview* in [Settings](#4-settings)). The **mouse wheel over a soldier's button** steps the card, or the soldier cam, on to the next soldier (up: the previous one), round the whole squad and scrolling the bar along, without moving the mouse; moving onto another button shows that one again.
- **TURRET** · **SPECIAL** · **NIGHT VISION** · **SUIT LIGHT** · **SUIT FLY**, shown when the soldier has them (labels shorten to TUR · SPEC · NV · LIGHT · FLY on a narrow bar). **TURRET**, for a unit with a turret (a tank): while it's pressed, turning the view turns only the turret, by the game's own turret turn (as the map's Ctrl turn and its aiming: a time unit an eighth with *Turning costs TU*), the hull staying as it is (a step still turns the hull the way it goes); off (at first), the whole tank turns as before; each tank's switch is kept with the save;
- **END** turn, at the right edge.

**Under the top bar**, at the left: the game's own line (its debug messages, and *Bug Hunt mode activated* each turn once that's on), one line down so it never covers the buttons; under the map camera's window while that's open.

**Enemies in sight**, at the right: the game's buttons, one for each enemy the squad sees (red: this soldier sees it; green: only the squad does), then blue for our own wounded and purple for those about to pass out (their markers: *Wounded markers*), the first at the bottom, numbered as the markers over the enemies (in the same colours, see *Enemy markers*), in a column under the compass, centred on the view's height on any screen, clear of the markers that point at enemies past the view's edge. With the free cursor, a click turns the view to that enemy and a right click switches to the soldier who sees it with the most time units left, as the classic HUD's own buttons do (H shows them in their place).

**Info panel**, bottom left (the classic HUD, H, has all of this in its own way):

- the soldier's rank and name (the game's own rank badge, made small, before the name; a mod's own tiny ranks if it has them, as XPiratez does), and on the right what matters most just now: PANIC or BERSERK this turn, BURNING, WOUNDS (fatal wounds), or KNEELING;
- a line for each hand. First the weapon that fires, then whatever is in the other hand (grey, or as bright as the first when it fires along, akimbo). A gun's line tells its ammo, for example **`R: Rifle 17 | 2 clips (40)`**:
  - **17** is the rounds in the loaded clip, for the fire mode you picked (a gun with several ammo slots shows the slot that mode uses). **EMPTY** in red means no clip is loaded: the game throws a clip away when its last round is fired, so reload (R, from a clip anywhere the soldier carries one) or load one in the inventory;
  - a gun that takes clips of more than one damage type (the heavy cannon and auto-cannon: AP, HE, incendiary; the rocket launcher: small and large rockets HE, incendiary; TFTD's gas cannon, hydro-jet and torpedo launcher) says which is loaded after the rounds, in two letters in the type's colour: **`R: Heavy Cannon 6 [HE] | 2 clips (12)`**. The letters come from the type's name as the Ufopaedia gives it, in your language and a mod's own names (ARMOR PIERCING **AP** grey-white, INCENDIARY **IN** red, HIGH EXPLOSIVE **HE** orange, LASER BEAM **LB** pink, PLASMA BEAM **PB** green, STUN **ST** light blue, ACID **AC** yellow-green; X-Com Files' ELECTRIC **EL**, a mod's own types purple). Small and large rockets are both HE: the tag tells the damage, not the size. The soldier cards show it too;
  - **2 clips (40)** is the spare ammo the soldier carries for that gun, anywhere in the inventory (belt, pockets, backpack, the other hand), and how many rounds they hold together. It counts every clip the gun can take by the game's rules, all kinds together (a heavy cannon's AP, HE and incendiary). **no clips** means there's nothing to reload from;
  - guns that never run out, like the lasers, show **∞**; a gun with its rounds built in shows just its count;
  - the numbers follow every round fired, in faithful and FPS-style shooting alike, and every reload, pick-up and drop. Two guns of the same kind (dual pistols) each count the same spare clips, since either could load them;
  - a primed grenade shows the turns its fuse has left: **PRIMED 3 turns**, **PRIMED 1 turn**, **PRIMED this turn** (it goes off when this turn ends); in Doom mode grenades burn in real time, so it shows the seconds: **PRIMED 2.4s**;
- time units, energy, health (with stun damage over it) and morale, as numbers and bars; and under them, where a mod uses mana and the map shows its bar (XPiratez's freshness, X-Com Files' sanity), a thin bar for it as on the map;
- the armor, and on the right DOOM MODE while that cheat is on (IDDQD and/or IDKFA for its extras; with IDDQD the four stats turn silver).

Hovering a soldier on the top bar shows the same weapon line on their card.

**Items:**

- Every item works from 3D. Its fire modes are on the wheel, the game's own: a gun's shots, and melee for anything with a melee cost (a stun rod, a knife, a gun with a butt stroke in a mod), the empty hand's fists and the built-in weapon included (G picks them). A blow strikes what the crosshair is on: a unit beside you (you turn to them first), or a wall, door or object within reach, or (with *Melee hits walls and downed*) a body lying beside you, diagonally too; with it on none of these, whatever stands the way you face.
- **A medi-kit** is used at once, no menu (it had one entry): the patient is the one under the crosshair, among those the game allows: a unit standing on any tile next to you, diagonally too (you turn to face them first: free, or with *Turning costs TU* an eighth of a turn's cost each, as on the map); a body lying at your feet, its picture under the crosshair or simply looking down (more than 25 degrees); aiming at nothing, yourself, if the kit may treat its user (a mod's `medikitTargetSelf`; the stock Medi-Kit can't), or else the game's own order. A unit farther off is **Out of Range!**, and a body that isn't at your feet says to stand over it (the game treats bodies only there). The crosshair says who: **USE: Fred**, **USE: SELF**, **USE: -** for nobody. A kit of one kind (heal, stimulant or painkiller only, as in some mods) works there and then, as the game's menu does it; the full Medi-Kit opens the game's own screen on that patient, to pick the body part and the treatment. **The motion scanner** is held up in the view: used (its time units, as the game's), its round screen, the game's own, shows at the bottom of the 3D view, about a third of its height across (just right of the info panel on the original 320×200), the weapon in hand making way for it, while the game goes on round it. It turns as you look round, the way you look up (its N, E, S and W go round with it); a ring sweeps out from the middle every two seconds with a whoop, and each movement it finds lights up as the ring reaches it, with a pip (higher the nearer, from its side), fading till the next sweep. It shows what the game's scanner finds (the units that moved this turn, up to 9 tiles across and along, but for the square's corners beyond the round screen), from where you stand now. It stays up while it's the item in hand: used again, or another item or hand, and it's put away. With *Live motion scanner* NO (the default) it's one look from where you used it, as the game's own scanner: a step to another tile or the end of your turn puts it away (turning on the spot keeps it up, turning with you), and using it again costs its time units again; YES keeps it up as you move and through the aliens' turn, a live radar for one use. On the isometric map the game's own scanner, as ever. The mind probe opens its menu, then is aimed like a shot.
- **Anything in a hand shows in 3D**, built from its inventory picture like the weapon in hand: a grenade, a medi-kit, a scanner, a clip... sits in the bottom corner with its lower half out of sight. A unit's built-in items (a fixed weapon such as a tank's cannon, or an armor's special weapon such as a dog's bite) leave the hands empty: they'd look like a weapon held in empty hands. One the map itself draws in the unit's hand shows in 3D too (an item with `fixedWeaponShow`: a shield unit's shield; GitHub issue #123). A mod can list more to show anyway (`builtInItemsShown`, see [For developers](#10-for-developers)).
- **Melee weapons** are held tip up, the way the inventory pictures stand them (the stun rod's prongs, a blade's point, at the top), and a blow swings the tip down at the crosshair and back up.
- **Switching a light on and off** (an item primed to switch on, as in some mods): the fire modes offer **UNPRIME** once it's primed, as the game's menu does.
- **Throwing:** as on the isometric map, anything that isn't built in can be thrown (fire mode **throw**, the arc on the crosshair): a grenade, a clip for a squadmate, even a rifle. It flies along the game's arc as a 3D object, tumbling end over end, and lands where the game says. The game refuses a throw it can't make ("Unable to throw here!"): under a roof (inside the Skyranger, say), past the soldier's range, or with someone right in front.
- **Priming a grenade** (fire mode **prime**): a click opens the game's own timer at once (the number of turns, 0 to 23), as on the isometric map after *Prime Grenade* (the fire mode already says that much, so its menu isn't asked again); a right click on the timer cancels. A grenade with no timer to set (a mod's) is primed at once. The panel then says **PRIMED** and the turns left (**PRIMED 3 turns**). A primed grenade lying on the ground goes off at the end of a turn with its fuse at 0: **0** when you end this turn, **1** at the end of the aliens' turn, **2** at the end of your next one, and so on (every side's turn counts one down). The game's option *Instant grenades* makes a 0 go off on landing instead. A primed grenade still in a hand doesn't go off.
- **In Doom mode** grenades work in real time, like in a first-person shooter, going off a few seconds after they're primed (*Doom grenade fuse*, 3 s by default) wherever they are then. How they're primed is up to *Doom prime on throw*:
  - **YES** (the default): just **throw** one. It's armed as you throw it (you hear the pin) and burns from the moment it leaves your hand, so it never goes off in your hand. **Prime** becomes **FUSE 3s**: it opens a **SET FUSE** box like the map's timer, with 1s to 5s to pick (a click, or the keys 1 to 5), and the seconds you pick hold for every grenade after that until you change them (it's the same as the setting). The panel shows the fuse for a grenade in hand, then counts the seconds down once it's thrown (**PRIMED 2.4s**). A grenade whose throw the game refuses stays in your hand, armed but not burning, until it leaves it.
  - **NO**: **prime** opens the same **SET FUSE** box, and your pick pulls the pin (you hear it): the fuse burns from then on, in your hand too. Throw it in time, or cook it for a shorter flight, but not too long. The panel counts the seconds down.

  With *Doom free grenades with airburst* a grenade flies wherever the crosshair points, and one whose fuse runs out in the air bursts there; without it, the game's own throw, and a fuse that runs out in flight goes off as the grenade lands. Proximity grenades work as the game makes them. This is the first step towards a real-time mode (see [Known limits and ideas](#9-known-limits-and-ideas)).
- The **blaster launcher** (any guided launcher), and psi or the mind probe aimed at no one in sight, switch to the isometric map to pick waypoints or a target, then come back. A missile takes as many waypoints as its launcher's rules give, as on the map: 9 for UFO's Blaster Launcher and the Hovertank/Launcher, TFTD's Disruptor Pulse Launcher and Displacer/P.W.T.; any number with the game's mod *Unlimited Waypoints*; a click past them is ignored. Back in 3D (F8) while you place them, the fire mode counts them, **WAYPOINTS 3/9** (**WAYPOINTS 3/UNLIMITED** without a cap), and a click on the view takes you back to the map with those placed kept; the map's launch button fires it. A psi amp's or a mind probe's **use** is a fire mode like panic and mind control: a click on a creature uses it on them, no menu first.

## 4. Settings

**Options → 3D VIEW**, from the main menu or in a battle (Esc → Options). The tab has three pages: **Settings**, **Controls** and **Advanced**. A left click flips YES / NO or raises a number; a right click lowers it (choices go round). The line at the bottom of the page describes the setting under the mouse; for the two presets it describes the one chosen.

### The first page: Settings

Nine rows, the ones most players touch:

- **Gameplay** (Extended+): how it plays, as one choice. See below.
- **Renderer** (CPU): the processor or the graphics card draws the 3D world. See *Screen*.
- **Graphics detail** (Medium): how finely it is drawn, as one choice. See below.
- **Render scale**, **Show trajectory**, **Realistic hitboxes**, **Mouse sensitivity**, **Start battles in 3D**: the same settings as under Advanced, here because they are changed often.
- **Recommended game options** (Apply): a click sets OpenXcom's own options, the ones on the game's other tabs, to a recommended lot (the list is under "1.15", the rulings on GitHub issue #193), and the row says Applied. Kept with OK, dropped with Cancel; a second click sets them again; there is no taking them back but on the game's own tabs. No Gameplay preset touches the game's options.

Everything else, and these too, is under **Advanced**: every setting in its section, the sections and the settings in each by the alphabet, but **CHEATS** come after them all and **EXPERIMENTAL** (*Voxel units*, *Live command feed*) is the very last. The sections below describe them all, with the default in brackets.

### Gameplay

Six presets, from the game's own way to a shooter's. Each sets 46 settings at once and keeps what the one before it set, unless the table says otherwise:

- **FAITHFUL**: soldiers' 3D view on top of OXCE: a graphics layer over the game, its rules and turns untouched.
- **Extended+** (the default): additional features, the intended play for most: the map camera, unit names, hitboxes shown, shots at the exact point under the crosshair.
- **TACTICUS**: a fuller tactical picture, information dense, fewer frills: cameras see as the eyes, the accuracy line, the rangefinder, the compass's step costs.
- **XMbl**: TACTICUS for the veteran: akimbo, lean shots, modern throwing, realistic hitboxes, damage numbers, sidesteps that face the view, a far clear view and thinner smoke.
- **FullQoL**: every feature on that leaves the rules of turning, moving and shooting alone: sound, music, UFO lights, jumping, leaning, the live scanner, instant chains.
- **FPS**: FullQoL by a shooter's rules: free turning and walking, FPS-style shooting, quick deaths, fall damage.
- **Custom**: your own mix. Change any of the 46 by hand (on the first page or under Advanced) and Gameplay says Custom; the mix is kept, so you can try a preset and come back to it, also after a restart. Custom is offered once you have a mix.

A setting no preset changes is yours alone, whatever Gameplay says. The cheats stay apart. A profile from before the presets keeps every value it has, and says Custom where they match no preset.

| Setting | FAITHFUL | Extended+ | TACTICUS | XMbl | FullQoL | FPS |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| FPS-style shooting (easier) | NO | NO | NO | NO | NO | YES |
| Turning costs TU | YES | YES | YES | YES | YES | NO |
| Movement | Tile by tile | Tile by tile | Tile by tile | Tile by tile | Tile by tile | Free |
| Jump over low obstacles | NO | NO | NO | YES | YES | YES |
| Lean | Off | Off | Off | Tilt | Peek | Peek |
| Lean shots | NO | NO | NO | YES | YES | YES |
| Realistic hitboxes | NO | NO | NO | YES | YES | YES |
| Aim at exact point | NO | YES | YES | YES | YES | YES |
| Quick deaths | NO | NO | NO | NO | NO | YES |
| Melee hits walls and downed | NO | NO | NO | YES | YES | YES |
| Live motion scanner | NO | NO | NO | NO | YES | YES |
| Fall damage | NO | NO | NO | YES | NO | YES |
| Akimbo | Off | Off | Off | Full | Full | Full |
| Throwing | Classic | Classic | Classic | Modern | Modern | Modern |
| Close range: from the eye | NO | NO | NO | YES | YES | YES |
| Akimbo volley | NO | NO | NO | NO | YES | YES |
| Force blocked shots | NO | NO | NO | NO | YES | YES |
| Instant chain explosions | NO | NO | NO | NO | YES | YES |
| Auto-equip guns first | YES | YES | YES | YES | YES | YES |
| Sidesteps face the view | NO | NO | NO | YES | NO | NO |
| Map camera | Off | Small | Small | Small | Small | Small |
| Cameras see as the eyes | NO | NO | YES | YES | YES | YES |
| Alien turn: Facing (when in 3D) | NO | NO | NO | YES | YES | YES |
| Unit names | NO | YES | YES | YES | YES | YES |
| Compass: step costs | NO | NO | YES | YES | NO | NO |
| Hitboxes | Aimed at | Always | Blink | Blink | Blink | Blink |
| Accuracy line | NO | NO | YES | YES | YES | YES |
| Rangefinder | Off | Off | Digital | Digital | Digital | Digital |
| Damage numbers | NO | NO | NO | YES | YES | YES |
| Shot info: the damage range | NO | NO | NO | YES | YES | YES |
| Glowing outlines | NO | NO | NO | YES | YES | YES |
| Downed unit icons | Crosshair | Always | Always | Always | Always | Always |
| See-through: crops | NO | YES | YES | YES | YES | YES |
| See-through: corpses (All) | NO | NO | NO | NO | NO | NO |
| Unseen terrain | Mist | Mist | Visible | Visible | Visible | Visible |
| Smooth lighting | as it is | as it is | as it is | YES | YES | YES |
| Distance haze | Normal | Normal | Normal | Clear | Clear | Clear |
| View distance | 60 | 60 | 60 | 130 | 130 | 130 |
| Smoke opacity | 80% | 80% | 80% | 65% | 65% | 65% |
| Night smoke opacity | 45% | 45% | 45% | 65% | 65% | 65% |
| Unit size | 100% | 100% | 100% | 90% | 90% | 90% |
| Discreet map edge | NO | NO | NO | YES | YES | YES |
| UFO lights and aura | NO | NO | NO | NO | YES | YES |
| Hit, kill and swing sounds | NO | NO | YES | NO | YES | YES |
| Dynamic music | NO | NO | NO | NO | YES | YES |

Choosing **XMbl** also sets *Pixel size* to 1 and *Flashlight* to Far light. Those two are no part of what makes the preset: changing either afterwards leaves you on XMbl, and a profile already on XMbl keeps what it has.

### Graphics detail

Low, Medium or High: fourteen of the *Screen* settings at once. What a level means goes by the *Renderer*, and changing the *Renderer* sets the level again for it (Low stays Low). Changing one of them by hand makes it **Custom**, kept like Gameplay's.

| | Renderer CPU | Renderer GPU |
|---|---|---|
| **Low** | *Pixel size* 2, no sun shadows: about twice the frame rate | *3D resolution* HUD, the palette's colours: the fastest |
| **Medium** (the default) | *3D resolution* HUD, a ray for every pixel, sun shadows | *3D resolution* Display, *Render scale* Auto |
| **High** | *3D resolution* Battle, a ray for every pixel | Medium with *True colour*, *Smooth colours*, *Shadows* Soft+lamps, *Ambient occlusion*, *Coloured lights*, *Reflections*, *Light shafts* |

*Pixel size* is 1 at every level but the CPU's Low; *Supersampling* and *Surface relief* are Off at every level.

### View and camera

- **Soldier camera preview** (YES): hovering a soldier's button on the top bar shows a card with their name and rank, stats and weapon. YES: the card also shows that soldier's helmet camera, live: what they are looking at right now (static if they're down). Like a real digital feed, a helmet camera now and then has a bad signal for a moment: a few patches of the picture fall into coarse blocks, a green square or two shows, the picture tears (bands of it slide sideways), or it freezes for up to half a second (every few seconds and brief on most missions; every second or so, longer and torn more often on the hardest ones and inside UFOs, see [Bad signal by mission](#bad-signal-by-mission); gentler or none with *Camera artifacts*, never with *Muzzle flashes* off). NO: the card without the camera (a camera popped up in the aliens' turn still shows its picture). Either way, a **right click** on a soldier's button opens their **SOLDIER CAM**: the helmet camera big, with a blinking red dot and now and then a spell of TV static (from nothing up to a snowstorm and back over a few seconds, each at its own pace, 10 seconds to 2 minutes apart; on the hardest missions a little static all the time and the spells more often), so it's plain it's a camera, not your view; moving along the bar, or the mouse wheel over it, shows each soldier's in turn (a fallen soldier keeps the card with its static), and moving off the bar or a second right click closes it. You stay with your own soldier all the while.
- **Map camera** (Small): on the isometric map in your turn, the selected soldier's helmet camera in a window at the top left, under the top bar, with their name over it: a hybrid of the map and the soldier's own sight. It looks the way the soldier faces; while you aim a shot, a throw or a psi attack on the map, it turns to the tile under the cursor (at the unit there, if you see one), so you see the target through the soldier's eyes. **Small**, **Medium** or **Large**: 30%, 40% or half of the width our top bar is laid out on (on the original 320×200 battlescape, Medium is 128×80, doubled on the screen). A **click** on it goes to the 3D view; a **right click** opens the SOLDIER CAM in its place (moving off it closes it); clicks on it never reach the map under it, and the map's cursor stays where it was while the mouse is over it. **P** (the *3D HUD on/off* key) shows or hides it on the map, coming back at the size it had. With OXCE's touch buttons (Ctrl, Alt, Shift and the mouse buttons, on the left edge) switched on, it keeps right of them; hovering a soldier's button shows their card over it. Hidden in 3D and in the aliens' turn (their cameras pop up instead, see *Aliens' turn cameras*). It's a live feed like the SOLDIER CAM: a blinking red dot before the name, a spell of TV static now and then and the bad signal's glitches.
- **Tactical map** (In 3D; **Off**, **In 3D**, **Both views**): the map opened from the 3D view (the game's map key or button) fills the screen, without the game's map frame. It shows what the game's map shows, from the same little pictures of the tiles (the levels up to the one looked at, the items on the floors), enlarged by whole steps. Every unit the game's map would show is a dot with a small cone the way it looks: yours green with their numbers (as on the top bar's buttons), the selected one blue with its cone the way the 3D view looks, enemies red with the first letters of their kind (**Sec.** for a Sectoid), anyone else yellow; a unit on another level than the one looked at is a ring with a mark for above (^) or below (v). Small buttons along the top do what the game's map does and more: **UP** and **DN** (the level; also Page Up and Page Down, or the wheel), **-** and **+** (zoom; also the keys, and Ctrl and the wheel, which zooms about the tile under the pointer), **CTR** (the selected soldier in the middle; Home), **NEXT** (select the next soldier; the game's next unit key), **ABC** (the numbers and letters on or off), **WPN** (what everyone holds, in a few letters under their dots: "H.Plasma" for a Heavy Plasma; an enemy's only if the game would name the item), **VIEW** (the cones on or off), **TU** (the selected soldier's reach: a blue line round every tile they can still walk to with the time units and the energy they have now, by the game's own pathfinding, on any level), **CAM** (a helmet camera in the bottom right corner, live: the soldier's the pointer is on, else the selected one's, turned to an enemy the pointer is on), and in the top right corner **?** (the help; also F1) and **OK** (back: also Escape, the map key, the right button). A drag scrolls the map (the arrow keys too), a click centres it there, and a click on one of your soldiers selects them (the camera shows their view at once); a click on the selected soldier closes the map into their 3D view. A unit two tiles by two (a tank) has a dot twice as big. On a big screen the buttons, the letters, the dots and the camera are drawn bigger by the same rule as the 3D view's own overlays (see *HUD scale*), so the map looks the same at any battlescape resolution; the tiles by the zoom alone. The line along the bottom is the selected soldier's: their number and name, health, time units and the weapon in hand with its rounds, each in the colour the 3D view's own panel has it in (with too little room the rounds go, then the name is shortened). With the pointer on a unit the line is that unit's; for anyone not yours: its name, what it holds if the game would name it, and how far it is from the selected soldier. With **?** on, the help is over that line: how the map is worked, or what the button under the pointer does (the helmet camera moves up to make room); off, as it starts, there is none. The zoom and the switches are remembered while the game runs. **Both views**: the game's map button and key open it from the isometric battlescape too, around the selected soldier, with the squad's numbers; it closes back onto the isometric map at the level it showed (a player, GitHub issue #217). **Off**: the game's own map screen.
- **Cameras see as the eyes** (NO): YES: the helmet cameras (the soldier card's, the soldier cam, the map camera, a fallen soldier's last picture) see through smoke and fire with the soldier's heat vision, as the 3D view does. NO: a realistic camera, without the wearer's eyes (night vision they keep either way).
- **Camera artifacts** (Light): how much bad signal the helmet cameras show (the soldier card's, the SOLDIER CAM, the map camera, the cards that pop up in the aliens' turn). **Normal**: as they are: now and then a glitch (coarse blocks, a green square or two, a tear, the picture frozen for up to half a second), the SOLDIER CAM's and the map camera's spells of TV static, and on the hardest missions and inside UFOs a little static all the time and much more of all of it (see [Bad signal by mission](#bad-signal-by-mission)). **Light**: the same feed, gentler: half the low static, the spells at three tenths of their strength (never a snowstorm), and every glitch short, none over 0.15 seconds on any mission, so the picture is never unreadable for more than a quarter of a second. **None**: a clean picture, no static, glitches or freezes; the frame, the name and the blinking red dot stay. At every setting a fallen soldier's camera shows its static (that's no signal, not a bad one), and so does your view when the soldier you look through is killed. *Muzzle flashes* NO keeps the glitches and the spells away whatever this says.
- **Mouse sensitivity** (5): how fast the view turns with the mouse, from 1 (slow) to 20 (fast).
- **Invert mouse** (NO): YES: moving the mouse forward looks down instead of up, as in a flight simulator.
- **Field of view** (75): how wide the 3D view is from side to side, in degrees, from 50 (narrow, like a zoom) to 110 (wide), in steps of 5. The weapon in hand keeps its size and place whatever you pick.
- **Start battles in 3D** (NO): YES: begin each battle in the first-person view instead of the isometric map.
- **Alien turn** (3D; **Map**, **3D**, **Facing**): **3D**: stay in the 3D view during the alien turn, looking through your last soldier's eyes (HIDDEN MOVEMENT until an alien comes into sight, glowing red on the aliens' turn and green on the civilians', as the map's turn bar), looking all round. **Facing**: the same, but your soldier can't turn in the alien turn, so the view stays with their facing: it looks at most 45 degrees to either side (the head turns), whether you move the mouse or an aliens' turn camera looks at an attacker, and goes along when the soldier turns to fire back. You see what the soldier can see, not the empty street behind them. (GitHub issue #126.) **Map**: switch back to the isometric map for the alien turn.
- **Aliens' turn cameras** (YES): with *Alien turn* 3D or Facing, the view goes where the action is. When one of your soldiers fires back (reaction fire), it cuts to them at once, looking at their target. While an alien moves, it looks through the nearest soldier who sees it, staying at least a second and a half before cutting to another, and turns to the alien each time it reaches a new tile. When an alien none of your soldiers sees fires at one of them (at night, say), it cuts to the soldier shot at, turned to where the fire comes from. A soldier who is shot at, hit or mind controlled pops up their helmet camera card for 3 seconds (TARGETED, looking at the shooter; HIT; MIND CONTROLLED), and one going down out of view too (static, DECEASED or UNCONSCIOUS); the soldier you look through going down gets the death camera, as before. Your turn then starts looking the way the soldier faces. With the aliens' turn on the isometric map (*Alien turn* Map, or you're on the map), the same cards pop up over the map, and so does whoever the 3D view would cut to: a soldier firing back (FIRING, looking at the target) and the nearest soldier who sees the alien moving (WATCHING, looking at it, for as long as they see it), one card at a time, the newest news first. The cards show the camera whatever *Soldier camera preview* says. NO: the view stays with your last soldier, and nothing pops up.
- **Head bob** (YES): the view bobs gently with the soldier's steps. Turn it off if it makes you queasy.

### Movement

- **Turning costs TU** (YES): YES: turning is the game's own (the default since 1.09: free turning is a cheat, the defaults are the game's mechanics): the view may look up to 45° off the way the soldier faces (they turn their head, for free); looking further turns the soldier 45° that way at the game's cost (1 TU for a soldier), as on the isometric map, and a turn stops when it spots a new enemy. Without the time units the view stops 45° off, and so it does while a shot is under way (yours, a burst, its hits, or anyone's): no turns pile up to be paid for after it. The snap turn keys turn the soldier a step each. NO: the soldier turns with the view for free.
- **Sidesteps face the view** (NO): with *Turning costs TU*. Your soldier's body may lag up to 45 degrees behind where you look (the head turns for free), and a sidestep keeps the body as it stands: that is the cheapest way, and the default. **YES** is the defensive way: before a sidestep the soldier turns to face where you look, at the armour's price for the turn, so the front armour is towards what you look at. A step back, which the game can't sidestep, becomes a sidestep too: straight back, the soldier turns side-on (a quarter turn, to the side nearer the way they stand) and sidesteps back; half back, an eighth off the view. The back is never shown, and the soldier stays as turned while you keep looking the same way. Walking the way you look is a plain walk either way. Measured on plain ground with a body facing north-east: looking east and stepping right, 1 TU for the turn and 5 for the sidestep; three steps straight back, 2 TU for the turn and 7 a step, 23 in all and side-on throughout, where NO turns right round, walks 6 a step and pays 3 more to face forward again, 27 with the back to the enemy on the way.
- **Movement** (Tiles): how W, A, S and D walk. **Free**: freely, as in a first-person shooter, stepping into the next tile when you cross into it. **Tiles**: a press walks exactly one tile, to the middle of the neighbouring tile that way (one of eight, relative to the view: W ahead, W and D together ahead-right), at walking speed, and holding the key walks on, a tile at a time. A held key stops at a closed door: press again to open it and walk through (**O** opens it without walking in). While a shot or another action is going on, presses do nothing, and a key held through it needs a fresh press. Either way the numpad hops a tile at a time, almost at once (8 ahead, 2 back, 4 left, 6 right; 7 and 9 turn; a press during a shot is dropped too), and doors, ledges, jumps, lifts and diagonal gaps work the same.
- **Walking speed** (4): how fast the soldier walks in 3D, in tiles per second, from 1 to 8. Only the pace changes: every tile entered still costs its normal time units. It sets the pace up and down too, at half of it: a level up or down a lift or a ladder, flying or falling, takes as long as walking a tile and a half at half the speed (0.75 s at 4; a fall speeds up as it goes, and the view dips a little on landing).
- **Flight cost per level** (Rules; **Rules**, **0** to **24**): **Rules**: each unit pays what the game charges it to rise or sink a level (its armor's own cost, 8 for the flying suit), so units that fly slower stay slower. A number: the time units every flying (or, under the sea, swimming) soldier pays to rise or sink one level with SUIT FLY switched on in 3D, from 0 (free) to 24. The original game charges about 8.
- **Jump over low obstacles** (NO): YES: Jump (Space) while a movement key pushes into something low that blocks the step (a fence, a table, a crate, a rock: up to 15 voxels high, of a level's 24; the game's shapes come in layers of two voxels, so 14 in practice) takes the soldier over it, onto the next tile, or the one beyond an object. Forwards or sideways, straight along the map's grid; not backwards. It costs 30 time units in all, the walk included (more only if the walk itself costs more), and needs them left after any reserve. No climbing onto ledges (that would change the game). It bends the rules a little, which is why it's a setting. NO: Space is just a hop on the spot (1 time unit in your turn), and the original rules decide what blocks you.
- **Falling** (YES): YES: you can walk off a ledge and fall to the level below, by the game's own fall, hurt by the height with *Fall damage* (Combat). NO: ledges stop you, as the map's paths do; a soldier flying (flight on) still crosses the air, as on the map.

### Aiming and combat

- **FPS-style shooting (easier)** (NO): NO: every shot uses the game's own accuracy, and the hit chance shows by the crosshair (this is the setting called *Faithful X-COM targeting* before 1.09, shown the other way round: a player saw new players switch it, thinking it the faithful way). **YES: FPS-style shooting**, far easier than the game. Rounds fly along the crosshair with a spread (the soldier's firing skill and kneeling tighten it; the accuracy line under the crosshair shows it, see *Accuracy line*), recoil lifts your aim with every round and eases back, and each round flies at a bullet's pace. Each round leaves from your eye's place on the tile (leaning out too), at the gun's height, so what you see past a corner you can hit: the game's own shots leave from a muzzle point on the tile's edge towards the target, up to seven voxels from where you look from, and past a corner the crosshair cleared they met the wall (a player's screenshot: the laser into the near wall). The game's three fire-mode slots become: **SNAP** = one round from the hip, **AIMED** = one tight round, **AUTO** = hold the trigger to keep firing until you let go, the clip is empty or the time units run out. Each gun offers only the modes it has in the game ([table](#5-fire-modes-per-weapon-fps-style-shooting)). Time units, ammo and damage stay the game's.
- **Recoil** (YES): each shot kicks the view up for a moment (and, with *FPS-style shooting*, climbs it round by round in a burst, easing back after). NO: the view stays where you put it; the gun in hand still kicks back, and the spread still grows in a burst (that's accuracy, not the view). For players whom a moving view bothers.
- **Akimbo** (Off): **Full** or **One-hand**: a soldier with a gun in each hand fires both on every shot, the two taking turns round by round (each gun with its own fire mode and ammo, see *Akimbo volley*), but each is 20% less accurate (FPS style: the spread a quarter wider), and the pair costs the more expensive gun's shot and a tenth more (two pistols: one pistol's snap shot and a tenth, not two; energy the same way). A shotgun in either hand fires its pellets. The other gun fires the same mode if it has it, otherwise its first shot, and its whole burst however long the chosen gun's is (a 4-round and a 5-round gun: 9 rounds; the crosshair counts both). No aimed shots with two guns that pair (who aims two guns at once?): Aimed isn't offered (the wheel skips it, and its key says so); **Y** switches akimbo off to aim. An aimed shot picked on the classic HUD all the same fires the chosen gun alone. Without the time units for both, only the chosen hand fires, at full accuracy. When either gun's shot arcs (a grenade launcher or a Panzerfaust in the big mods), the two fire in turn instead: the chosen gun first, and once its shot has landed the other one at the same target, each at full accuracy and its own cost (the crosshair says IN TURN). The 3D view shows the second gun on the other side, and the info panel shows both guns' ammo. Two-handed weapons also take the game's own one-handed penalty, and a gun that blocks both hands (the game's red 2: it won't fire with the other hand full) never pairs. **One-hand**: only with guns that aren't two-handed: two pistols fire together, while a rifle or a rocket launcher fires alone, at full accuracy, whatever is in the other hand. In battle, **Y** switches akimbo off and back on to the kind set here; with the setting **Off** it does nothing (a player: a finger slipping onto Y switched akimbo on). **Off**: one hand at a time, as in the game (G switches hands). (Before 1.09 these were two settings, *Full akimbo* and *Realistic akimbo*, now Full and One-hand; the one that was on carries over.)
- **Akimbo volley** (NO): how two guns fire. Either way each is 20% less accurate, and the pair costs the more expensive gun's shot and a tenth. NO: they take turns round by round, the chosen gun first (A, B, A, B...; the gun with the longer burst finishes alone), each round its own as an auto burst's: aimed as it leaves, its hit, deaths and destroyed walls worked out before the next one leaves, so a burst can kill one alien, then go on through to the next behind it; the crosshair says DUAL. YES: both at once, a volley: the other gun's round flies beside the chosen one's and their hits land together, as a shotgun's pellets do (a round that gets there first shows its hit at once); the crosshair says VOLLEY. The rounds fly at the game's own speed either way. (Arcing guns, which always fire gun after gun, keep their own rule: see *Full akimbo*.) The *Akimbo style* key (unbound: set one under 3D VIEW) switches it in battle, and the warning line says which.
- **Lean shots** (NO): YES: a soldier standing more than a couple of voxels to one side of the tile's middle (walk or step sideways within the tile), across the line to the target, shoots from that side, to peek round a corner: the game's own off-centre origins (the ones its "off-centre shooting" option tries), a little to the left or right. If that side has no line of fire to the unit aimed at, the shot comes from the middle as usual. It needs *Movement: Free* (with Tiles the eye always settles in the middle of the tile); leaning out (setting *Lean*) shoots from its side either way. NO: shots start from the middle, as in the game. (With the game's own targeting; FPS-style rounds always leave from the eye.)
- **Throwing** (Classic): how throws and arcing shots made from the 3D view are aimed. **Classic** (by tile): as on the map, at the middle of the tile under the crosshair, from one of the game's eight fixed points round the unit; the curve snaps from tile to tile as you turn, and a 2x2 unit's start jumps from side to side. **Modern** (continuous): at the exact point under the crosshair (a throw: that point on the tile's floor), from a start that slides round the unit as the view turns, so the curve moves smoothly with the aim and the throw lands where you point (the game's accuracy still applies). The preview and the throw agree in both; orders given on the map are always Classic.
- **Lean** (Off): **Peek** or **Tilt**: holding **Left Shift** with **A** or **D** leans the soldier out to that side, to peek round a corner (A and D don't strafe while Shift is held; the lean key is rebindable, and each side can have a key of its own: *Lean (hold with strafe)*, *Lean left / right* under Controls). The eye moves up to six voxels to the side and a little lower, stopping short of walls and of anyone in sight (never into a wall or a teammate's head); letting go straightens up. With **Tilt** the view tilts about ten degrees with it, as a head does; with **Peek** it stays level. A shot fired while leaning out starts from that side (the same off-centre origins as *Lean shots*). The soldier stays on their tile, and who they see is still up to the game: the lean shows the street round the corner, not an alien the soldier hasn't spotted. **Off**: Shift does nothing and A and D strafe, as before. (In 0.9 the lean was on Q and E, which then weren't the hand keys; they are again.)
- **Melee hits walls and downed** (NO): a melee blow at the wall, door or object under the crosshair strikes it, by the isometric map's own terrain melee (in a mod that switches it on, the 3D view does so whatever this setting): within a tile and a half of the eye, on the soldier's own tile or one next to it, diagonally and a level up or down too (on stairs, the walls of both floors ahead; the map's own strikes only straight ahead), damaged by the game's own terrain damage if the weapon can damage terrain (a stun rod can't). A blow at the floor or ceiling under the crosshair within reach strikes it too (the floor at your feet or around, the ceiling above: the game's own hit, and if it breaks, the game's falling; a body lying there takes the blow first), which the map's own terrain melee can't. YES also switches that on for the original games, and lets a blow hit a unit lying unconscious (it can't dodge; the game's own damage, as a shot at the body would do), at the blow's usual cost: the body under the crosshair at the soldier's feet or on a tile next to them, diagonally too (the soldier turns to it first, paid with *Turning costs TU*; one farther off is out of range), or with none under the crosshair, one in front of the soldier or at their feet.
- **Aim at exact point** (YES): YES: shots go for the exact point under the crosshair; on the open sky, the point of air on the crosshair's line, up to 20 tiles out (nearer where the line leaves the map or the weapon's range ends): the game takes an empty tile as a target as readily, and the round flies on past the point along the same line. If the line from your weapon to the point meets something else first (the gun sits on the tile's edge, beside your eye), it's tried from the game's other muzzle points, the middle and, with OXCE's *Off-centre shooting* on, left and right of it (as the game tries them for a unit), and fires from the one that clears; if none does, the shot goes as the game's own aim would instead: at any part of the unit (or the object, the wall) a line reaches, from the middle, and left and right of it with OXCE's *Off-centre shooting* on; the path shows the line it takes. An arcing shot (a grenade launcher, a Panzerfaust in the big mods) goes for the exact point too, a spot on the floor rather than the tile's middle; where no curve reaches it, the game's own points on the tile. NO: like the original game, shots go for the tile and the game picks the spot (the middle of a unit, the object, the wall...; an empty tile's middle on the open sky). The game's accuracy applies either way, and the hit chance shows on the open sky too. (FPS-style shooting always aims at the exact point.)
- **Close range: from the eye** (NO): with the game's own targeting (*FPS-style shooting* off). YES: a straight shot at something within two tiles of your eye (32 voxels) leaves from the eye's place across the tile instead of the game's muzzle point, so up close the round goes where the crosshair is. The game's muzzle point sits on the tile's edge towards the target, and to a side with a lean: at something a tile or two away its line often can't reach what the eye sees, and the shot preview's laser then ends elsewhere (marked **OFF**). Only where the shot starts changes: the accuracy roll, the line of fire from there and everything else stay the game's. Arcing shots and throws keep the game's origin. NO: every shot from the game's muzzle point, as on the map.
- **Show trajectory** (All): while aiming, the path the next throw or shot will take: a throw's or an arcing shot's curve as light cyan dashes fading away from you, with a diamond where it lands (yellow for a forced shot, see *Force arcing shots*); a straight shot's line as a thin red laser from your weapon, with a brighter dot where it meets something; a shot the game will refuse (its own targeting: no line of fire) shows its laser dimmed, a dotted dark red, so "No line of fire" never comes as a surprise. Under night vision the path is light in the goggles, as a laser sight is: a white-green core in a green glare, the dot white in the middle; a forced arc is dotted there, since colour can't tell it apart. It's the game's own path, worked out as the game will (the curve it picks for a throw, the line of fire for a shot), only without the accuracy roll: the real one scatters as the hit chance says.
