It starts from the side the shot will leave from, so leaning (see *Lean shots*, *Lean*) moves it with the shot. **Arcs**: throws and arcing shots (a grenade launcher or a Panzerfaust in the big mods, a spitting alien's weapon); nothing shows where the game couldn't throw. **All**: every shot's line too, up to the first thing it meets. **Off**: none.
- **Confirm fire** (NO): YES: the first click marks the target and shows its path (whatever *Show trajectory* says), with "Click again to fire"; a click on the same spot fires, a click elsewhere moves the mark, the right button forgets it. Holding the trigger after a confirmed burst keeps firing. NO: one click fires, as always.
- **Force blocked shots** (NO): with the game's own targeting (*FPS-style shooting* off), a straight shot with no line of fire, neither to the point under the crosshair nor to any part of the target (off-centre too, with OXCE's option), fires anyway, as with Ctrl held: along the line to the crosshair, the accuracy roll as always, so a round may clear thin cover (a fence top, a crate's edge) or hit it. Its path shows yellow, its dot on what's in the way. NO: the game refuses it ("No line of fire").
- **Instant chain explosions** (NO): YES: in 3D, a chain of explosive terrain (barrels, fuel, the big mods' explosive walls and machines) goes up as one big blast. The blast that sets it off (a grenade's, a rocket's, or the first barrel's that your shot set off) plays as ever, with the big explosion's sound, and the whole chain settles in that same moment; its fireballs ripple out from it, a fireball at each explosive in the order the game sets them off, a moment apart (closer together in a long chain, the whole ripple within a second and a half), each tile's wreckage and fire showing as the fireball nearest to it grows over it, and its dead fall once the ripple has passed (the battle waits for that, once). Long chains no longer keep you waiting or load the computer with blast after blast. The outcome is the game's own, only not played out. An explosion that sets nothing off (a grenade on open ground, a shot at one lone barrel) plays exactly as ever. On the isometric map, and with NO, the game's chain, blast by blast.
- **Realistic hitboxes** (NO; named *Full sprite hitboxes* until the rulings on GitHub issue #193, and so in the history below): YES: a unit is hit, and seen, exactly where its full sprite (its picture) shows, not by the game's voxel shapes: in every line of fire (everyone's shots, the AI's and reaction fire's checks, "no line of fire", and so sight too, which the game checks along the same lines: a unit is seen when its picture is), in 3D and on the map alike. The picture stands where the 3D view draws it, its feet on the floor's top, as wide as it is drawn: what it reaches over the next tiles counts there too (a bat's wings, a Cyberdisc's rim). Its frame is the one seen from that side (its turn, kneeling, its animation, made again as its pose changes) at the *Unit size*. A line meets it at the very point where it crosses the picture: a hit on a solid pixel, on through a see-through one (between legs, round arms), thin parts too. Your own shots in 3D meet the picture exactly as you see it, leaning as it leans when you look up or down, the smooth one up close with *Sprite upscaling*; the crosshair finds a unit exactly where it shows, and an FPS-style round goes through the point under the crosshair. Everyone else's lines meet the picture upright, facing them. A throw's or an arcing shot's curve (a bow, a grenade launcher) meets the picture facing the hand it left, wherever the curve crosses it, and an arcing shot at a big unit (two tiles by two) goes for its middle, where its picture stands, and lands on it over whichever of its tiles it comes down. So a shot between the legs passes, one at a gun held out hits, and a kneeling soldier is as small as he looks: a shot just over his head goes over. Only the art a unit's picture lays flat on the floor in front of its feet is never hit. The hit's damage, armour side and body part are the game's, worked out on the unit's own tiles. With *Voxel units* the unit is its 3D body instead, as it shows to whoever fires (see *Voxel units*). The picture is the hitbox, so *Hitboxes* outlines what the lines test, a line round it in the colour of the unit's side (Aimed at, Blink, Always or Off, as for the boxes). A *Voxel units* body is outlined the same way, round the body as drawn. Melee reach stays the game's, by its own shapes, so a blow reaches a big unit as before. NO: the game's own hit shapes. In short: NO is the classic hitbox, as the original game has it; YES means the whole body shown, a picture or a *Voxel units* body.
- **Quick deaths** (NO): YES: a unit killed or knocked out by a shot or a blow is down at once for the battle, as one blasted by an explosion always was, so nothing waits for its fall: a burst fires on (its next rounds fly on to whoever is behind), several units fall at the same time, and the battle goes on at once. The fall still shows, on its own: the game's own fall frames at the game's own pace (about 0.3 s; nothing waits for them), with no turn to face the screen first, in 3D and on the map, its body and what it dropped appearing as it lands. When the soldier you look through goes down, their fall plays on their helmet camera card (the view keeling over, washing red, or dark blue if knocked out, then the static of a dead camera, for 3 seconds) while the view goes on to the next soldier at once, out of a moment's static, and the game selects that soldier as soon as it is free. With nobody left standing, the full death camera as before (the battle is lost). NO: the game's own deaths, one after another, each held while the unit turns and falls.
- **Live motion scanner** (NO): YES: the motion scanner held up in 3D stays up as the soldier moves and through the aliens' turn, its screen showing what moves round them wherever they go: a live radar for its one use (a player called it "potentially neat", and an advantage the game never gives). NO: one look from where it was used, as the game's own scanner: a step to another tile or the end of your turn puts it away, and using it again costs its time units again; while it's up it still turns with the view and sweeps. One of the *Gameplay* presets' settings (on from FullQoL).
- **Auto-equip guns first** (YES since the rulings on GitHub issue #193; it was NO): YES: when the game equips soldiers who have no saved loadout (a New Battle's, or soldiers never equipped), it hands out guns before melee weapons, the hardest-hitting first (by the power of one round of the strongest ammo for them in the craft), direct fire before launchers (guns whose shots arc or whose every round is a blast: rockets, mortars, grenade launchers): two-handed guns, one-handed guns, then two-handed and one-handed melee weapons, ammo and the rest; the same every time. NO: OXCE's own auto-equip, which has no ranking at all: it counts a two-handed melee weapon as a rifle and hands the craft's kit out in the order the items happen to sit in memory, which changes between builds and even between runs (the same battle gave one soldier a rifle, then a musket), so in XPiratez or X-Com Files soldiers often start with staffs, claws and swords. A gun here is a firearm with a shot reaching past 12 tiles (some mods' melee weapons are firearms with a one-tile "shot"). NO: OXCE's own auto-equip, unchanged, as modders rank their items for it.
- **Obliteration in UFO and TFTD** (NO): YES: in the original games, explosives, lasers and plasma (TFTD's Gauss and Sonic weapons too) have overkill, as XPiratez and The X-Com Files give their explosives, plasma and many of their lasers: a unit they take below minus three quarters of its health (between XPiratez's armours, 0.75 to 1, and OXCE's own 0.5, which lost a plasma squad most of its corpses; the owner: "Make it 75%") dies leaving no corpse and no body to recover, as the map burns it black as it falls, and with *Obliteration* it bursts in 3D. Bullets, blades, stun and fire are as ever. This changes the games' rules: a plasma-armed squad loses many alien corpses, and their autopsies wait for others. It does nothing where a loaded mod gives any weapon overkill of its own (the big mods, which have their own). NO: the original games as they are: nothing is ever destroyed without a corpse by a hit (a blast still destroys bodies on the floor).

### Graphics and light

The flashlight and the tactical flares have no setting: they are on their keys, and a key you don't want can be left alone or unbound.

- The **flashlight** (**F**, every battle starts with it on) is a light on the weapon: a bright, tight round spot right on the crosshair with a faint halo round it, reaching 20 tiles, day or night, so a dark UFO, cellar or barn lights up by day too (in daylight it adds next to nothing). Only the 3D view is lit: what the soldiers see by the game's rules doesn't change. The suit light (L, or SUIT LIGHT) is separate: it is the game's personal light, round every soldier, by the game's rules (it lights their tiles, so aliens see them more easily at night).
- A **tactical flare** (**T**) is thrown at the crosshair, free and as often as you like. It bounces off walls and glass (the glass in a house's window shatters instead) and burns with red fire and red light for about a minute; under the sea it only goes a metre or two and bubbles hard. It lights the 3D view only.

- **Fall damage** (NO): with *Falling*, the landing hurts by the height fallen (a little from one level, a lot from two or more; armor takes some of it). NO: a fall never hurts; the view still jolts.
- **Smooth lighting** (YES): YES: the battlescape's light (sun, flares, fires, personal lights) blends smoothly from tile to tile. NO: tile by tile, as the isometric map shades it.
- **Shadows** (On; **Off**, **On**, and with *Renderer* GPU **Soft**, **Lamps**, **Soft+lamps**): ray-traced shadows from the sun by day, from street lamps, your flashlight and tactical flares at night, and under every unit; a unit lit by a flare also throws a soft shadow along the ground away from it (longer the lower the flare, up to three tiles). Turn them off on slow machines. **Soft**: the sun is a small disc instead of a point: four shadow rays go to it instead of one, so a shadow's edge fades, wider the further the shadow fell (a 25th of the way), instead of a hard stair-stepped line. About 1 ms at 1080p on an RTX 4060. **Lamps**: normally only the strongest lamp in reach is tried for a shadow, and the two strongest flares (to spare the CPU). With this the three strongest lamps in reach and every flare are: where the strongest lamp lights a wall, another lamp's shadow still falls on it. (Three, not all: a ray for each of a lit base's sixteen lamps cost 19 ms a frame at 1080p, and a fourth lamp's share is under a shade.) **Soft+lamps**: both.
- **Distance haze** (Normal): how soon far terrain fades into the haze, the sky's colour at the horizon. **Normal** from about half the view's reach (27 tiles at the usual 60), **Light** from three quarters, **Clear** only in the last tenth, so the world doesn't stop with a hard edge where the view ends; the reach is *View distance*. The fade blends the colours step by step, so far walls fade evenly. It also sets how thick the fog over unseen terrain is with *Unseen terrain* Fog: all of it at Normal, half at Light, none at Clear. Under the sea the murk stays as it is.
- **Unseen terrain** (Mist): how terrain your squad hasn't seen yet is drawn (the map's black, unexplored tiles; units and items there stay hidden whichever you pick, and the map itself doesn't change). **Mist**: plain slate shapes, never textured, turning to a soft misty blue-grey from about 10 tiles on (at most part of the way into the haze), fading out at the view's reach as all terrain does. **Fog**: the shapes darker the further off, as 0.9 drew them, in a fog that rises smoothly from the edge of your soldiers' sight (the game's own sight range, 20 tiles) to all fog at the view's reach, no flat band of grey where the seen ground ends; *Distance haze* sets how thick the fog is: Normal all of it, Light half, Clear none, so with Clear the dark shapes stand all the way out to the horizon, as in 0.9. **Visible**: unseen terrain drawn as it really is, its own colours, light and shadows, toned a little towards slate grey so you can still tell it from what your squad has seen, then the usual distance haze; the most work for the computer (every unseen pixel is lit and shaded), handy with a long *View distance* for screenshots of a whole map. With *Fog of war in 3D* Off, terrain the view itself sees within your soldiers' sight range is drawn as seen anyway; this setting is for the rest.
- **View distance** (60): how far the 3D view reaches, in tiles: 60, 80, 100, 130, 160 or 200 (a click goes round them; the other mouse button the other way). The distance haze closes in over the last stretch whatever the distance (see *Distance haze*), so the far end keeps a slight mist instead of a hard edge, and the ground beyond the map's edge stretches as far again. Further costs a little more where much of the world is in sight; units are still drawn only once your squad has seen them. At 60 a mod that sets its own reach for the 3D view (`maxViewDistance` in its `firstPerson:` rules) keeps it; the other values override it. The helmet and map cameras keep their own short reach (30 tiles).
- **Fog of war in 3D** (Off): the game explores the map tile by tile, and the isometric map shows only what it has explored; looking through the soldier's eyes that can be odd, as going down stairs, where the floor below stayed dark slate while the soldier looked straight at it, until they stood on it. **Off**: terrain the view itself sees within your soldiers' sight range (the game's own, 20 tiles) is drawn as seen, explored or not; beyond that range *Unseen terrain* draws it. **As map**: only what the game has explored is drawn as seen, as before. The 3D view only: the map, the game's own exploring and what the soldiers see by the rules don't change, and units and items the game hasn't spotted stay hidden either way (smoke and fire there too, as on the map).
- **Smoke** (Modern): how smoke looks, in two ways, both drawn where the game has smoke as the isometric map draws it: nothing solid where your soldier's sight runs out (the game hides units in smoke, not the world). **Modern**: the game's own smoke, made truly see-through. The original smoke is a stipple of dots that stood in for see-through smoke on the isometric map; here the dots are blurred into soft clouds (how densely they lie is how opaque the smoke is, their colours its colour, mostly grey), so the smoke keeps the game's shapes and colours but you see through it. Thin smoke, from a rocket say, is a light haze that's easy to spot; thicker smoke is a denser cloud, up to *Smoke opacity* at most: smoke over smoke builds up to it and no further, so a cloud never hides more than the setting. The clouds change slowly and smoothly from frame to frame, sway and bob a little, and a few wisps rise off them. **Original**: the original games' own smoke animation, exactly as the map draws it: their dots in their own colours (UFO's greys and browns, TFTD's white under the sea), solid, one frame a tile, the holes between the dots what you see through; the clouds stay put as on the map, and their frames melt smoothly into each other instead of blinking. No grey, no wisps, and an explosion's flash and embers without our grey puffs (the smoke it leaves is the game's). Eyes with heat vision (an armor's `heatVision`, as the big mods' drones and scout gear have) see the smoke thinner, in either look: 50% heat vision, half as thick (never less than a fifth, so it stays in sight). With both, units the soldier can see stay clear through the smoke; an enemy only squadmates see (its button green) isn't drawn when smoke lies between it and the soldier, only its marker, as for one behind a wall; and units the game hides stay hidden. Fire is drawn the same in both. (0.9's *See-through smoke* gives the first value: YES Modern, NO Original; 1.0's *Solid* becomes Original.)
- **Smoke opacity** (80%): the most smoke hides, for both looks (since a player's report, GitHub issue #145, and the owner's scale: "almost translucent at 5%", "completely opaque at 100%"). Modern: how opaque a cloud is in its body (its soft edges and wisps less), and smoke over smoke adds up to exactly this and no further. Original: how solid the map's dots are drawn, dots over dots up to this too. 0%: no smoke is drawn. 100%: nothing shows through (Modern a solid grey cloud, Original the map's solid dots). Heat vision still sees it thinner; a unit the soldier sees stays clear. 0 to 100%, in steps of 5.
- **Night smoke opacity** (45%): the same in the dark (a player: "non full smoke can be blinding for some players"): on a night mission, however lamps or the suit lights light the smoke, and by day where it hangs in the dark (an alien base, an unlit room), blended with the day value in between.
- **Weapon in hand** (YES): YES: the weapon in the soldier's hand shows at the bottom right of the view (bottom left in the left hand), Doom style, built in 3D from its inventory picture (little cubes, thickest in the middle). Every weapon is held barrel first with the barrel on the line to the crosshair, pistols, cannons and launchers as well as rifles (the games draw their weapons standing up, muzzle at the top), and your shots leave its muzzle with a flash and a kick, even a round fast enough to land before the next frame (a laser up close). It bobs as you walk, kicks when you fire and dips out of sight while reloading, looking far down (from 45°, gone at 72°) and zoomed in. A melee weapon is held tip up and swung; a sword the art draws leaning (the big mods' swords) comes down edge first (its edge is the side lower in the picture). Anything else in the hand (a grenade, a medi-kit, a clip) is built the same way and sits in the bottom corner, its lower half out of sight. With the classic HUD shown it stays hidden (the HUD covers the bottom of the view). NO: nothing drawn.
- **Sprite upscaling** (Finest): soldiers, aliens, items on the floor, explosions and smoke seen up close are scaled up the way pixel art scales well (xBRZ), in the game's own colours as the art uses them: every pixel takes a shade of a colour the original has right there, so no stray colour shows at an edge between two, and armour colours and the view's shading work on it as on the original. On the desktop **Finest** or **Off**; the Meta Quest also has **Smooth** and **Smooth+** (a desktop profile on either has Finest now). **Smooth** rounds off the stair-stepped edges and softens the steps of the shading inside each colour, never across an outline or into another colour; **Smooth+** also sharpens the shading a little; **Finest** does that six times bigger instead of four, finer still up close, and keeps one-pixel eyes and highlights as they are (the bigger copies take 2.25 times the memory: about 60 MB at most); **Off** keeps the original pixels. The same frame always comes out the same, so animations don't flicker. (A profile saved before 1.11's update keeps its choice.)
- **Unit size** (100%): soldiers and aliens drawn a little smaller, 80 to 100% of the isometric map's size, standing on the same spot, so tall sprites fit under low ceilings. Corpses and items on the floor shrink with them, so a soldier going down doesn't grow into a bigger corpse. Only the picture changes: what shots hit stays the game's (see *Hitboxes*; at about 80 to 90% the sprites come closest to their hitboxes' heights).
- **Muzzle flashes** (YES): YES: gunfire lights up its surroundings for a blink, units flash when hit, the view flashes red when your soldier is hit (and at each shot of a berserk soldier), and a blast flashes. NO: none of these flashes, UFO lights hold steady, and the helmet cameras' bad signal (coarse blocks, green squares, torn bands, a frozen picture) and their spells of static stay away. Turn this off if flashing light bothers you (photosensitivity, epilepsy).
- **Flashlight** (Beam): **Beam**: a spot from the weapon on the crosshair, fading with range, to 20 tiles. **Far light**: a light standing where the crosshair's line first meets the world, a pool six tiles across, as bright at any range.
- **Screen shake** (YES): explosions nearby, hits taken, near misses, falls and landings, melee blows, panic and your own shots shake the view for a moment. NO: nothing shakes it (*Head bob* and *Recoil* are their own settings).
- **Obliteration** (YES): a unit killed beyond its overkill, which the game leaves no corpse of (the isometric map burns it black as it falls; the big mods' heavy weapons do it, the original games' damage never does), bursts into the pieces of its body at the hit: the cubes of its body with *Voxel units*, else the pixels of its picture, each piece in its own colour. They fly away from the side the hit came from (outwards from a blast, straight up from one at its feet), char as they fly as the map burns the unit (more the further the hit went past its health: some stay their colour, some go dark, the brightest burn away), bounce off walls and lie on the floor for a few seconds. Flesh sprays blood and leaves a mist of it, in the colour its corpse's picture shows its blood in (what the corpse shows and the living unit never does: a soldier's red, a Sectoid's, a Snakeman's or a Chryssalid's green, a Floater's purple; the game's red where nothing tells), with *Voxel units* too; a vehicle, or a robot its mod makes immune to bleeding, throws sparks and smoke; fire, lasers and plasma make the charred pieces glow as embers. A blast that destroys a body lying on the floor (a corpse, or one out cold) bursts it the same way. Only what the squad sees, and none with *Particles* Off. NO: the unit falls and is gone, as on the map.
- **Futuristic night vision** (YES): with night vision on (**N**), YES leaves units, bodies and items on the floor their own colours, as the isometric map does, while the world goes green (smoke thick over them greens them too). NO: realistic night vision, everything green.
- **Particles** (All): **Off** none; **Effects** sparks, debris, smoke, fire and the shots' trails (a rocket's smoke and the white specks it leaves hanging for about ten seconds, embers, the glow behind plasma); **All** also dust, fireflies, bubbles and weather. Shots look the same in all three: the rounds, beams and blobs, their start at the muzzle, the flash on the weapon, the kick and the marks they leave aren't particles.
- **UFO lights and aura** (NO): UFOs stand out from the Earth around them. Their hulls stay still and only their lights glow (lit panels, lift pads, screens, navigation lights, found in the original animation frames), pulsing gently in a slow wave round the UFO. Each UFO casts its own two-coloured light (its hull's hue close in, its lights' hue further out) on the ground around it, its underside and its skin, strongest at night, and colours its inside evenly, a fifth of the way to its light by day (each colour kept as bright as it was, strong colours kept almost as they are, so the art stays sharp); drifting motes and sparks wrap it from outside, never on its own tiles, and a beam of light rises up each grav lift. It all shows only once the UFO has been seen, so it never gives a UFO away; with Muzzle flashes off nothing pulses. The small scout's blocky hull is trimmed to its sprite's round outline (only the look: line of fire is still the game's). NO: the original animation frames (calmed, as everywhere in 3D).
- **Parts without shape** (All): some parts the map draws have no shape in the game's data, so the 3D view had nothing to show: the wooden ladders of the big mods (Piratez), the upper pads of the UFOs' grav lifts (they showed as holes) and some lifts' pads in TFTD. **Lifts** shows those with the grav lift flag (lifts and ladders), and the cages of bars round some lifts (Cydonia's pyramids), as see-through ghosts of their art: a ladder stands upright where the map draws it, a pad lies on the floor, a cage's bars stand on all four sides of the tile (the map draws only the back two); dotted, a little thicker up close (half the pixels) than from afar (a third), so you see through a lift pad to whoever is above or below it. They are only a picture: shots, sight and walking go through them as the game says. **Lifts** also makes a solid body from the art of a big object without a shape that no one can walk through (XPiratez's consoles), fills in a flat floor without a shape that is no lift's pad (a pool's water in XPiratez), solid as the map draws it, and stands up low parts without a shape, no higher than a knee: crops (X-Com Files' and XPiratez's fields), rubble heaps, the debris of a blown wall, a scatter of broken glass; each column of the art stands where it meets the floor, as tall as the art there, so from the map's side it looks just as on the map (from the side, as thin cards), and drawn solid as on the map, not dotted, though walking and shots go through it as in the game; art lying flat on the floor (paint, shards of glass) is laid on top of the floor. A field drawn as a block of crops though the game gives it only a few voxels (UFO's wheat, also in X-Com Files and XPiratez: a block of wheat 14 voxels tall on four thin posts; UFO's low crop rows; the town gardens' flower beds) stands as a solid block with **Lifts** or **All**: its top at the crops' height, the ears on it, its sides where the field ends; whoever stands in it shows from the waist up, and kneeling you see wheat. **All** does the same for every part without a shape (some mods draw laser walls or decorations that way); **Off** shows none. The original games' own such parts that stand in the way have shapes made for them in the mod (`firstPerson: shapes:`, boxes of voxels cut to the art's outline): with **Lifts** or **All** UFO's shop racks, and in TFTD a platform in the liner's hold, the foam along its hull, the sunken plane's door frames, the box on the Triton's back, a stalk in the alien ship and a clamp in the base's hangar, stand as ghosts in their real shape, at the *Ghost opacity* setting like every ghost. A floor the map draws as a box of liquid, slowing whoever wades through it, gets a ghost surface at the box's height with **Lifts** or **All**: XPiratez's sewers under its bases are a green block of sludge about half a level tall (the game gives it only a floor's thin slab), and a soldier wading through it shows through the surface from the thighs down, as the map draws them sunk in it; kneeling, your eye is just under it. Found by the art alone (a floor drawn as a tile-wide box, voxels only in its slab, walking slower than on a plain floor), so any mod drawing liquid that way gets it; in UFO and TFTD no floor is drawn so. And a floor or wall the game gives only a few voxels where its art draws a whole grating, railing or mesh, so shots go through (XPiratez's catwalks in Chem Town, nine voxels a layer; railings with posts on every other layer; TFTD's cargo ship and port railings and chain-link fences; UFO's iron railings and hedges), has the rest of its art drawn as a ghost in its own plane (the floor's, the wall's edge), cut to the art, so a grating keeps its holes; its voxels stay solid, as the game has them. Found by the art too: the voxels all in the part's plane, filling under half of what the art draws there; not doors, whose open art shows the leaves swung. TFTD's own floors without a shape (the sea round islands, ports and ships, a strip of the cargo ship and T'leth's great lid) are drawn solid as on the map, with any setting.
- **Open doors as ghosts** (YES): what is left of a sliding door once it has slid open (a UFO's door, the Lightning's, a mod's electric door) is drawn as the parts without shape are: seen through, never in the way, and not at all with *Parts without shape* off. The game has an open door as no obstacle and nothing to see past; the picture of its last frame still shows part of it, and **NO** draws that solid, as before.
- **See-through** (Crops; **Off**, **Crops**, **All**): **Crops**: the crops, flower beds and rubble that *Parts without shape* stands up solid as the map draws them (a wheat field's block, a heap of rubble) drawn a bit see-through instead, three pixels of four near or far, so whoever stands in a field shows. **All**: also a corpse or item whose picture is under the crosshair (or the free cursor) is drawn faint, at the ghost opacity, while you aim past it, at a unit, a wall or another floor: the game's shots pass through items, and a tall corpse (XPiratez's big beasts) hid what they reach. Aimed at the floor it lies on, it stays solid. **Off**: all solid.
- **Discreet map edge** (NO): YES: no yellow tape and posts round the map; small red dots on the floor along its edge, a tile apart, mark it instead, with the land beyond as before.
- **Pixel size** (1): **Auto** gives full detail up to a 640×400 view and blocks of 2 on bigger ones (a 1920×1080 screen at the default battlescape scale makes a 960×540 view); **1** full detail; **2** or **3**: each ray colours a block of 2×2 or 3×3 pixels, chunkier but much faster on big screens or slow machines. A click goes round them: Auto, 1, 2, 3, then Auto again; the other mouse button goes the other way. With *Renderer* GPU, Auto is always full detail. The small cameras (a soldier's card, the map camera up to Medium: views of at most 320×200 pixels) always have full detail: blocks made mush of them, and they cost little.

### HUD

- **Crosshair** (YES): shows the crosshair. Its shape tells the fire mode: an open cross for snap, a scope ring for aimed, brackets with one dot per round for auto, an arc for a throw, a diamond for a launcher, claws for melee, rings for psi. A hit on an enemy in sight puts four white ticks on its diagonals for a moment (red and longer for a kill); a round or blow that struck one but did no harm (bounced off the armour, rolled nothing, met a full resistance) puts a little blue shield below the crosshair's left instead. A door under the crosshair (a swinging one or a UFO's, once you've seen it) shows a little green door to its left, gone when the crosshair moves off it or onto a unit in front of it. NO: no crosshair (the shot info and the hit ticks stay).
- **Shot info** (Costs; **Off**, **Basic**, **Costs**, **Damage**, **Full**): **Basic** shows the fire mode, the hit chance and the time unit cost right of the crosshair (with *Crosshair* off, a little lower, so the middle of the view stays clear) (DUAL in front when both guns fire). A melee blow's hit chance is the game's own, as the map's action menu shows it for the blow (**MELEE 23% 4TU**), in either targeting style. Yellow: you can; red: you can't (not enough time units, no ammo, out of range). **Costs**: under it also the action's other costs, those it has: **EN** energy, **HP** health, **STUN**, **MOR** morale, **MP** mana ("EN 6  STUN 2"; a gain, a mod's negative cost, with a plus). Both guns' with dual wield, as the game charges them. A cost the soldier can't pay is red, and so is the crosshair: the game would refuse it. The original games' weapons cost only time units, so there it shows nothing; the big mods' weapons often cost energy. (A unit's name under the crosshair moves a line lower while it shows.) **Damage**: instead, under the hit chance, the shot's damage range, as the map shows it with Alt held: the power of what deals the damage (the ammo loaded, or the weapon itself for a blow, a throw, a psi amp) with your soldier's stat bonuses (strength for blows, firing for some mods' guns), less its dropoff over the distance to what the crosshair points at, as the lowest and highest damage its type rolls (**0-60** for UFO's rifle, the damage roll being 0 to 200%; a star after it for the two-dice kind); a psi attack its odds' range (**12-67%**); **?-?** while the weapon or its ammo isn't researched. In the damage type's colour, as the ammo tag (AP grey-white, HE orange, IN red...). The other costs go a line lower. **Full**: both.
- **Rangefinder** (Off): the range to what you look at, **in tiles**. **Digital**: left of the crosshair, **RANGE 12**: how many tiles away the point under it is, counted as the game counts a shot's range (a dash at the sky). **Analogue**: a scale left of the crosshair, in the manner of the PSO-1 scope's: a level line marked **1.7m** and a dotted curve over it with five numbers. Put a standing unit's feet on the line: the number where its head meets the curve is its range in tiles (the 1.7 m is the height of the unit the scale is drawn for, a soldier; the numbers are tiles; a small alien reads farther than it is). Zoomed in, the scale grows with the picture and reads the same. The key *Range (hold)* (no key until you give it one) shows the rangefinder while held, in the aliens' turn too (the digital one when this setting is Off).
- **Accuracy line** (NO): a green line under the crosshair, with a small notch up at each end, as wide as a shot can stray to either side (grey while it's not your move). With the game's own targeting (*FPS-style shooting* off), for a straight shot: as far as a **successful** accuracy roll can send the round sideways from the point under the crosshair, by the game's own roll (OXCE's `Projectile::applyAccuracy`). That bright middle is short: a roll under the accuracy strays at most half of a deviation that grows with the range (about 1.3 degrees to either side for a shot north or south on the map, half that east or west, unless OXCE's *Uniform shooting spread* is on; up or down far less), whatever the soldier's skill; how likely that is, the hit chance says. Beyond it, **dotted and dimmer**, the line runs out as far as a **missed** roll can send the round: the game's miss deviation goes up to 150 less the hit chance where a hit's goes up to 9, so at 16% nearly fifteen times as wide, at 90% under seven (none at 100%). At a low hit chance most rounds miss and spray out to the dotted ends; zoomed in, the dotted part can reach the view's edges. In FPS style: the spread, which 95% of rounds land within, widening with the bloom as you fire. While it shows, a pointed unit's name sits under it. NO: no line.
- **Unit names** (YES): the name of the unit you point at (the unit whose hitbox the game's line through the crosshair, or the free cursor, meets, as a shot would; else whose picture is there), just under the crosshair, as the game's unit screen names it: an alien by its race (or race and rank, where a mod shows those), a civilian by kind, a soldier by name, in green for X-COM, red for aliens, yellow for civilians.
- **Stat bars** (Scaled): how the bars of time units, energy, health and morale read, on the map and in 3D. The game draws a pixel per point and cuts a longer stat off at the bar's end (a player: a 300-HP unit with 100 health gone still showed a full bar; in 3D, whose bars are short, morale above 52 always looked full). **Scaled**: a pixel per point while every maximum of the stat among your units fits the bar, else one smaller scale for all of them, so a longer maximum is still a longer bar and two soldiers stay comparable (every vanilla soldier fits the map's bars, which look as before; the 3D view's are short, so there most stats shrink, all soldiers' alike). **Percent**: each bar full at its own maximum. **Game's own**: a pixel per point, cut off at the bar's end. The numbers beside the bars always show the real values.
- **Hitboxes** (Always): outlines round the units, the voxels the game counts as that unit when a shot or a blow reaches it (its armor's footprint, from the floor to its height, kneeling or standing): green for X-COM, red for aliens, yellow for civilians. The sprites are only pictures, often taller or wider than what can be hit. With *Realistic hitboxes* the picture is what can be hit, so the outline goes round what the lines of fire really test, worked out the same way they are, so the outline is the hit shape itself: a line a pixel wide just outside its edge, in the same colours, wherever what's beside it is behind the unit (a wall or another unit in front of it is never outlined, nor the art lying flat on the floor in front of its feet, which is never hit). It hugs the picture; where it doesn't, that part can't be hit, or something nearer covers it. **Aimed at**: only the unit you point at (its hitbox or its picture under the crosshair, as for its name), steady, so a shot at its picture that would miss shows at once; **Blink**: every unit in sight, the outlines fading in and out once a second; **Always**: every unit in sight, steady; **Off**: none. *Glowing outlines* makes either kind glow.
- **Glowing outlines** (NO): YES: the hitboxes' outlines and the path of a throw glow, spilling a soft light round them (the bloom). NO: plain lines.
- **Downed unit icons** (Always since the rulings on GitHub issue #193; it was Crosshair): small icons for a unit lying unconscious: a flame if it's burning, a drop of blood if it's bleeding (fatal wounds), a bolt if its health is draining away (shock), and Zz, out cold. Our own pixel art, in the order the game's own floor indicators take where a mod has them. **Crosshair**: for the body you point at (the crosshair, or the free cursor), just under the crosshair. **Always**: over every body in sight. **Off**: none. Only bodies you can see get them, not one behind a wall or in smoke too thick to see through.
- **Enemy markers** (Clear shot): the numbered arrows over the enemies, in the colours of the game's own buttons for them at the right, as on the isometric map: **red**, the soldier you look through sees it; **green**, only the squad does (another soldier sees it, or one saw it this turn and it hasn't moved since: the game keeps it shown). **Clear shot**: of the green ones, those this soldier has a clear line to are **yellow**: in his view (the game's 90 degrees), no wall, object or unit in the way, and still he doesn't see it, for the smoke or fire on the line, the dark (X-COM sees 9 tiles in the dark), the enemy's camouflage or the distance. A shot can get there, at the game's odds (where a mod has a penalty for shooting at what the soldier can't see, it counts). **As map**: red and green only. **All red**: every marker red, as before. An enemy nobody sees has no button and no marker, whatever the setting, and our own wounded and stunned soldiers (the blue and purple buttons) have theirs with *Wounded markers*. Each yellow is the first half of the game's own sight test (the view's cone and the line past walls, objects and units): at most one such line is worked out a frame, each kept for a quarter of a second while nobody moves.
- **Wounded markers** (On): the same numbered arrows over your own the game flags at the right with a button after the enemies', in that button's colour, as on the isometric map: **blue**, wounded (fatal wounds; burning too with OXCE's hidden `oxceShowBurningAsWounded`); **purple**, about to pass out (stunned to three quarters of the health, or out cold with the health draining away, in mods that have that). A soldier with both buttons has both markers, stacked as the buttons are, the first at the bottom; a body lying out cold has its marker over where it lies, a body being carried over whoever carries it (where its button centres the map). They're the game's own buttons read as they show, so they always agree with them: the same units, numbers and colours. None over the soldier you look through (his button is there, his marker isn't). Like the enemy markers they're drawn over everything (what the squad knows, not what's in view), kept at the edge of the view, and hidden with the HUD. **Off**: none, as before.
- **Damage numbers** (Off; **Off**, **On**, **Overkill**): **On**: when a unit is hit, numbers rise over it and fade away in about a second and a half, side by side: the health the hit took after the armour, in red; the armour points it took off the side it struck (the game wears armour down as it's hit), in blue; what an energy shield lost, in white and first (the big mods' shields: X-Com Files, XPiratez); the stun it dealt, in purple; each only when there is some, and a grey **0** for a hit that did none of these (the armour stopped it all). They're the game's own numbers, read where it deals the damage, so they match what the unit lost. Every hit gets one, whoever struck, yours and the aliens' (reaction fire too): a shot, a blow, and each unit a blast hurts its own; but only on a unit your squad can see. In 3D it hangs in the world over the unit's head (over its red marker), hidden behind walls like anything else there, and stays the same size on the screen, so it reads far off too (none over the soldier you look through: the map shows theirs); one right under the crosshair rises over it, clear of the shot info; on the map it rises over the unit's sprite. Hits close together stack upwards, the newest at the bottom, and a blast's numbers over units side by side step up over each other. The game's small font. NO: none. **Overkill**: the same, and a killing hit that took more health than the unit had shows the rest in orange after the red: **1 +52** is a unit on 1 health hit for 53. Most of the game's damage types stop a unit's health at 0, so a killing blow's red number is only what was left (a player, GitHub issue #262).
- **Detailed hit log** (NO): the game's own hit log (Ctrl+H; the turn's diary, Ctrl+Alt+H) works in 3D exactly as on the map: your weapon's name when you pick it (in 3D, at the first shot of a pick, see the history), an arrow for each round, thrown thing or blow, and for each unit a hit hurts 0 (nothing got through), hit (up to a fifth of its full health) or HIT (more); "Reaction fire..." before the enemy's shots. YES: after each hit's word, in brackets, whom it hurt (a soldier's name, an alien's or a civilian's kind, as the game names them), the health it lost and the stun it took, and "killed" or "down" when that hit put it out: HIT (Sectoid Soldier 30, stun 2, killed), HIT (a soldier's name 32, stun 4), hit (Sectoid Soldier stun 15). A unit your squad doesn't see is only "unseen", with no numbers. On the map too. A log too long for the window's big letters is shown in its small ones, as the game does with any message.
- **Compass** (YES): a compass at the top right. The disc turns as you turn, with N, E, S and W going round it, under a fixed white arrow on top that shows your heading; a yellow triangle on the rim shows the way the soldier faces. With *Turning costs TU* on, a green arc shows how far the head turns for free (45° either side of the facing): within 10° of its end it turns yellow and says what looking further costs (**TURN 1 TU**), or red with **NO TU** when the soldier couldn't pay for the turn. And when a shot or a throw at what's under the crosshair would make the game turn the soldier first (aimed outside the eighth they face), a small orange triangle on the rim shows the way they'll turn, and the line under the dial says what the turn adds to the shot (**SHOT +1 TU**; red if the time units won't cover both). With free turning, the soldier faces the target for free before a shot, so that never costs anything.
- **Compass: step costs** (NO): YES: round the compass, outside the dial and turning with it, the time units a step each way would cost right now, as the game will charge it (a walk, a sidestep with *Turning costs TU*, a run while sprinting): the number at the top is the step forward, those at the sides the steps to the side. No number where you can't step. Left of the climb arrows, what a level up and a level down cost (a lift, a ladder, flying). With *Turning costs TU*, the line under the dial always says what a turn costs. A sidestep's number is light blue. A step dearer than the same step over plain ground is yellow (sand, tall grass, rubble), red from 160% of it, and grey when the soldier can't pay for it. Needs *Compass*.

### Sound

- **Dynamic music** (NO): YES: while aliens are in sight the music switches to a tenser track (the base defence one), and goes back to the battle music once they've been out of sight for a few seconds. NO: the battle music all along (switching it off mid-battle goes straight back to it).
- **Hit, kill and swing sounds** (NO): a short tick when your shot or blow hits an enemy in sight, a duller one when it struck but did no harm, a double tick for a kill, and a swoosh for your melee swings: the sounds the 3D view adds. NO: silent (the crosshair still marks the hit), leaving the game's own sounds, such as death cries and a weapon's own hit sound, alone. It also gives the heartbeat you hear when badly hurt: under half health, for the soldier you control on your turn, once they have been hurt in this battle (health carried over from earlier fights, as the big mods keep it, doesn't start it).
- **Ambient sounds** (On): the rain, the wind and the fires near you, heard in the 3D view and made by the game itself (no sound files). In a battle the weather brings (the jungle's rain and a big mod's acid rain, the polar snow, the deserts' and Mars' blowing sand, as the sky has them) you hear rain with a little wind under it, or wind alone in snow and dust. The wind comes in slow gusts. Under a roof the sound is muffled: the rain drums on the roof above you, with a drip now and then, and the wind sinks to a low murmur. The sound eases between the two as you walk in and out. A roof on posts or a porch open on its sides still sounds like the open, and a doorway somewhere between. A fire within nine tiles (any burning tile your squad has seen, and any burning unit in sight) crackles and roars quietly from its side. It gets louder as you come closer and adds up when more tiles burn. From further off, or through a wall, it is duller and quieter. It all plays at your sound effects volume, well under the game's footsteps and shots. Where a terrain plays its own ambient sound (TFTD's coasts and sea floors, some big-mod towns), the rain and wind play at half to share the room with it. Back on the isometric map it fades away. Quiet: half as loud. Off: none.

### Cheats

- **Doom mode (debug)** (NO): a cheat for testing and fun, and a first try at real time: in the 3D view the selected soldier never runs out of time units or energy on your turn: every shot, throw, blow and item is free. Grenades go off in real time, a few seconds after they're primed, wherever they are then (see [Items](#3-the-top-bar-and-items) and the settings below). Ammo still counts. DOOM MODE shows on the info panel while it's on.
- **Doom IDKFA** (NO): Doom mode plus endless ammo: the selected soldier's weapons spend no rounds in the 3D view, one-round weapons like the rocket launcher included, and loaded clips are filled up. IDKFA shows on the info panel.
- **Doom IDDQD** (NO): Doom mode plus invincible soldiers: X-COM's units take no damage at all (shots, explosions, fire, smoke, falls) and keep full morale, on either side's turn and in either view. Their four stats on the info panel turn silver, and IDDQD shows there. (Mind control still works: it does no damage.)
- **Doom IDCLIP** (NO): Doom mode plus noclip. In 3D the soldier walks through walls, objects, closed doors and other solid things, out into the air, and with the up and down keys (Page Up, Page Down) straight up or down a level through ceilings and floors, anywhere; no one is in the way but other units, and the map's edge still holds (a soldier must stand on the map). Nothing is paid, and nothing happens on the way: no reaction fire, no mines. While it's on, X-COM's units never fall: one walked into the air stays there, and falls once IDCLIP goes off (its setting, or Insert). Whatever the eye is inside (a wall, an object, a floor) is left out of the picture, so the view looks out of it. A 2x2 unit keeps the rules. Soldiers still spot and are spotted as they go. IDCLIP shows on the info panel.
- **Doom prime on throw** (YES): in Doom mode, a grenade is armed as you throw it and burns from the moment it leaves your hand (FUSE in battle sets the seconds). NO: *prime* sets the seconds and pulls the pin, and the fuse burns in your hand.
- **Doom free grenades with airburst** (NO): in Doom mode, a grenade flies to wherever the crosshair points, by the 3D view's own throw instead of the game's: under a roof, over a wall, at a point in the air. It reaches as far as the soldier could throw it by the game's rules, but never further than a person can throw: about 40 m (25 tiles) for the strongest, less for weaker soldiers (the game's own throw reaches 21 tiles at strength 27 and 51 at 60). It flies a real arc and drops where it meets something, and it bursts **in the air** if its fuse runs out before it lands. NO: the game's throw (refused where the game can't throw), and a grenade whose fuse runs out in flight goes off the moment it lands.
- **Doom grenade fuse (seconds)** (3): in Doom mode, how long a grenade burns, from 1 to 5 seconds. The **SET FUSE** box in battle (the prime mode) changes it too, and it holds for every grenade until changed.
- Insert switches every cheat that's on off in battle, and the same ones back on (Doom mode alone if none was on); the warning line says which, and the settings keep it (as for Y, akimbo, and the SET FUSE box). The names are a tribute to Doom, whose cheat codes they borrow (see [Thanks](#12-thanks)).

### VR (experimental)

> **VR game design experimentation — not for gaming at the moment.**

The 3D view in stereo for a VR headset, with head tracking and controllers. The headset build is the Meta Quest 3's (built from source and installed with adb: [quest3/README.md](quest3/README.md)); these settings are the Quest build's alone: a desktop doesn't list them (VR mode stays Off there; OPENXCOM3D_ALL_SETTINGS=1 lists them, for the desktop's preview). The section is at the end of the 3D VIEW tab.

- **VR mode** (Off): **On** or **Preview**: the 3D view becomes two eye pictures side by side (on the headset, an immersive stereo view): the head only looks, the right controller holds the weapon, only turning (the right stick) turns the soldier, at the game's turning cost; the aliens' turn plays on the map. **Off**: nothing changes.
- **VR eye picture** (360x390): each eye's size, 360x390, 460x500 or 710x770; sharper is slower (the Quest build starts at 460x500).
- **VR weapon** (YES): the weapon in hand drawn in 3D in each eye. NO: none in VR.
- **VR turning** (Smooth): the right stick turns the soldier smoothly, as far as it's pushed; **Snap**: 45 degrees a push.
- **VR laser** (YES): a thin red laser from the weapon's muzzle to where it points, the crosshair at its end.
- **VR smoothing** (YES): the headset's graphics chip smooths each eye's picture, drawn three times bigger, its pixel steps turned into smooth edges. NO: the pixels as they are.
- **VR eyes kept on the card** (YES): VR on a headset, with *Renderer* GPU. Each eye's finished picture and its depth stay on the graphics chip as textures, and the headset's display takes them from there: nothing is read back and nothing sent up again (the last marks, the weapon in hand, its laser and the crosshair, which the CPU draws, go up as small pictures and the chip lays them on). **NO**: each eye is read back and uploaded, as the CPU's eyes are; so it is too where the two can't share textures. Checked on the desktop and in an Android emulator against the read-back eyes, the same frames, pixel for pixel.
- With *Renderer* GPU (section Screen) each eye's picture is the graphics card's too: its rays, units and effects, through the eye's own off-centre field of view, checked pixel for pixel against the CPU's eyes on the desktop preview. Not yet run on a headset.

### Screen

New in 1.14. The CPU renderer stays the default and the reference: these are options.

- **Renderer** (CPU): **CPU** draws the 3D world on the processor, as ever, on any machine. **GPU** has the graphics card cast the world's rays: the same walk through the voxels and the same shading, as a shader, checked pixel for pixel against the CPU's picture (see [Performance](#8-performance)). Units, items, smoke, effects and the HUD are drawn as before, on the card's picture. On Linux, Windows and macOS: it opens its own hidden OpenGL context (EGL on Linux, WGL on Windows, CGL on macOS), whatever the game's display mode. Played on Linux (Intel and NVIDIA cards), on an Apple silicon Mac (OpenGL 4.1 over Metal) and on Windows; under Wine no card is found, and the CPU draws. On a machine without a graphics card, with a software OpenGL, or if the card fails, the CPU draws by itself. On a Windows laptop with two graphics chips the game now asks for the fast one. The card is opened on the side the first time (its driver builds the shaders for a few seconds): the CPU draws until it is ready. Where a battle begins, the 3D view's first frame is drawn ahead, unseen (the game waits up to 8 seconds for a card still being opened): the driver finishes its programs and the map and the units' bodies are sent to the card then, not at the first click of the 3D button (the first time in a run of the game the screen says PREPARING THE 3D VIEW while it does); the log says how long it took (`the 3D view's first frame drawn ahead in ... ms`). The log (`openxcom.log`) names the card: `OpenXcom3D GPU: the ... 3D view's rays are cast on ...`. The first switch to GPU can freeze the game for a while (longest in the middle of a battle): the card prepares its programs once; the setting's description says so.
- **3D resolution** (HUD): how fine the 3D world is drawn. **HUD**: as fine as its HUD, and magnified with it on a big screen (640×360 on a 1920×1080 screen at *Battlescape scale* screen): a big screen costs what a small one does. **Battle**: at the game's own battlescape size, only the HUD magnified over it. **Display**: at the display's own resolution, whatever the *Battlescape scale*: with the battlescape at a half or a quarter of the display (so the isometric map and the menus stay big), the 3D world is still drawn pixel for pixel. The view then isn't drawn into the game's screen at all (so the game's *Display Filter*, xBRZ, HQx or a CRT shader, which works on that screen, leaves the 3D world as it is: for a filter over it, choose HUD): it is laid into the display when the frame is shown, with the 3D HUD over it at a scale of its own (see *HUD scale*), under the game's windows and the mouse pointer, which keep the battlescape's (with the game's own output, with its HQX and xBRZ scalers, and with its OpenGL output). Display is many times the rays of HUD: heavy on the CPU (try *Pixel size* 2), made for *Renderer* GPU.

  The three choices name what the 3D world's pixel count matches. *3D resolution* is only about the 3D world (the walls, the units, the sky); how big the buttons, bars and crosshair are is *HUD scale*'s business, below. A worked example, on a **1920×1080 (Full HD)** screen:

  | Choice | The 3D world is drawn at | On a 1920×1080 screen |
  |---|---|---|
  | **HUD** | the 3D HUD's own grid, then magnified with the HUD | 640×360, each pixel shown 3×3 (with *HUD scale* Auto, which is x3 at 1080 lines); 960×540 shown 2×2 with *HUD scale* x2; 1920×1080 with x1 |
  | **Battle** | the game's battlescape size, which OXCE's own *Battlescape scale* sets (Options, Video) | 1920×1080 with the battlescape at the display's full size; 960×540 at a half; 640×360 at a third: whatever the isometric map is drawn at |
  | **Display** | the screen's own pixels | always 1920×1080, whatever the *Battlescape scale* and the *HUD scale* |

  How to choose:

  - **HUD** is the cheapest, and the classic look: big square pixels, the same work on a large screen as on a small one. The only way to make it finer is a smaller *HUD scale*, which also makes the HUD smaller.
  - **Battle** follows the isometric map. If you play with the map pixel for pixel (battlescape at the display's size), the 3D world is as fine as the screen while the HUD stays big. If you keep the map magnified (a half, a third), the 3D world is exactly as coarse as the map.
  - **Display** is the sharpest and doesn't care about the rest: the map and the menus can stay big and the 3D world is still drawn pixel for pixel. On a 1920×1080 screen that is about two million rays a frame, nine times what HUD casts there: made for *Renderer* GPU. On the CPU it is slow; *Pixel size* 2 halves it each way.

  In short: HUD = "as blocky as the 3D HUD", Battle = "as fine as my isometric map", Display = "as fine as my monitor". None of them changes the size of anything on screen, only how finely the 3D world is drawn; *Supersampling* (on the card) then casts more rays than pixels on top of whichever you chose.
- **HUD scale** (Auto): how many times the 3D HUD (its buttons, bars, texts and crosshair) is magnified on a big screen. **Auto** makes it as big as fits: ×2 from 720 lines, ×3 from 1080. **x1** to **x4** choose it (never bigger than fits): x2 on a 1920×1080 screen gives a smaller HUD and more room. With *3D resolution* HUD the 3D world is drawn at the HUD's size, so a smaller scale also makes it finer. With *3D resolution* Display the HUD's scale counts the display's own pixels, not the battlescape's: with the battlescape at a quarter of an 1800×1169 display, Auto draws the 3D HUD ×3 on a 600×389 layout (it was four display pixels to one before, whatever this said), x2 on 900×584, x1 pixel for pixel. Over the isometric map the top bar keeps the battlescape's scale, like the map.
- **Render scale** (100%): *Renderer* GPU only. The card casts the 3D world's rays on a grid smaller than the picture and magnifies the result: **50%** is half as many each way, a quarter of the rays. The units, the effects, the HUD and its texts are drawn at the full resolution over it, so they stay sharp; only the world gets softer. In the palette's colours the world's pixels just get bigger (as with *Pixel size*, but in any ratio); with *True colour* the magnified picture is smoothed along surfaces and kept sharp at their edges. **Auto** picks the scale by itself, frame by frame (dynamic resolution): it steps down through 85, 75, 67, 60 and 50% while the 3D view takes more than four fifths of a frame's time at the game's frame-rate limit (Options → Video; 60 a second if there is none), and back up when there is room, waiting a few seconds before it tries finer again. Made for weak cards (a Steam Deck, integrated graphics): measured on an integrated Intel Arc at 1280×800, a frame took 14.0 ms at 100%, 10.9 ms at 75% and 7.9 ms at 50% (15.8 and 9.3 ms at 100% and 50% with *True colour* and *Smooth light*). About 5 ms of a frame there doesn't go with the rays (the units and effects, the picture's way back to the game), which is why half the rays isn't half the time. The small camera views are always cast in full. The CPU renderer has *Pixel size* for the same purpose.
- **Supersampling** (Off): *Renderer* GPU only. **x2** or **x4** casts two or four rays through each pixel instead of one and blends what they show: edges of walls, far fences and fine texture stop shimmering as you turn. A flat surface comes out exactly as without it (the same dither); where a pixel's rays differ, it takes the palette colour nearest their mix. The further rays are cast only where the first ones show an edge or texture (a pixel that differs from one of its eight neighbours, in colour or in depth): on a flat stretch, most of a picture at a high resolution, they would all show the same. Where a voxel covers several pixels (texture seen close up) only a strong line between two colours counts, not every voxel's edge. So it costs less than that many times the rays: with everything else on, at 1728×1080 on an RTX 4060, x4 draws in 16 ms indoors where every ray would take 28, and in 33 for 38 over a town's grass.
- **Ambient occlusion** (NO): *Renderer* GPU only. Corners, the foot of walls, the ceiling over tall racks and the gaps between things are up to four or five shades darker: the blocky world gets depth. Sixteen places round each point are looked at, near and further, the near ones smoothly between their voxels; not beyond 26 tiles' distance. About 1 ms at 1080p on an RTX 4060.
- **True colour** (NO): the 3D world in full colour, past the palette's 256. It needs *Renderer* GPU and *3D resolution* Display. The display itself must be in full colour, and the game sees to that: with these three set, the display is opened in full colour whatever the video settings (the game's plain output is 8-bit otherwise; before, True colour and everything that needs it did nothing there, without a word). The card hands over each pixel in full colour beside its palette colour; a pixel nothing was drawn over shows in full colour, and units, effects, smoke, the screen's washes (night vision is one) and the HUD stay the palette's. By itself it changes one thing: with *Supersampling* a pixel is the true mix of its rays. It is the ground for *Smooth light*. About 1.5 ms at 1080p on an RTX 4060.
- **Smooth colours** (NO): with *True colour*. Where the palette's picture has a dot pattern (the dither between two shades of light, into the haze far away, in the sky's bands and the clouds' edges, in soft shadows and ambient occlusion), the full-colour picture blends smoothly, in the art's own colours. The dots that swim over walls at high resolutions are gone. An animated part's two frames are mixed smoothly too, where the palette's picture shows the one or the other in a dot pattern.
- **Coloured lights** (NO): with *True colour*. A shot in flight (plasma, a laser's bolt, a rocket's flame, a burning arrow; not plain bullets), a thrown grenade that burns (a mod's Molotov cocktail), an explosion, a muzzle flash and the eight fires nearest you light the walls and the ground round them in their own colour, by the square of how near they are, and (with *Shadows*) only where they are in sight: a plasma bolt paints the corridor green as it passes, a fire flickers orange on the walls. Units and effects, drawn by the CPU, don't take it. The program on the card that has these lights in it is built when the battle starts (some seconds more of the CPU drawing, the first time), and used only for the frames that have a light: with its code merely present, everything else ran at half speed on one driver.
- **Reflections** (NO): with *True colour*. The sea mirrors the sky and its clouds, and what stands in it (one more ray off the water, tilted by the waves), more the flatter you look at it; the sea past the map's edge mirrors the sky. Not under the sea. Window glass mirrors what is in front of it (one more ray off the pane): faintly seen straight on, more the flatter you look along the pane. A frame with mirrors is drawn in two goes, the second over the mirroring pixels only: a street with a few windows costs about a quarter more than without the setting (it cost nearly three times as much when every pixel went through the mirrors' program). Still water mirrors too: a swamp, a pond, a paddy, a puddle, the shallows of a beach, where a mod's table names their terrain (`stillWater:` in the rules: a data set and the palette's colour groups that are water on its floors; XPiratez's and The X-Com Files' swamps, beach, rice farm, muddy mountain and nuclear pools are named). Only the water's own pixels mirror, in their own colour (a waste's swamp stays green), with ripples a third of the sea's; banks, reeds and lilies stay as drawn. The units stand in every mirror, yours and the enemy's that the squad sees, and you yourself in a pane (window glass mirrors with *Smooth light* on). A UFO's metal mirrors too with *Metallic surfaces* On. Off, nothing mirrors anywhere: this is the one switch for every mirror.
- **Liquid screens** (YES): with *Reflections*. The alien entertainment panels' screens (the yellow and the blue ones of X-COM's alien bases, and the same panels in XPiratez and The X-Com Files) are still water standing on end: they ripple, and mirror the room round them through the show (a player's idea, GitHub issue #261: "the panels in lore are supposed to make you feel weird looking at"). The panel's frame and pedestal stay as drawn, and so does its back. A mod names its panels in its rules (`liquidScreens:`: a data set and its records; the screen is the pixels its animation changes, less the colours of its still frame). NO: the panels as drawn.
- **Light shafts** (NO): with *True colour*. Light shows in the air it crosses: the flashlight's cone, the flares, the lamps by night and the coloured lights are beams and glows where there is smoke (the smoke the game draws is lit by them), and everywhere under the sea. The light is added up at twenty places along each ray, by how much smoke lies there (blended between tiles). Walls don't hide a light from the air: a lamp behind a wall still glows in the smoke on this side. Costs nothing in a frame without smoke above the sea.
- **Surface relief** (Off): *Renderer* GPU only; **Subtle** or **Strong**. Walls, floors and hulls show a little relief, read from their own drawings: where a drawing gets brighter the surface is taken to rise, where it gets darker to sink, and each face is tilted that way towards or away from the lights: the sun's side by day, the lamps, the flashlight, the flares, the coloured lights. Hay bales stand out as blocks, a door's panels are bevelled, bricks and floor tiles have edges, and asphalt under a street lamp at night is rough stone. No pictures are stored: the slope comes from a voxel's four neighbours on its face as it is drawn, and goes smoothly across the face. It fades out between a dozen and twenty tiles away, and wherever a cube is too small in the picture to show it (far ground looked along), where it would only shimmer. Subtle suits every terrain; Strong shows more, and makes walls drawn as smooth ramps of shades (inside a UFO, in a base) look rippled. Undersides and ceilings stay flat, units and items are not touched, and a door has none while it swings. Works in the palette's picture and in *True colour*. On an RTX 4060 laptop at 1280×800 it costs next to nothing (0 to 5% of the card's time).
- **Metallic surfaces** (Off): *Renderer* GPU only; **Off** or **On**. A UFO's hull and its inner walls are metal. The sun glints on a face turned halfway between it and you, and the glint moves as you move; a hull looked along its face shows the sky's light; and whatever lights it from near you (the flashlight, a flare) gleams where the face is turned to you. The metal is speckled: one voxel in five is a flake turned a little its own way, which flashes where the sun or your light catches it and goes out as you move on. A highlight is a colour a few shades brighter than its drawing has it (three and a half at most, and never more than halfway to its colour group's brightest), so the hull keeps its colours and what is drawn bright already doesn't go white (in the palette's picture the steps are dithered; with *Smooth light* they blend). With *Surface relief* on, the glint slides over the panels and seams the drawing shows. With *Reflections* (and *True colour*) the metal also shows what its face mirrors, the sky or what stands before it, the units too, tinted by its own colour: faintly head on, up to a third when looked along; *Reflections* off, it only glints and sparkles. One more ray for each pixel of a hull, so it costs some performance near a UFO. The isometric map's UFOs stay as they are; so does the CPU renderer's picture. (Until the change below it was Off, Sheen or Mirror; a profile with either is On.)

### Experimental

The last section under Advanced.

- **Voxel units** (**experimental**; NO). Experimental means: play with it and tell us what looks wrong (a unit's shape, its colours, its pose, the weapon in its hands): [open an issue](https://github.com/b0realis/OpenXcom3D/issues) with a screenshot and, if you can, the save. YES: units are 3D bodies of little cubes made from their own sprites instead of pictures standing in the world: the soldiers, aliens and civilians of both games, one tile big and two by two. Every unit's body is made from the battle's start (or from the moment the setting is switched on in a battle), on the map too, so none is left to make when the 3D view opens: from its frames for the eight directions as the game draws them (its armour, colours and items), on threads of their own, several at once; a whole battle's take a few seconds, and a unit whose body isn't ready yet, or that changes its pose or what it holds, shows as its picture for that moment. It is one body, the same from every side and fixed in the world as you walk round it: the shape its eight drawings agree on, each cube coloured from its front drawing or its back one, whichever side of the body it lies on (before, the body was matched to each direction's drawing and changed as you crossed from one side to the next). A turret (a unit two tiles by two that can't move, mounted on a vehicle: XPiratez's truck and tank turrets) is a plain block set on its vehicle: its outline as seen from above, as thick as its drawings show it, its upper edge cut at a slant, with its barrels lying on it; each of its cubes takes its colour from the drawing that sees it best, of all eight. Rough, but a turret from every side. Tanks, hover tanks and the Cyberdisc are made as every big unit is, from what their eight drawings agree on. A fall shows each of its shapes (one for each death frame, then lying) for about a sixth of a second, so it reads as a fall and not as standing, then a pile. The Celatid's body hovers up and down and swells a little, as its picture does. With *See-through corpses* a body lying on the floor (a heap, a big unit's wreck) is see-through whenever it lies in the way of the aim: the line through the crosshair passes over it and ends beyond it, whether the crosshair is on it or on the enemy behind it; and it shows fainter than a picture does (six tenths of the *Ghost opacity*). A unit struck down keeps its body until it falls (it showed as its flat picture for a moment when the hit burnt its frames). It holds its items, walks as its walk frames show, goes down as its death frames show (each frame's own picture given volume: it collapses as its sprite does) and lies as its corpse's inventory picture, laid on the floor with volume. A unit already down when the battle is loaded lies so too. A big unit (two tiles by two) keeps its body standing while the game takes it down, and its wreck then lies there (the same body set down on the ground, as much lower as the map's wreck picture stands lower than the unit did, and painted with that picture's dents and holes), while its four parts lie in their places (parts carried apart are their pictures). What the game draws over a unit's picture is particles on a body (with *Particles* on; else the game's flames drawn over it): a burning unit is in tongues of flame all over, sparks flying and thick smoke; its breath under the sea is bubbles from its head; a hovering big unit (a hover tank, a Cyberdisc) rains sparks under its hull and raises dust. It stands as tall as its picture (a Sectoid smaller than a soldier, a Muton taller). With *Realistic hitboxes* a shot hits the body itself, the same shape from every side (a unit going down is hit by the shape its fall has come to; a speck left out of the drawing is not hit); without it, what shots hit stays the game's. NO: the game's pictures, as before. The cubes fill one grid and pack side by side: from no side does one see between them. With *Renderer* CPU a body's cubes are put in their rows' order in one pass, those that turn no bare face to the eye are left out, and a body so far away that a cube is under a pixel is drawn in blocks of 2 or 4 cells: the same picture for less than half the time (GitHub issue #182). With *Renderer* GPU the graphics card draws the bodies' cubes itself (each body's are sent to it once), so the frame rate stays about what it is with the pictures. On a desktop the bodies are made fine (cubes a quarter of a voxel across, from the frames enlarged with their edges kept), on a headset coarser. **What a shot hits is not changed by this setting**: with *Realistic hitboxes* NO it is the original game's hit shape, which resembles the body but is not it; with YES it is the whole body as drawn, the item in its hands too.

- **Live command feed** (**experimental**; NO). YES: the battle becomes a command post's screens, as a tactical unit's helmet cameras stream to its command (the owner's idea). The top bar stays as in the 3D view, with the squad's buttons and their cards (a soldier's helmet camera on hovering one). Under it at the left the selected soldier's inventory, the game's own as its screen draws it (the soldier's figure, the slots, the ground), scaled into the window and worked there as on its screen: an item picked up and put down costs its time units (the title shows the soldier's), its arrows go to the soldier before and the next, the gun button unloads, the arrow by the ground scrolls it; the window's button opens the inventory's screen. At the right the selected soldier's 3D view, in its window: a click in it gives it the mouse and the soldier is played there as in the full 3D view (looking round, walking, firing; the crosshair with the shot's odds and cost in the window's middle); the mouse mode's key or Escape lets go of the mouse. The 3D view is drawn at the window's size, so the whole feed costs less than the full 3D view. Below at the left the drone's feed: the isometric map itself, with all its clicks, scrolling and the game's panel under it, in black and white with scan lines, snow and now and then a tear, following the soldier looked through; its buttons NV (night vision's green), ID (each soldier's number and name over them; a name that would cover another's is left out) and BOX (a box's corners round each soldier; the selected one's in yellow). At the right the tactical map with all its buttons, keeping a zoom of its own and following the selected soldier. Drone, tactical map and inventory go on as the soldier moves and fires in the 3D window. The drone's and the tactical map's windows can be shown all over the screen (Escape or the same button puts the drone back); the 3D window's button, the 3D key or the top bar's 3D button go to the full 3D view, and there FEED (or the key) comes back. On a small screen (the original 320x200) there is no room for the tactical map, and the drone's window is the whole width. NO: the isometric map and the 3D view as ever. Not in VR mode.

### Fixed, off the tab

After 1.15 the owner went over every setting (2026-11-24): these no longer have a row on the 3D VIEW tab. Each keeps its code and its line in the profile and is always as given here (a profile with another value is given it). Run the game with the environment variable OPENXCOM3D_ALL_SETTINGS=1 to list every setting again and change these too. The game's own *Frame rate limit* and frame counter are no longer listed either: they are on the game's General tab and on F7. And these are now part of another's row: *Tactical map from the isometric view* (*Tactical map* Both views), *Alien turn: look as the soldier faces* (*Alien turn* Facing), *Flight cost overrides rules* (*Flight cost per level*), *Expanded crosshair info* and *Shot damage* (*Shot info*), *Soft sun shadows* and *Shadows from every light* (*Shadows*), *See-through corpses* (*See-through* All).

- **Tactical map: wheel changes level** (always YES): YES: on the tactical map the mouse wheel goes a level up or down and zooms with Ctrl; the level's and the zoom's tips say so. NO: the wheel zooms about the tile under the pointer and changes the level with Ctrl. (A player, GitHub issue #215; off by default: the owner had the wheel zoom.)
- **Camera when a soldier falls** (always YES): with *Aliens' turn cameras*, a soldier of yours going down out of view (killed or knocked out) pops up their helmet camera card for 3 seconds: static, with DECEASED or UNCONSCIOUS. NO: only the soldier you look through gets the death camera, as before.
- **Eye height lowered by** (always 3): X-COM soldiers fill a level almost to the ceiling, so the 3D camera sits this many voxels lower (about 8 cm each), from 0 to 6, and rooms feel less cramped. 0 is the game's own eye height. What the soldier can see by the game's rules doesn't change. Standing on something tall right under a ceiling (a stack of crates, stairs under a floor), the game pushes the soldier's sight down under the floor above; there the camera sits only 1 voxel under the game's eye, so the room stays in view instead of a slit over what they stand on.
- **Right click opens doors** (always YES): a right click on a closed door the crosshair points at, within the soldier's reach, opens it without walking in, as a right click on a door does on the isometric map (the door's time units; a turn to it by the turning rules first). With no such door under the crosshair the right button does what it did: it forgets a marked target, cancels a picked action, or zooms while held. NO: never a door.
- **Check the landing** (always YES): with *Falling*, a step off a ledge first looks where the soldier lands. Onto ground no one can walk on (a blood moat, the sea, the craft's hull: places the map never lets a soldier walk onto) or where not a single step leads on, the soldier stops at the edge with **UNSAFE LANDING! PUSH AGAIN** (a player's report: X-Com Files' vampire castle, a soldier soft-locked in its blood moat). Let go of the movement key and push that way again within 6 seconds, or jump (Space) while pushing, to go over anyway. NO: every ledge can be stepped off, as before.
- **Melee hits floors** (always YES): with *Melee hits walls and downed* on (or the mod's terrain melee), a blow at the floor or ceiling under the crosshair within reach strikes it too: the floor at your feet or around, the ceiling above. The game's own hit: its damage, the floor's armour, and if it breaks, the game's falling (a body lying there takes the blow first, as with a shot at the floor). NO: walls, doors and objects only, as the game's terrain melee.
- **Force arcing shots** (always YES): an arcing shot the game would refuse (none of its own curves lands on the tile you aim at, as at the side of a building) fires anyway, as with Ctrl held (OXCE's force fire): at that tile (at the exact point with *Aim at exact point*, where the game takes the tile's corner), whatever the curve meets on the way, and its path shows yellow ("at your own risk"). Where the game's own aim works (a unit, an object, a wall, the floor), it's kept. NO: the game refuses such a shot, as on the isometric map without Ctrl. (Aimed at the open sky, an arcing shot or a throw goes through the point of air on the crosshair's line and on to where it comes down, whatever this setting: a shot forced through that point, a throw at the tile it comes down on; the nearest point on the line whose arc stays in the map, and for a throw within the soldier's reach. None: refused, "No trajectory".)
- **Shotgun pellets in flight** (always YES): YES: a shotgun's pellets fly beside its round, in 3D and on the isometric map alike, each to where the game's spread sends it (traced as it's fired, the same targets and spread), all drawn as the round is (on the map with the round's bullet sprite; shotgun ammo that has none, as some big mods' shotguns, borrows the one its damage type's ammo uses most).
