With *Each pellet on its own* each hits as it gets there, else as the round lands. NO: the game's own way: the whole blast lands the moment it's fired. Either way every pellet lands with its hit sound (a few to a volley) and, in 3D, its sparks and bullet hole.
- **Each pellet on its own** (always YES): YES: a shotgun's pellets, and the two rounds of an akimbo volley, each land on their own, in either view and whoever fires. In OXCE the game traces only the round (the lead pellet): the others are traced and hit as it lands, and when it flies off the map (over a target into the sky, past one at the map's edge) none is traced at all, every pellet lost with it (a player's report: the "shotgun void"). Now none is lost, and with pellets in flight each hits as it arrives; the deaths come with the last to land in the map, as the game's come with its round. Misses still flying then fly on to the map's edge, only to be seen; nothing waits for them. NO: the game's own way, the lead pellet deciding for the rest.
- **Smooth fire** (always YES): YES: a fire's flames (the game's own on a burning tile, and a mod's campfires and fireplaces) flow from frame to frame with their colours mixed, no speckle. NO: the frames dissolve into each other dot by dot, as before 1.14.
- **Hit flash** (always YES): YES: a unit hit flashes for a moment as it flinches, standing or already down (a knocked-out unit's body on the floor flashes too, and sprays blood from there); a hit counts whether it took health or only stun. NO: hit units only flinch. With *Muzzle flashes* off there's no hit flash either, whatever this says.
- **Wall paint** (always All rooms, since GitHub issue #264; the owner: "lets have All rooms always and thats it"): the isometric map shows each wall from one side only, the south-east, with the rooms' roofs cut away, so a house's west and north outer walls are drawn in the paint of its rooms and its east and south ones in its outside paint; in 3D you see both faces of every wall. The face the map never shows takes the paint of the side it faces, as a building's own walls show it: from behind a house its outside paint (the city's brick house is brick all round, not the beige of its rooms; TFTD's island houses white siding, not pink plaster), inside a room the room's paint on the inside of its front walls, and on a wall between two rooms each face the paint of the room it looks into. The faces the map shows stay exactly as it draws them. Doors and their frames keep their own; a window keeps its frame, glass and curtains, and only the wall round it takes the paint; fences, crafts and UFOs stay as they were. A display, a wall whose picture animates (an alien base's entertainment panels, a computer's screen), lends its paint to no other wall, and its back takes the paint of the nearest plain wall made of the display's own frame (in an alien base the base's grey wall, not the cave's rock). Worked out when the battle starts (a millisecond); a wall blown away later leaves its rubble as the map draws it. Its row (with OPENXCOM3D_ALL_SETTINGS=1) has the two other choices: **Each side**, only a building's outer walls take the paint of the side they face, the walls between rooms keep the art on both faces; **As the art**, both faces of every wall in the paint of the face the map shows, as before GitHub issue #111.
- **Lift beams** (always YES): every lift (a building's elevator, an alien base's: a grav lift's floor with another over or under it, as the game floats units only between two such; one on its own is a floor like any other, GitHub issue #265) shows a beam of light rising through it, in its pad's colours; none with *Particles* Off, and a UFO's lift has its own with *UFO lights and aura*.
- **Ghost opacity** (always 50%): how solid the parts without shape look up close, from 10% (a faint dotting) to 100% (solid); far off, 60% of that. 50% is as they always were.
- **Craft icons** (always YES): on the soldier cards, before the name, each soldier's little figure from the craft screen: the one the mod draws for the armor worn (a tank's for its tank), as X-Com Files and XPiratez draw one for every armor, so you tell your agents apart at a glance. It's the armor's, so it shows in a base defence too. The original games give every soldier the same generic icon, which tells no one apart: none there. With the icon, a long weapon line drops its spare clips to keep the gun's name.
- **Facing icons** (always YES): with the *Show facing* key held (Left Alt), an arrow on each unit in sight as well as the ring round its feet, in its side's colour, at a fixed size on the screen so it reads from any height and distance (from a soldier's eye a ring far off is a line). It shows the unit as seen from above with you at the bottom: up, it faces away from you; down, towards you; left and right as you see them; one of eight ways. NO: the ring alone, as before.
- **Middle click identifies** (always YES): YES: the middle button works as on the isometric map: a click on a unit you can see (its hitbox or its picture under the crosshair, or the free cursor) opens the game's own unit screen, who it is and what it carries; anywhere else it switches between mouse look and the free cursor, as always. NO: the middle button only switches mouse look and the free cursor.

## 5. Fire modes per weapon (FPS-style shooting)

With **Options → 3D VIEW → FPS-style shooting (easier)** on, the 3D view keeps the game's three fire-mode slots (keys Z and X for snap and aimed, the mouse wheel or J to cycle; the auto shot's key is yours to bind) but fires them FPS style. Each gun offers only the modes it has in the game; the table is generated from the game's rulesets (`items.rul`). Time units are the game's, as a percentage of the soldier's time units.

- **SNAP (Z)**: the game's single snap shot, from the hip (a wider spread than aimed)
- **AIMED (X)**: one tight round
- **AUTO** (no key of its own by default: the wheel or the next fire mode key; V before): a click fires a burst; **holding the trigger keeps firing** until you let go, the clip is empty or the time units run out
- **sweeping a burst**: each round goes where the crosshair is as it fires, so dragging the mouse during a burst sweeps it along the drag. The game's two-point spray (Ctrl+Shift and two clicks, OXCE's) stays on the isometric map (F8 there and back)
- guided launchers (Blaster Launcher, Disruptor Pulse Launcher, ...) place their waypoints on the isometric map, as before, as many as the launcher's rules give (the fire mode counts them in 3D: WAYPOINTS 3/9)

With akimbo on (Full or One-hand), the other hand's gun fires too, the two taking turns round by round (or as a volley, *Akimbo volley*): the same mode if it has it, otherwise its first shot, its whole burst (a pistol joins a rifle's auto with a snap, after the rifle's first round). There's no AIMED with a gun in each hand (Y switches akimbo off to aim), and with an arcing gun the two fire in turn (see *Full akimbo*).

## 6. Rules for mods

A mod can tune the 3D view in the `firstPerson:` section of its ruleset (the defaults are in `mod/OpenXcom3D/OpenXcom3D.rul`).

### Built-in items in the hands

The section can list built-in items (fixed weapons, armors' special weapons) that should show in the hands anyway, where a mod's picture of one looks right held (by default they don't show, see [Items](#3-the-top-bar-and-items)):

```yaml
firstPerson:
  builtInItemsShown:
    - STR_MECH_ARM_CANNON         # a mod's built-in weapon drawn like a held gun
```

### Bad signal by mission

The helmet cameras (the soldier card's, the soldier cam, the map camera) glitch every few seconds on any mission, now and then tearing (bands of the picture slide sideways, a bright seam along their edge), and the soldier cam and the map camera get a spell of TV static every 10 seconds to 2 minutes. On the hardest ones, on the aliens' own ground, and on any mission while the soldier you watch is inside a UFO, they glitch a lot: a glitch every second or so, longer, in more and bigger patches, torn more often, with a little static all the time and longer, stronger spells every 5 to 40 seconds. Our mod lists those missions (alien deployment types) under `badSignal:` with level 2: Mars (both stages), an alien base, a base defence, and in TFTD an alien colony, T'leth and an artefact site. Level 1 is the usual, 0 none. A mod can list its own:

```yaml
firstPerson:
  badSignal:
    - missions: [STR_MY_ALIEN_FORTRESS]
      level: 2
```

With *Camera artifacts* Light the bad signal is gentler on every mission (half the low static, the spells at three tenths of their strength, no glitch over 0.15 seconds); with None the cameras' picture is clean.

### Underground maps

Past a map's edge the 3D view draws the world going on: the ground, the X-COM tape, the sky (under the sea, the water). Underground it draws rock instead, worked out from the map itself: a level whose outer ring of tiles is (nearly) all rock (walls and objects, or floors with something over them, like a base's sewers) is underground. So a map that starts on the surface and goes on underground has the sky over its surface and rock round its underground levels, and the ground beyond the edge lies at its surface's height; a map underground all the way up (every base, an alien colony's inside, Cydonia's complex, the XPiratez hideout) has rock round it and over it, in the colour of its own rock: the commonest colour of the walls round its edge, unless that colour is too dark to show (TFTD's alien city, T'leth, and the artifact site's second stage are walled round in the deep sea's black), when the next commonest is taken. Under rock no sun shines, so a ceiling there isn't shaded as turned away from it; and a dark rock ceiling (the hideout's) shows its texture, a few shades short of its darkest, as the rock beyond the edge does, where one plain colour would sink into near-black (a lighter one, like an X-COM base's grey, stays plain). A mission the view gets wrong can be listed as underground all the way up:

```yaml
firstPerson:
  undergroundMissions: [STR_MY_BUNKER]
```

Never list a map that starts on the surface, nor a mission name two games share for different maps (TFTD's seabed colony is `STR_ALIEN_BASE_ASSAULT`, as UFO's alien base is). Ours lists XPiratez's Thule base ("the sprawling complex under the surface of the Moon", its top open on the map), its Old Earth metro and sewers (their roof is rock the art leaves invisible, so the stars showed through it), the ninja bases' underground stages, and in the Shadowlands the sewers under the City of the Dead, the Rabbit Crypt and the Realm of Fear's dungeons; and X-Com Files' hideouts in the city sewers (the Cyberweb lairs, the smugglers' refuge, the ooze nest). Every other map tried was told right by itself.

### Water

Past the map's edge the ground goes on as the commonest floor of the edge. The floors of the terrain data sets listed as water are the sea instead: where it reaches the edge, the sea goes on past it, and its top is drawn in waves (its colours taken from its own art). A data set's name makes all its floors water; a data set with records, only those. Ours lists TFTD's sea round islands, ports and ships, the big mods' copy of it (XPiratez's and X-Com Files' islands, ports, liners and cargo ships), and the sea round TFTD's liner and cargo ship that is one of their own parts (drawn as a dark floor, so its waves take TFTD's sea colours). Where the sea lies a level or more above the floors at the map's edge (round a ship's lower decks), the world past the edge is at the sea's height:

```yaml
firstPerson:
  water: [SEA, SEAURBAN, { dataSet: LINERB, records: [1] }]
```

A list replaces the one before it (a mod adding its own water lists ours too).

### Things that turn

A tile part has one shape in the game's data for all eight frames of its animation, and the 3D view builds each frame from that shape, coloured by the frame's art. A thing whose art changes shape (a collar rising round a rod) only changed its paint. A part listed under `turned` is, from the height `from` up (voxels, 0 to 23; the game's shape stands below it), turned from each frame's art as on a lathe: round its own upright axis, as wide at each height as that frame's art is there. The art shows a round top as an ellipse, so a height is given a disc of its own only where the art is wider than the far rims of the discs below it would be: a flat top stays flat, and what stands on it starts there. It is for upright round things in motion (pedestals, pistons, columns), named one by one by data set and record: a single drawing can't tell every shape, and any other part keeps the game's.

```yaml
firstPerson:
  turned:
    - { dataSet: ORGANIC2, records: [3], from: 4 }
```

Ours lists TFTD's colony pedestal (a drum, a collar and a rod rising and sinking over a slab) and the same one in XPiratez's and The X-Com Files' sets (T'leth, the Deep Ones' halls). A named part plays its frames with *UFO lights* on too. A list replaces the one before it.

### Skies and weather

Over the Earth the 3D view draws the day's sky (the sun, drifting clouds) or the night's (the stars, the moon). Where it rains or snows, clouds cover it (a player, GitHub issue #94): a grey deck of them drifting overhead, paler over snow, darker at night; no sun, moon or stars, no sun shadows, and the walls lit evenly from the sky. Rain, snow and blowing sand (dust) come by the battle's environment (its `enviroEffects`, as the big mods name them), or else by terrain data set. Ours gives the polar maps snow, the jungle rain, the deserts and Mars dust, and XPiratez's acid rain and dust storm their rain and dust:

```yaml
firstPerson:
  weather:
    - dataSet: POLAR
      type: snow
    - enviroEffects: [STR_ENVIRO_ACID_RAIN]
      type: rain
```

A battle somewhere else has its own sky, by its mission (alien deployment) type, or else a terrain its map is built of, or else its environment (which many missions share):

- **space**: black, the stars all round, nothing past the map's edge (no ground, no rock, no tape): a ship in deep space;
- **orbit**: the same, with the Earth filling the view below, its clouds drifting by: a station in orbit;
- **moon**: black, the stars and the Earth hanging in the sky, over the Moon's ground;
- **mars**: a dusty butterscotch sky by day with a smaller sun, the stars by night, no Moon;
- **sea**: under the sea, as TFTD's battles under water (the murk, bubbles, marine snow, the light rippling on the floor), for a sea floor the game keeps dry;
- **void**: a dark violet sky with slow wisps, no sun, moon or stars;
- **earth**: the Earth's own.

Out of the air (space, orbit, the Moon) there's no haze, as with *Distance haze* Clear, and no dust floating in the open. Ours sets Cydonia's surface to Mars, in XPiratez and X-Com Files their Mars, Moon, orbital stations, space freighter and sea floor, and their other worlds and dreams (XPiratez's Shadowlands, X-Com Files' dreamscape and the land beyond the Doom portal) to the void. An environment that recolours the map (XPiratez's heat, cold and acid, X-Com Files' red sun) recolours the sky the same way. A mod can add its own; a later entry overrides:

```yaml
firstPerson:
  skies:
    - sky: moon
      enviroEffects: [STR_MY_MOON]
      missions: [STR_MY_LUNAR_BASE]
      terrains: [MY_MOON]
```

### The item table

How an item looks in 3D is worked out from its rules and its inventory picture, and the item table (`items:`) can say otherwise for any item, by type. It holds two things so far (more may follow: it's meant to tune the 3D shapes item by item):

- `held:` **plate** (a flat piece one voxel thick) or **cushion** (rounded, thickest in the middle), how the picture is given volume in the hand and when thrown. Worked out: a melee weapon that isn't blunt (its damage isn't concussive or stun, the clubs' and stun rods') and whose picture is thin everywhere is a plate (swords, knives, axes); everything else a cushion.
- `shot:` **object** (flies as an object, with no powder: no muzzle flash, barely a kick, and a white streak unless it carries fire, acid or a shock, which keep their own look) or **powder** (a gun's round, with its flash). Worked out: weapons aimed with the throwing skill and not the firing skill (the big mods' bows, blowpipes, javelins) fire objects, and so do guns firing an arrow picture drawn mostly for those (the big mods' crossbows); every other gun fires powder.
- `bow:` **true** or **false**: whether the weapon is held as a bow (upright, its curve towards the crosshair, the arrow leaving from the curve's middle) or as a gun. Worked out: a ranged weapon with BOW in its type that isn't a crossbow (CROSSBOW, XBOW).
- `underside:` **left**, **right**, **top** or **bottom**: the side of the weapon's inventory picture, as drawn, that is its underside in the hand. Worked out as the side standing out further from the picture's middle line (the grip, the magazine); name it for a gun that gets it wrong (The X-Com Files' P90: `right`).

Our mod's table has the XPiratez items the automatic rules get wrong (its wide blades, its gloves and claws, its harpoon guns and crossbows). A mod can add its own; a later entry overrides what it sets and leaves the rest:

```yaml
firstPerson:
  items:
    - types: [STR_MY_GREATSWORD, STR_MY_CLEAVER]
      held: plate
    - types: [STR_MY_SPEARGUN]
      shot: object
```

### Units by name

The voxel bodies (setting *Voxel units*) are made from a unit's sprites by general rules, the same for every unit. What is done for one kind of unit only (a ghost, a dog) is done for the units the unit table (`units:`) names, and for no others: a unit the table doesn't name is made by the general rules, as ever, so a rule added for one kind can't spoil another that was good. The table lists words to look for in a unit's names (its type, its armor's type, the armor's sprite sheet, its race, a soldier's type) and what it says of the units that have one:

- `seeThrough:` how much of the body is left out, in hundredths: **50** draws every other cube, a ghost that is there but seen through. (A unit the game itself draws as a dither, such as XPiratez's stealth and spectre suits, is see-through without being named.)
- `shape:` **beast** (a long body on four legs, a dog or a cat: made from its side drawings, its four legs apart, walking at a trot), **relief** (the unit's one drawing given volume: for a unit drawn the same from every side; the mod finds the lopsided ones by itself, see below) or **upright** (carved from its eight drawings, as ever: to take a unit out of an earlier entry, or out of what the mod finds by itself). Only a unit drawn on one frame, one tile big, whose side drawing is at least 1.2 times as long as its frontal drawing is wide is made so: a werewolf drawn in parts stays upright whatever it is called. **turret**: a unit one tile big that cannot move, a gun on a mount, is a plain block standing on the floor with its gun as a barrel, as the turrets two tiles by two are without being named (see `barrels:`); its wreck is that block, lower.

- `torsoDepth:` the most a torso is deep below the head, in hundredths of its width (**80**), trimmed at the back: its front stays where the side drawing has it; 0 for no limit. For units whose side drawings have a torso deeper than wide, most of it behind the body's middle (XPiratez's gals: hair, a back plate, what lies under the arm).
- `waist:` **drawn** (the rows where the trunk meets the legs are as the drawings have them: a waist that draws in under the chest stays so) or **filled** (as ever: filled out to the trunk's fullest row near its foot, for bodies whose parts end in a point and would pinch there).
- `top:` **back** (what faces up is painted by the back drawing, so the top of a head is hair) or **slant** (as ever: along the slant of the front or back drawing). For units whose faces are drawn high on the head.
- `sculpt:` **frontal** (nothing of the body stands outside what its front drawing or its back drawing shows, all its parts as the game lays them: the gap between an arm and the waist, the legs apart below the hips, as drawn) or **all** (as ever: by all eight drawings, a little forgiving of each). For human bodies, which the side drawings alone leave heavier than they are drawn. **sides**, for a big walking creature (two tiles by two): its shape is its four drawings' from front, back and sides alone, rounded between them, each cube painted by the drawing it is turned to (the table names frogs and toads so).
- `animation:` **none** (one body, whatever its sprite does), **pulse** (one body that rises, sinks and swells in a slow beat: a jellyfish) or **sprite** (as ever; a unit two tiles by two has it only when named so, and never one with a turret). Any unit drawn on one frame, one tile big, with nothing showing in its hands, whose standing sprite changes outline with the battle's animation and comes round after 8 frames, has a body for each of the 8, played as the sprite is: a bat flaps as it hovers, a Celatid and a Silacoid pulse. This needs no entry; `none` switches it off for the units named.
- `arms:` **outside** (the arms lie outside the body: for a unit in a robe, whose body is carved as wide as the robe, with the arms its drawings lay over the robe inside it), **along** (an arm posed on what it holds is coloured along its length, shoulder to hand, as its longest drawing has it: the sleeve ends where it does on the arm), **both**, or **drawn** (as ever). Or **own**: an arm is painted by its own drawing alone, never by the body drawn over it (hands on a shirt); the table names The X-Com Files' human race so (`HUMAN`: its civilians of every trade, gangers, hunters). Or **posed**: an arm that has bones is where its bones are, shoulder to elbow to the hand on what it holds, and of what its drawings make of it only what lies close to the bones stays (2 voxels at the shoulder, a quarter at the hand): no second forearm beside the one that holds. Or **elbow** (with posed): an arm that holds its item up, its drawing ending 2 voxels or more below its hand, has its elbow where the drawing ends: the upper arm down, the forearm up to the item. Or **reach** (with posed): an arm whose hand holds its item 2 voxels or more under its shoulder hangs straight down from the shoulder to the hand's height and reaches from there to the item, each bone as long as that makes it: for units drawn with long arms, whose arm on a pistol at the waist is drawn bent short. Several words may be given: `arms: own posed elbow`.
- `head:` **face** (only what of the head is turned to the front, within 53 degrees, is painted by the front drawing; its sides, back and top by the back drawing: hair, a helmet. For a body drawn in parts the head is the torso above where the arms begin) or **drawn** (as ever).
- `trunk:` **round** (a long body, `shape: beast`, is as broad as it is tall whatever its frontal drawings show: for a creature drawn from the front with its legs out beside its trunk. What hangs under its belly without reaching the ground, a muzzle, a tail's tip, is of its head or tail, not a pair of legs, and nothing of it stands apart as ears: one big round head) or **drawn** (as ever: as broad as its frontal drawings).
- `humanoid:` **standard** (a person: the unit's body is cut by its front, back and side drawings alone, holds no item if it is drawn on one frame, and is made again as a humanoid is built: an egg of a head on a neck, sloping shoulders, a waist, round arms and legs, feet that point ahead; sizes and pose stay its drawings'; only a unit that walks on two legs) or **drawn** (as ever: as its drawings alone leave it). The table names X-COM's soldiers and the games' civilians.
- `flat:` and `flatUnder:` how much of what stands above the body's widest level, and of what hangs under it, is kept, in hundredths, rounded off; 100 as ever. A body whose eight drawings are all made from the same height above it comes out of the cut with a cone on its top and one under it, each a third of its width high: `flat: 35` makes the cone on a saucer a low dome. A level takes the place of one further out, only ever down towards the widest level: nothing stands outside what the drawings show. (The Cyberdisc: 50 and 60; TFTD's Hallucinoid: 70; XPiratez's Academy drone: 60 and 50.)
- `tail:` **drawn** (the legs' frames draw a tail too: of the side drawing's stretches in a row the widest is the legs', the others the tail's, behind the body's middle) or **none** (as ever: every stretch is a leg's). For XPiratez's cat girls.
- `paint:` **hull** (a body drawn on one frame is painted as a vehicle's hull is, each cube by the drawing that looks onto it most squarely: a small saucer's rim keeps what is drawn low on its front and back) or **figure** (as ever: by its front and back drawings).
- `legs:` how many thin legs stand round the body, all told (8: a spider); 0 as ever. The body is made from the thick part of its drawings (what a square two pixels a side fits into), and the legs are made anew: spread evenly from ahead round to behind, reaching as far to the side, ahead and behind as the front and side drawings show, the knee as high and as far out, each two round bars a pixel thick, hip to knee to foot, coloured as the front drawing's legs. Only a unit drawn on one frame.
- `barrels:` how many barrels a turret's or a tank's gun is made with: 0 none (the turret as its eight drawings leave it, for a unit whose thin end is no gun), 1 to 4 that many, spread over the gun's width; a turret whose drawings show no gun to read gets barrels of a plain size on its front when given a number. Not said, as the drawings show: one, or two where the frontal drawing has two muzzles side by side (see "Turrets and their barrels" under the changes after 1.14).
- `back:` **smooth** (no hollow across the back: where a head of hair, a helmet's crest or a collar stands out behind the neck and a jacket or a pack lower down, the nape between them is filled out level with them, up to four voxels high; only the trunk, behind the body's middle) or **drawn** (as ever).
- `kneel:` **low** (kneeling, the body sits down on its heel: all of it but the legs, with what the hands hold, comes straight down until the hips are just over the ground, the raised knee before the chest. The games' kneeling drawings have a soldier on one knee with a straight back, his head not three pixels lower than as he stands, where the rules have a kneeling unit two thirds as tall (14 voxels of 22): made as drawn, a kneeling body stood in the line of fire of a soldier standing behind it) or **drawn** (as ever: as the kneeling drawings have it). The mod's table says `low` of every unit (the entry named `"*"`); take that entry out, or say `kneel: drawn` of a unit in a later entry, for the drawings' own height.
- `stands:` **floor** (standing, the body is drawn as much lower as its lowest cube is over the floor: for a creature the game stands on the ground whose feet the cut loses, so that it hung in the air; not a unit the game holds off the ground) or **drawn** (as ever).
- `hover:` **sparks** (a big unit that flies hovers on a rain of sparks and the dust they raise: saucers and hover vehicles) or **none** (nothing under it; not said, the same: a creature that flies, a gate, a jellyfish in the sea).
- A name of one star, `"*"`, is every unit. And a unit can be named by the routine the game draws it with, as the whole name `routine:N` (0: drawn as the soldier is, part by part).

A word matches a whole word of a name (a name's words are its runs of letters: `STR_GHOST_GAL` has STR, GHOST and GAL), so `CAT` finds `BLACK_CAT` and not `CATGIRL`. A star opens it: `GHOST*` a word's start, `*DOGE` a word's end (`BLOODOGE`), `*SPIDER*` any part of a word. A whole name names one unit (`BAN_6.PCK`, a sprite sheet; `STR_MY_UNIT_ARMOR`). Capitals or not is the same. A later entry, or a later mod's, overrides what it sets.

```yaml
firstPerson:
  units:
    - names: [GHOST*, "*GHOST", SPECTRE, POLTERGEIST, PHANTOM]
      seeThrough: 50
    - names: [DOG, "*DOGE", HOUND, CAT, RAT, SPIDER, SCORPION]
      shape: beast
    - names: [STR_MY_TALL_DOG]      # a mod's unit that stands upright after all
      shape: upright
    - names: [PIR]                  # XPiratez's gals and peasants (armors STR_PIR_..., sheets PIR_n)
      torsoDepth: 80
      top: back
      sculpt: frontal
    - names: [DAGON]                # The X-Com Files' Church of Dagon: robes, long sleeves
      arms: both
    - names: [STR_MY_STILL_BLOB]    # a mod's unit that should not pulse
      animation: none
```

The lists the mod comes with match 16 ghost armors and 49 animals of XPiratez and The X-Com Files, and XPiratez's 433 armors of gals and peasants; no unit of UFO or TFTD.

Three things the mod finds by itself, for any unit, by what its drawings are (they need no entry):

- **A unit animated as it stands** has a body for each frame (see `animation:` above).
- **A unit drawn the same from every side, and lopsided** (the game's Silacoid, Celatid and Bio-Drone routines draw one picture whichever way the unit faces) is that one drawing given volume, facing the way the unit does: thicker inside the drawing's outline, rounded at its edges. Lopsided: fewer than three quarters of the drawing's pixels have their mirror pixel drawn too. A Celatid or a Silacoid, even on both sides, stays carved as it was; The X-Com Files' Abomination, a cluster of bubbles off to one side, is a relief.
- **What a unit holds**: a melee weapon points the way its hand drawings draw it, where that is not the way a gun is held; a long item drawn from corner to corner in its inventory picture (a staff, a spear) is laid straight along the way it is held; an item standing in the hand shows its flat to the front unless its side drawings are clearly the broader ones; a plate the hand drawings show broad and solid from the front and thin from the sides (a shield) stands with its face ahead; and an item that would lie inside the body for more than a third of its length is held out in front of it.

## 7. What you'll see

- The map rebuilt in 3D from the game's own voxel data, with the original art and proportions, out to 60 tiles by default and up to 200 (*View distance*); what your squad hasn't seen yet in slate-coloured shapes, misty further off (or in a fog, or as it is: *Unseen terrain*).
- Doors that swing (each turn of a door, open or shut, swings to where the game has it) and UFO doors that slide.
- Inside a UFO or a craft, its floor goes on under the diagonal pieces of its hull (the map leaves the ground there), coloured from every UFO floor beside it.
- Animated machinery and light, calm and eased: animated terrain steps a frame about three times a second and cross-fades, so nothing large blinks (the photosensitivity guideline WCAG 2.3 allows at most three flashes a second). Where a part's colours in the two frames are far apart (another hue, or more than three shades: a piston's gold band where its rod was, steps folding flat), that spot goes over whole at the step's middle, so a part in motion doesn't stand in a coarse dot pattern; a light (two pictures taking turns, or colours changing over one pattern of shades) always cross-fades. In full colour with *Smooth light* the two frames are mixed smoothly.
- UFOs that stand out: steady hulls with glowing, softly pulsing lights, their own coloured light around them and an aura of drifting motes (the "UFO lights and aura" setting).
- Light and shadow: lamps, the sun, your flashlight, flares. Indoors (a roof overhead, or rock) no sun shines: faces there are lit by the room's own light, as on the map.
- TFTD's sea in waves: swells rolling across it in the sea's own colours, lighter crests breaking into foam, deeper troughs, and where the sea reaches the map's edge it goes on past it (round islands, ports and ships, the big mods' too).
- Bloom, night vision, rain and snow under clouds, dust, and in TFTD bubbles, marine snow and caustics.
- Other worlds' skies in the special missions: the stars all round in space, the Earth below a station in orbit, the Earth over the Moon, Mars' dusty sky (see [Skies and weather](#skies-and-weather)).
- Smoke, fire, explosions and animated machinery that flow from frame to frame.
- Lifts you ride: standing on a grav lift (or a ladder) shows LIFT PLATFORM arrows, and the view rises or sinks to the next level, at half the walking pace (see *Walking speed*); ladders and lift pads that have no shape in the game's data show as see-through ghosts of their art.
- Units seen from the side they face you.
- Sound that fades with distance (and is a little quieter behind you); rain and wind in the weather that has them, muffled under a roof, and fires crackling near you (*Ambient sounds*).
- Melee that lands: the weapon winds up and drives in towards the crosshair with a swish, the view lunges, the blow jolts, and the target flinches.
- Weapons:
  - shots by damage type and by gun: a round with a short tracer streak for bullets, a beam for lasers, a glowing blob for plasma, a flame with a smoke trail for rockets and explosive shells (and white specks that hang along a rocket's path for about ten seconds), embers for incendiaries, a blue blob for stun; each bigger for a bigger gun, a pistol's the smallest, then a rifle's, an auto-cannon's, a heavy weapon's (a laser beam is wider for a heavier laser, and up close);
  - under the sea: yellow darts and harpoons, pale blue gauss beams, pale cyan sonic blobs, torpedoes with the same smoke and specks, and bubbles behind every shot;
  - the weapon in hand built from voxels, its barrel aimed at the crosshair: your shots leave its muzzle, in either hand;
  - arrows, darts, bolts and javelins (weapons aimed with the throwing skill, as the big mods' bows, and guns firing their arrow, as crossbows; see [the item table](#the-item-table)) fly as a white streak with no muzzle flash and barely a kick (a burning arrow trails embers);
  - recoil, muzzle flashes by weapon class (bullets, explosives, fire, lasers, plasma, stun) and size (bigger for bigger guns), hitmarkers with sound, enemies that flinch, and with *Damage numbers* each hit's damage rising over the unit;
  - bullet holes (a dark core in a lighter ring) and scorch marks on whatever was hit, near or far (a far hole is a dot), blown-out walls in a burst of debris, burnt ground.
- When it gets tense:
  - enemy shots whizzing past you (you hear which side) with a flinch; a red arc on the screen's edge towards where a hit came from; blood on the view after a heavy hit or an alien's blow;
  - a red heartbeat at the screen edges when badly hurt;
  - going down: killed (red, then the helmet camera dies in static), unconscious (a fade to dark blue) or mind controlled (an orange haze), each with its message, before the next soldier's view comes in; killed by a Chryssalid (or a Tentaculat), TURNED INTO A ZOMBIE: down in red, then the body gets up again, lurching in a sickly green, until the camera gives out (their squad button shows a green skull, their camera says ZOMBIE);
  - on fire: the view in flames (a sheet of fire up from the bottom and the sides, embers drifting up, its light flickering), a BURNING banner and a flare-up when you catch fire, BURNING on the panel and the camera card; burning soldiers and aliens you see are in flames too;
  - breaking down at the start of a turn, seen through that soldier's eyes while they do what the rules make them do: panic (YOU HAVE A MENTAL BREAKDOWN, a fade to white, the heart racing, while they freeze, or drop their weapons and run) or berserk (BERSERK, white and red, every wild shot flashing red); then they come to, and their camera says PANIC or BERSERK for the rest of the turn;
  - tenser music while aliens are in sight;
  - "ENEMY SPOTTED!" and HIDDEN MOVEMENT banners (HIDDEN MOVEMENT glowing green on the civilians' turn).

## 8. Performance

The default 1280×800 window renders the 3D view at 640×400, shared out over every CPU thread the game may use (up to 32). On the test laptop (a Core Ultra 7 155H) a whole frame takes 9 to 10 ms there, 97 to 112 FPS with no frame limit, so the game's 60 FPS cap holds with room to spare.

On smaller machines it runs the same way on fewer threads. Measured by holding the game to some of the laptop's cores, at 1280×800:

| cores | pixel size 1 | pixel size 2 |
|---|---|---|
| 4 cores, 8 threads (the Steam Deck's count) | 57–60 FPS | 116 FPS |
| 4 efficiency cores | 41 FPS | 69–70 FPS |
| 2 low-power cores (2.5 GHz) | 19 FPS | 34 FPS |

So on a machine with four cores or fewer, set **pixel size** to 2: the picture gets a little blockier, and the frame rate nearly doubles.

The 3D view is drawn at the battlescape's size (the game's **Battlescape scale**), then magnified to the screen; its controls get bigger in steps from 720 lines (×2) and 1080 lines (×3). So the cost follows the battlescape's size, not the screen's. On a 1920×1080 screen (measured on the test laptop, a Core Ultra 7 155H, walking and turning in a town):

| Battlescape scale | 3D view | Frame rate |
|---|---|---|
| *screen/2* (default), pixel size **Auto** (default) | 960×540 (480×270 rays) | steady **60 FPS** (80–82 with no frame limit) |
| *screen/2*, pixel size 1 | 960×540 | 50–52 FPS |
| *screen* | 640×360, controls ×3 (small isometric map) | steady 60 FPS (70–74 with no frame limit) |
| *original* | 320×200 | steady 60 FPS (100 with no frame limit), chunky |

Sprite upscaling costs nothing measurable in a frame, *Finest* included: the bigger copies are made once each on a thread of their own (about 4 ms a frame's copy with *Finest*, 1.5 ms with *Smooth+*), the original drawn until it's ready; a crowded view up close (two squadmates filling a 640×400 view, on four cores) takes 5.8 ms a frame with any of them, and Off. Use **pixel size** 2 or 3 on slow machines too.

Smoke costs what it covers. A smoke grenade's cloud (about 90 tiles over two levels) in view adds about 1.8 ms a frame with *Smoke* Modern, 2.7 ms from inside it, and 1.3 ms with Original, 1.1 from inside (1920×1080, screen/2, the test machine's 22 threads); on a single core at 640×400 about 2 and 3 ms with Modern, 1.7 and 1.4 with Original. At night it costs the same (*Night smoke opacity* changes the look, not the work). All of it is drawn, but nothing that wouldn't show: puffs off the view or wholly behind a wall or a unit are left out, each puff is drawn only on the rows it covers, and only along its frame's coloured pixels (Original's dots leave most of a frame empty), the picture the same to the pixel. On a slow machine choose Original: one frame a tile, as the map draws it, it costs less than Modern's soft clouds, under half as much from inside a cloud.

**On a graphics card** (new in 1.14, setting *Renderer*: GPU). The card casts the world's rays and hands the picture back; the rest of the frame is drawn on the CPU as before. Measured on the test laptop's GeForce RTX 4060 (a town, walking and turning):

| 3D view | Rays on the CPU | Rays on the GPU | The whole 3D view on the GPU |
|---|---|---|---|
| 640×400 | 4.7–6.8 ms | 2.5–2.7 ms | |
| 1920×1080 (*3D resolution* Battle or Display) | 35–38 ms | 6–9 ms | 10–13 ms (38 ms on the CPU) |

So a full 1920×1080 view, which the CPU draws at about 25 frames a second, runs at 60 and more on the card. On built-in graphics the gain is smaller: the same laptop's Intel Arc casts a 1920×1080 view's rays in about 20 ms (the whole 3D view in 23–24 ms, about 40 frames a second), so there *Pixel size* 2 or a smaller *Battlescape scale* still helps. The picture is the CPU's: in every test save the two differ in under 0.1% of the pixels (a voxel's edge here and there, where the two round a number differently).

## 9. Known limits and ideas

- **A real-time mode** will be attempted in the future: the battle running continuously instead of turn by turn, for those who want it, as a setting like everything else. Doom mode's real-time grenades are the first piece of it.
- **A 3D battlescape** is another idea for the future: the same voxel renderer seen from a free camera above the battle (turn, zoom, pan), with the levels above cut away as on the isometric map, and orders given by clicking tiles and units, a 3D take on the isometric map beside the first-person view.
- **The art:** soldiers and aliens are 32×40 pixel sprites seen from the side they face you, terrain is the game's 16×16×24 voxel shapes coloured from the isometric pictures, all in 256 colours. Sides the isometric art never shows are filled in (crafts and UFOs from their mirrored side; with *Wall paint* a wall's face the map never shows takes the paint of the side it faces). Ideas that would add resolution without changing the style: rendering the view at a higher scale, smoother voxel shading, colour sampled at the exact hit point, units given volume like the weapon in hand. Good ideas are welcome.
- **Shots are drawn by kind,** not from a mod's own projectile pictures (an arrow, a bolt): a mod's arrows are white streaks, its custom damage types (Piratez's electric, warp, mind...) bullets for now. Using the mods' pictures is an idea for later.
- **Sound:** stereo can't tell front from back; sounds behind you are only a little quieter.
- **Not saved:** bullet holes, scorch marks and flares are gone after a quickload or when the screen is rebuilt (each soldier's flight switch is saved with the game).
- **Leaning** shows what's round a corner, but the game's own sight still starts from the middle of the soldier's tile: an alien there shows once the soldier (or someone else) spots it. The weapon in hand stays level while the view tilts.
- **Leaning and fair play** (a player's note: the aliens can't lean by choice, so leaning gives the player a small edge). What the lean gives, exactly: it **shows no more than the game does**: the game's sight doesn't change, units it doesn't see aren't drawn, and the lean's room check leaves them out, so a peek shows terrain, never a hidden alien. A **shot** from a lean (and with *Lean shots*) starts from the game's own off-centre origins, a couple of voxels to the side. OXCE has a game option, **off-centre shooting** (`oxceEnableOffCentreShooting`), with which every unit, the aliens included, tries left and right of centre when the centre has no line of fire; with it on, the aliens have that reach too, and the player only gets to pick the side. For the most even game, play with that option on, or leave *Lean shots* off and lean only to look. (A shot whose exact point is blocked is tried from left and right of centre, and falls back on the game's own aim, left and right of centre too, only with that option on: the same tries the aliens get. FPS-style shooting is no part of the game's rules anyway, no roll and no "No line of fire", and its rounds leave from your eye.) An idea for later: shots from a lean only when that option is on. Letting the AI lean by choice belongs to the game itself (a fork such as Brutal-OXCE could), not to this add-on.
- **The game's own messages** (e.g. "has become unconscious") pause the 3D sequences behind them until you click OK, as all battlescape animation does.
- **Platforms:** Linux (x86-64, the Steam Deck included) is built and played here. Windows (64-bit) is built here too and played under Wine; the automatic builds check on a real Windows that it starts and finds the games. macOS (Apple silicon and Intel) is built by the automatic builds only, and hasn't been played on a real Mac yet: reports are very welcome.
- **Languages:** English (US and UK) only.

## 10. For developers

### What it is based on (exactly)

| | |
|---|---|
| Upstream repository | **https://github.com/MeridianOXC/OpenXcom.git** (OpenXcom Extended by Meridian) |
| Branch | **`oxce-plus`** |
| Commit | **`f0134ef8065799c7723953d64b565f7832bfd782`** — 2026-09-29 15:04:49 +0200, author Meridian, "Merge remote-tracking branch 'SupSuper/master' into oxce-plus" |
| Version string | **OpenXcom Extended 8.7.1 (v2026-09-19)** |
| OpenXcom3D | engine add-on and mod version **1.15** (`requiredExtendedEngine: OpenXcom3D`, `requiredExtendedVersion: "1.0.15"`: OXCE would read "1.09" as 1.9, so the engine counts 1.0.9, 1.0.10, 1.0.11, 1.0.12) |

`openxcom3d.patch` holds every change on top of that commit:

- the core hooks: 48 files, about 1,900 lines (half of them the shot's own, in `ProjectileFlyBState`), with the build files and the logo's icons;
- the new `src/FirstPerson/` add-on;
- the `mod/OpenXcom3D/` data mod.

It applies with `git apply` to a clean checkout of the commit above.

Built with `-DOXCE_FIRSTPERSON=OFF`, it is plain OXCE again: the add-on is left out and the hooks are no-ops. A few fixes to the game itself stay in: a muzzle never inside what the unit stands on, and no division by zero for a sight line straight up or down.

To work on it: check out the commit above, `git apply openxcom3d.patch`, build (see [Option B](#option-b-build-from-source)), and make your changes; `git diff` (plus the new files under `src/FirstPerson/` and `mod/`) gives the next patch.

### Automatic builds

Every push and pull request runs two workflows on GitHub ([Actions](https://github.com/b0realis/OpenXcom3D/actions)). Neither machine has the original games' data, so each game is checked as far as it gets without it.

- **Build** builds the three downloads the way the releases are made and checks each where it runs:
  - *Linux:* the tarball and the AppImage, in a clean Ubuntu 26.04; SDL2 must have the releases' features, no more, no fewer (`tools/ci/sdl2_features.sh`). Then both start on Rocky Linux 9 (glibc 2.34, the oldest supported), Ubuntu 22.04, Debian 12, Fedora and Arch (`tools/ci/linux_release_check.sh`).
  - *Windows:* the zip, built on Linux by the build guide's own commands, then checked on GitHub's Windows (`tools/ci/windows_zip_check.ps1`): the game starts; `play.bat` with no data says where it goes; with a stand-in Steam (its folder in the registry, a second library on another disk) it finds both games, links them, writes the starting options and starts the game; and it follows the data when it moves.
  - *macOS:* the app, universal and for macOS 11 and newer (`tools/macos/`, `tools/make_release_macos.sh`), checked by `tools/macos/check.sh` (both architectures, no newer macOS asked for, only the system's libraries and the two inside, the signature), then started natively and under Rosetta.

  The downloads stay with each run for 30 days (the run's *Artifacts*). A tag `v*` also makes a draft release with all three, which a person reads over and publishes; a tag whose release is there already (its Linux and Windows downloads made and checked by hand) gets what it lacks: the macOS app.
- **Build guide** (also once a week, since the systems change underneath it) runs the [build guide](https://b0realis.github.io/OpenXcom3D/)'s commands as a reader copies them: `tools/ci/guide_commands.py` reads them off the page and changes only what a machine without a person needs (package managers don't ask; the patch is the checkout's; the game is asked its version instead of started), each change marked next to the guide's own line. They run on fresh Ubuntu 26.04, 24.04 and 22.04, Debian 13, Fedora and Arch, in MSYS2 on GitHub's Windows, and `install.py --from-source` runs too.

Both get OXCE at the commit `install.py` names, with the patch applied (`tools/ci/fetch_oxce.sh`, which also checks that `src/` and `mod/` here match the patch).

### Code layout

**`src/FirstPerson/`, about 45,000 lines**

Every file starts with the project's philosophy and a description of what's in it, and every function and block is commented. The two big classes are split into one file per topic, and their headers follow the same order.

| File | What |
|---|---|
| `FirstPersonApi.{h,cpp}` | **The only header core files include.** No-op inline stubs when built with `-DOXCE_FIRSTPERSON=OFF` (`OXCE_NO_FIRSTPERSON`). Also registers the add-on's options |
| `FirstPersonMode.h`, `FirstPersonModeShared.h` | **The controller and UI** (`FirstPersonMode`); layout constants, timings and colour helpers its files share |
| `FirstPersonMode.cpp` | Creating the view and the HUD, the canvas magnified on big screens, switching views, the frame (`think`), input, drawing the screen |
| `FirstPersonModeCamera.cpp` | Turning by mouse and keys, free facing, turning to an enemy, following the soldier's eye |
| `FirstPersonModeMovement.cpp` | Real-time walking (each tile through the game's own walking), doors, jumps and vaults, grav lifts, flying, falling, stopping when an enemy is spotted |
| `FirstPersonModeShooting.cpp` | Weapons and fire modes, aiming along the game's line of fire, firing through the game's own actions, FPS-style spread and recoil, dual wield, the crosshair, flares, aiming on the map |
| `FirstPersonModeWeaponInHand.cpp` | The weapon (or any other item) in the hands, made from its inventory picture (measured, then given volume as voxels), muzzle flashes, swings |
| `FirstPersonModeHud.cpp` | Top bar, squad buttons, info panel, soldier cards with the live helmet camera, the soldier cam, the map camera, banner, enemy markers |
| `FirstPersonModeSoldierStates.cpp` | Hurt, near misses, on fire, going down (killed, zombie, unconscious, mind control), breakdowns (panic, berserk) |
| `FirstPersonModeFeed.cpp` | Setting *Live command feed*: the battle as a command post, the game's inventory (its `Inventory` widget) worked in a window, scaled, the 3D view played in a window (the view made at the window's size), the map in the drone's window (through `Camera::setViewWindow`, the game's panel moved under it) turned black and white with static, the soldiers' marks, the tactical map in its window |
| `FirstPersonView.h`, `FirstPersonViewShared.h` | **The renderer** (`FirstPersonView`); voxel proportions, dithering, shading and noise helpers its files share |
| `FirstPersonView.cpp` | The camera, and drawing one frame (the order of a frame and the threading rule are explained at the top of `FirstPersonView.h`) |
| `FirstPersonViewTerrain.cpp` | Voxel models from the LOFT shapes coloured by the isometric sprites, crafts' mirrored sides, doors, the ground outside the map, the per-frame tile snapshot |
| `FirstPersonViewRayWalk.h` | The ray walk: tile by tile, then voxel by voxel in tiles that have something in them; a byte per tile and a bit per voxel let it skip the air (the renderer's hottest code: every pixel's ray and every shadow ray) |
| `FirstPersonViewRayCasting.cpp` | Colours for the time of day, light and shadow rays, shading what a ray hit, the border and the sky, the rays; the view's threads (one per CPU thread, up to 32, kept between frames), which also draw the units, items, see-through smoke and colour wash in bands of rows |
| `FirstPersonViewSprites.cpp` | Units and items as the game's own sprites standing in the world (worked out on the game's thread, recorded, then drawn on all threads in bands of rows); the see-through smoke; enemy markers |
| `FirstPersonViewUpscaling.cpp` | Optional smoother sprites up close (xBRZ on a worker thread) |
| `FirstPersonGpu.{h,cpp}`, `FirstPersonGpuGl.h`, `FirstPersonGpuShaders.h` | Setting *Renderer*: GPU. The rays on the graphics card: the voxel models and each frame's tile snapshot go up as plain buffers, and a fragment shader walks and shades each pixel's ray. The shader is `castRay`, `traceTile`, `shadeHit`, `colorOutsideMap` and `skyColor` line for line, under the same names (change one, change the other; the constants are written into it from `FirstPersonViewShared.h`). Its own thread and hidden OpenGL context (EGL on Linux, CGL on macOS, looked up at run time: no new build dependency; Windows' WGL in `FirstPersonGpuWindows.cpp`). The finer picture's settings live in the shader alone (`uSoftShadows`, `uAllLightShadows`, `uAmbientOcclusion`: `crease`); *Supersampling* runs the rays' program once for each of a pixel's rays (`uRayOffset`) into pictures of its own, and a second small program (`ResolveSource`) makes the frame's picture of them with a table of the palette colour nearest each mix (`sendBlend`) |
| `FirstPersonViewGpu.cpp` | The view's side of it: the frame handed to the card, its picture, depth and glow into the view's own buffers; and the shadow run that proves it (`OPENXCOM3D_GPU_SHADOW=1`: the CPU draws, the card beside it, every thirtieth frame each ray and each colour compared, both timed in the log) |
| `FirstPersonPresent.cpp` | Setting *3D resolution*: Display. A view the display's size is not drawn into the game's screen: the mode fills the screen with a key where it is to show, and when the engine shows the frame (`Screen::flip`, or `Zoom::flipWithZoom` with OpenGL output) the display takes the view's pixels wherever the key still is. The 3D HUD goes the same way: its canvas is laid out for the display (`canvasTarget`, `relayout`) and put over the view in a display-sized picture (`blit`), which is what is laid in. In full colour (*True colour*): the card writes each pixel's colour to a fourth picture (`outTrue`), the view keeps it with the rays' palette colours (`trueColor`), and `blit` hands the flip a full-colour pixel wherever the finished palette picture still has its ray's colour |
| `FirstPersonViewEffects.cpp` | Particles: explosions, fire, smoke, weather, UFO devices, bloom |
| `FirstPersonViewShots.cpp` | Shots in flight, thrown items as tumbling voxel models, trails, beams, impacts, bullet holes and scorches, debris |
| `FirstPersonViewFlares.cpp` | Tactical flares and their light |
| `FirstPersonViewUfo.cpp` | UFOs ("UFO lights and aura"): steady 3D-only models of UFO parts made from their frames (lights marked, tall parts trimmed to the sprite), the UFOs found on the map, their two-coloured light as a per-tile field, the lights' pulse, the aura |
| `FirstPersonViewScreenEffects.cpp` | On the screen itself: the hurt arc, blood, flames, colour washes, static |
| `FirstPersonVoxelModel.{h,cpp}` | An item's inventory picture made into a voxel model, a cushion or a blade's flat plate (the weapon or item in hand, thrown items) |
| `FirstPersonVoxelUnits.{h,cpp}` | Setting *Voxel units*: the units' bodies, one for each unit and pose, shared by the 3D views and the battle's hits; the frames captured from the game's unit drawer, the builder's thread |
| `FirstPersonVr.{h,cpp}`, `FirstPersonModeVr.cpp`, `FirstPersonViewStereo.cpp` | VR mode (experimental): the headset's interface (head and hands in, eye pictures out), the VR rules (turning, walking, the weapon in the hand, the laser), the stereo eyes |
| `FirstPersonSdl.h` | one code for SDL 1.2 (the desktop) and SDL2 (the Quest build): key names, key state, the wheel |
| `FirstPersonVoxelBodies.{h,cpp}` | How a body is made from a unit's frames: its shape, painting, matching each direction's picture, walk, fall and hits |
| `FirstPersonParticles.{h,cpp}` | The particle pool (sparks, smoke, weather...) |
| `FirstPersonSounds.{h,cpp}` | Sounds made in code (no files): hitmarker, no-harm and kill ticks, heartbeat, dry-fire click, flare hiss, near-miss whizz, melee swoosh, the motion scanner's pulse and blips |
| `FirstPersonAmbience.{h,cpp}` | Setting *Ambient sounds*: the rain, the wind and a fire, made in code (no files) the first time the 3D view shows a battle. Six loops, outdoors and indoors, near and far, each pair in step, on six mixer channels of their own (5..10; the game keeps its eleven free ones). They cross-fade by how open the sky is round the eye, and a fire's loudness and side follow where it burns (`FirstPersonView::ambientScene` in FirstPersonViewEffects.cpp) |
| `FirstPersonDamageNumbers.{h,cpp}` | Setting *Damage numbers*: each hit's health, armour and stun lost (from `TileEngine::hitUnit`), rising and fading, stepped clear of each other; drawn in 3D (`FirstPersonView::drawDamageNumbers`) and over the isometric map (`FirstPerson::drawMapDamageNumbers`) |
| `FirstPersonScanner.{h,cpp}` | The motion scanner held up in 3D: the game's own scanner picture turned with the view, a sweeping ring, blips that fade, and their beeps |
| `FirstPersonSpriteHits.{h,cpp}` | Setting *Realistic hitboxes*: where a line of fire crosses a unit's picture, a card standing where the 3D view draws it (its feet on the floor's top, a unit on the move between its two tiles, the view's own leaning card for the soldier looked through), over any tile the picture reaches (`spriteHitAt`, `spriteImpact`), the frame from that side kept per unit and side; the same cards for the *Hitboxes* outline (`viewCard`, `cardRay`); or with *Voxel units* its body |
| `FirstPersonQuickDeaths.cpp` | Setting *Quick deaths*: the falls under way, the frame of each, and a unit put in that frame's pose for drawing (on the map and in 3D) |
| `FirstPersonChains.{h,cpp}` | Setting *Instant chain explosions*: a chain settled at once shown as one big blast rippling out (`chainLink` keeps the map and the units as they were; the fireballs' sprites, the tiles held as they were until the ripple passes them, the dead standing until it has; `ChainStandingPose`) |
| `OptionsFirstPersonState.{h,cpp}` | 3D VIEW options tab: Settings / Controls sub-tabs, scrolling lists |
| `RuleFirstPerson.h` | `firstPerson:` ruleset section (header-only): enabled, view distance, window glass, weather, skies, combat music, built-in items shown in hand, the item table (held shape, shots) |

What `FirstPersonView` draws:
- terrain from LOFT voxels with iso-sprite projection, animated frames, UFO doors cut out as they slide, doors swinging, window glass;
- smooth light plus sun face shading, Bayer dithering and horizon haze; a colour shaded past the darkest of its colour group turns black only where the place is really dark (night past the lights, an unlit room), and in a lit place stays that darkest colour (a burnt wooden floor dark brown, not a black hole); colour washes (hurt, dying, unconscious, mind control, breakdown, zombie); purple edges through a unit under our mind control; night vision recolouring; the fire over the view of a burning soldier;
- the outside world: ground, X-COM tape and posts (or the discreet edge's dots); the sky (the Earth's, overcast in rain and snow, or another world's: see [Skies and weather](#skies-and-weather); in space nothing but space past the edge). Underground, rock: below a map's surface beyond its edges, and over a map that's underground all the way up (a base, an alien colony's inside, Cydonia's complex), found from the map's own edge (see [Underground maps](#underground-maps));
- terrain not explored yet: as *Unseen terrain* has it (by default plain slate-coloured shapes, never black, which reads as missing, turning to a soft misty blue-grey further off; or 0.9's dark shapes in a rising fog; or drawn as it is, a little greyer), and with *Fog of war in 3D* Off drawn as seen where the view itself sees it within the soldiers' sight range; a hole through the ground floor shows the earth below;
- cards for units, floor items, projectiles and explosions, facing the camera and leaning with its pitch only up to 45° looking down (so they stay above the floor seen from above) and 10° looking up (so a unit close by doesn't lurch over the camera), standing on the floor's top; the art of items and corpses below their anchor lies flat on the floor; units glide, and fade right in front of the camera;
- particles; numbered markers over the enemies the squad sees (red, green or yellow: setting *Enemy markers*) and over our own wounded (blue, purple: *Wounded markers*); the path a throw or shot would take (setting *Show trajectory*);
- UFO parts: the art's black holes (the inside of a curved hull, its black outline) painted like the voxels around them, and on a UFO a colour shaded past the darkest of its group stays that darkest colour instead of black; a craft's unseen sides borrow from its mirrored side, never black, and inside only from the same kind of tile part;
- the view tilted about its own axis when leaning (setting *Lean*: Tilt): the rays and everything projected take the tilt as it is, and the cards, which run row by row level, are then drawn pixel by pixel instead.

**Core changes** (see `openxcom3d.patch` for every line), placed where upstream rarely edits:

| File | Change |
|---|---|
| `CMakeLists.txt` (top) | `option(OXCE_FIRSTPERSON ... ON)` |
| `src/CMakeLists.txt` | Adds the `firstperson_src` list, or defines `OXCE_NO_FIRSTPERSON`; links Threads |
| `BattlescapeState.{h,cpp}` | `FirstPerson::create/destroy/think/handle/blit/wantsMouseCapture/resized`; `friend class FirstPersonMode` |
| `BattlescapeGame.h` | `BattleAction::targetVoxel` (exact aim point), `instantMove`, `freeAim` (FPS-style shot), `dualWield`/`dualWeapon`/`dualType` (dual wield) |
| `Projectile.h` | `getAmmo()` and `getWeapon()` getters (trail style from the ammo's damage type, size from the gun) |
| `ProjectileFlyBState.{h,cpp}` | Uses `targetVoxel` when set (accuracy still applies); with `freeAim` asks the first-person view for each round's target; dual wield: the other gun's round fired, flown and landed with each round (like a shotgun's extra pellets), both half as accurate; a failed close-quarters check clears `targetVoxel` and `freeAim`, so the deflected shot goes where the game sends it; a lean side (`relativeOrigin`, setting *Lean shots*) with no line of fire falls back to the centre |
| `BattlescapeGame.cpp` | A soldier panicking or going berserk: in 3D the first-person view shows it (`FirstPerson::breakdown`, which holds the battle as long as the box would) instead of the "has panicked" box; `primaryAction`: akimbo on the map (`FirstPerson::mapAkimbo`); `handleState`: the battle waits while a chain of explosions settled at once ripples out in 3D, its dead falling after it (`FirstPerson::chainRippleHolds`; setting *Instant chain explosions*) |
| `ExplosionBState.{h,cpp}` | FPS-style shooting: our own bullet hits play quicker (`FirstPerson::fastShots`); a missed blow's or psi attack's animation is marked (`Explosion::setMiss`); a blast that sets off explosive terrain settles the whole chain at once in 3D, under it (`settleChain`: each explosive's damage and dead as the game's own, without its animation; `FirstPerson::instantChains`, `chainLink`, `chainSettled`), and sounds as the chain's big boom |
| `MeleeAttackBState.cpp` | A blow at terrain lands on the very point the crosshair struck (`targetVoxel`) |
| `UnitDieBState.{h,cpp}` | Setting *Quick deaths*: a direct hit's victim goes down at once (`FirstPerson::quickDeaths`, `noteQuickFall`), but not one that turns into another (a Zombie); a unit going down over no floor comes down in 3D (`FirstPerson::noteAirFall`) |
| `MainMenuState.cpp` | The mod's version in small letters beside the title (`FirstPerson::addMenuVersion`) |
| `BattlescapeGenerator.cpp` | Setting *Auto-equip guns first*: guns before melee weapons, the hardest-hitting first (`FirstPerson::autoEquipGunsFirst`, `sortAutoEquip`, `autoEquipTakes`) |
| `SavedBattleGame.{h,cpp}` | `addFallingUnit`: X-COM's units float where they are with Doom IDCLIP (`FirstPerson::noclipFloats`); `getBrokenGlass()`: the window glass the 3D view shows broken, kept in the save (`openxcom3dBrokenGlass`), cleared for a new map |
| `BattleItem.cpp` | `spendAmmoForAction`: no ammo spent with Doom IDKFA (`FirstPerson::endlessAmmo`), beside the game's own debug mode |
| `BattleUnit.{h,cpp}` | `damage`: X-COM's units take none with Doom IDDQD (`FirstPerson::invincible`); `getMapAkimbo()`: akimbo on the map; `getFlightSwitch()`: the 3D view's flight switch, saved with the unit (`flightSwitch`); `getTurretOnly()`: a tank's turret switch (`turretOnly`) |
| `Projectile.cpp` | With `freeAim`: no accuracy roll, no line-of-fire refusal, the line carried on through the target, a bullet's speed; `calculateThrow`: an arc blocked at its very first step (a shot from against a wall) leaves no path, which was read from its end (a crash): another try; a forced arc that leaves the map is tried again (another deviation), as an unforced one is, not taken as a hit where it left (it burst in the open air); with *Realistic hitboxes* an arcing shot at a big unit aims at its middle, where its picture stands (`FirstPerson::arcAimAtPicture`) |
| `UnitWalkBState.cpp` | `instantMove` walks take the no-animation path |
| `Map.{h,cpp}` | `getNightVisionColor()`, `isNightVisionOn()` getters; `setSoundListener()`: in 3D, `getSoundAngle` pans sounds for the 3D camera; `setNightVisionHoldBlocked()`: in 3D the hold key (Space) jumps; `getPartnerProjectile()`: the dual-wield round in flight; `drawTerrain`, last: the *Damage numbers* over the map (`FirstPerson::drawMapDamageNumbers`); `noteShotLanded`: each round's landing for the 3D views (where it ended and came from, its ammo and weapon, and the gun that fired it, only compared, to tell a soldier's two guns apart) |
| `Sound.{h,cpp}` | `packDistance()`: in 3D the listener packs the distance into the stereo angle, so sounds fade with distance without touching the call sites |
| `Pathfinding.{h,cpp}` | `setPath(unit, path)`: a step set by hand (the step off a ledge). `setFlightCost(unit, tu)`: the soldier flying in 3D with SUIT FLY on pays the "flight cost per level" setting for up/down (everyone else: the rules). `setVault(unit, parts)`: for the step a vault takes, those low tile parts don't block that one unit and cost nothing (`isBlocked`, the wall and object costs). `setStrafeMove()`: the 3D view prices a sidestep as the game's strafe |
| `main.cpp` | Window caption " - OpenXcom3D" (add-on builds) |
| `InteractiveSurface.cpp` | Upstream UB fixes found by UBSan: button 0 ignored (no shift by −1), `SDLK_ANY` inside the enum |
| `Script.h` | Upstream UB fix found by UBSan: `ScriptRef::compare` doesn't `memcmp` empty (null) refs |
| `TileEngine.{h,cpp}` | `hit`: a unit struck to no harm (its health and stun as before) marks the 3D crosshair (`FirstPerson::unitStruck`). `tryReaction`: a reaction shot with the soldier's akimbo marked fires both guns (`FirstPerson::reactionAkimbo`). `validateThrow`: an arc blocked at its very first step (a shot from against a wall) leaves no path, which was read from its end (a crash): no arc at that curvature. Upstream UB fix found by UBSan: `canTargetUnit` and `checkVoxelExposure` don't divide by zero when the shooter and the target stand in one column (the side scan lines then lie on the centre line); `getOriginVoxel`: on something tall under a ceiling, where the game pushes the muzzle under the floor above and 4 voxels lower, it never goes below the top of what the unit stands on (it went a voxel into it, and shots struck whatever stood about as high nearby); an FPS-style straight shot (`freeAim`) asks the first-person view, which moves its origin across the tile to the 3D eye's place, at the same height. `hitUnit`: a hit's health, armour and stun lost for *Damage numbers* (`FirstPerson::unitDamaged`, `armorPoints`). With *Realistic hitboxes*, `calculateLineVoxel` keeps each line's start, end and number, `voxelCheck` meets a unit's picture where the line crosses it over any tile (`FirstPerson::spriteHitAt`), and a round that met a picture stops on the unit's own tile (`spriteImpact`); a curve's steps meet the pictures facing its start, the hand (`calculateParabolaVoxel`, `FirstPerson::ArcLines`), and `validateThrow` takes a shot's curve that meets the unit aimed at on any of its tiles (`arcMeetsAimedUnit`, never a throw's). `checkReactionFire`: reaction fire at a unit stops its walk in 3D, as on the map (`FirstPerson::reactionFiredAt`) |
| `Explosion.{h,cpp}` | `getLastFrame()`: the last frame of an explosion's own animation (the 3D view's frame blend stops there); `setMiss()`/`isMiss()`: a missed blow's animation (no sparks in 3D) |
| `State.h`, `Game.{h,cpp}` | Generic hook: the top state may ask for mouse capture (relative mouse look); released with the cursor centred for every other state, each release counted (`getMouseReleases`) so the 3D view can ignore the cursor's jump |
| `UnitSprite.{h,cpp}` | `setViewRotation()`: draw a unit from any side |
| `Options.cpp` / `Options.inc.h` | `createOptionsOTHER()` → `FirstPerson::createOptions(_info)`; declarations at the end of the file |
| `OptionInfo.cpp` | Loading a key binding clears Shift, Alt and Ctrl, except for `keyFpsLeanHold` (the lean key, held with the strafe keys, Left Shift by default) |
| `OptionsBaseState.{h,cpp}` | 3D VIEW tab via `FirstPerson::hasOptionsTab/createOptionsTab` |
| `Mod.{h,cpp}` | Parses `firstPerson:` at the end of `Mod::loadFile` |
| `ModInfo.cpp` | Engine row `{"OpenXcom3D", 1.0.15}` (add-on builds only; 1.15) |
| `CrossPlatform.cpp` | Add-on builds use their own user/config folders (`~/.local/share/openxcom3d`, `~/.config/openxcom3d`, `Documents/OpenXcom3D`, …). Data folders are shared with stock OXCE |
| `OpenXcom.2010.vcxproj{,.filters}` | The add-on's files |

## 11. Version history

### After 1.15 (not released)

- **Palms and tree crowns without pillars** (the owner: "why do trees have floating green blocks?"). The games give a tree's leaves a shape of a few lone voxels scattered over the tile (LOFT template 78: nine single voxels, so that a shot now and then meets a leaf), repeated layer over layer; drawn as it was, each of them stood as a pillar a voxel thin and up to a tile high, green from the art: a crown of floating green bars (XPiratez's and The X-Com Files' palms, TFTD's island palms, UFO's forest trees). Where such a scatter runs five layers or more up an object, those layers are now made from the art instead, as the map draws them: each leaf a few voxels deep around the middle of the tile, so from the map's angle the leaves are the art and from the ground a crown of fronds. Shots still meet the game's own scatter. Low ones (flower beds, wheat, a car's or a tower's layers between solid ones) keep the game's shape: 1 record in UFO, 13 in TFTD, 52 in XPiratez, 36 in The X-Com Files.

- *Voxel units*: **the reptoids' and the Tasoths' tails behind their legs** (a player, GitHub issue #267: "MOST tails seem to merge with leg", and TFTD's Tasoth "one weird, deformed tail leg"). Their sheets draw the tail in the legs' frames, down behind the legs and off to one side; side on it is a second run of pixels, which was taken for a leg: one leg came out as a tail-leg. A new unit-table word, `tail: swept`, given by name to the sheets that draw such a tail (The X-Com Files' Reptoid agents and engineers, the Croc Cadet, the Commando, the Shadow, the Drake leader and commander, its Tasoth; TFTD's Tasoth soldier and squad leader; XPiratez's Tasoths, sheet DEEP_8), keeps the tail out of the legs, so they come out as two clean legs, and makes the tail from what all eight drawings agree on behind them, no wider than the drawn tail. It stays with the body as it walks and kneels (sitting low as before). The Pharaoh and the reptoid VIP, drawn without a tail, are not named. Every other body unchanged: 1139 bodies byte for byte offline, and in the game on the tester's save, a TFTD zoo and an XPiratez save only the named units changed.

- **No falling into solid rock** (a player, GitHub issue #266: a chem grenade melted a sewer's floor in XPiratez, a gnome fell "into the floor", and a Humongous Raider stepping into the hole in 3D fell in on top and knocked it out). XPiratez's sewers stand on rock: every tile of the level below is a solid block that fills it, yet drawn at the tile's foot, so the game didn't count it as ground under a floor that was gone, and a unit fell into the rock and stuck there. Now a tile whose floor is gone stands on a solid block below it (one that fills its tile to the top and that no one walks into), with OpenXcom3D's mod on: the hole is walked over on the rock's top, as the map draws it, and nothing falls in (in all the games: UFO has 33 such blocks, TFTD 197, XPiratez 955). And in 3D a step off a ledge (*Falling*) never ends on a unit the game knows of or inside a solid block, not even pushed again: the map's own walk never ends there. A unit already stuck in rock in an older save stays there.

- **No floor's edge under the outer walls** (a player, GitHub issue #264: "Siding on some faces uses some of the interior floor texture on backsides, right side texture is presented differently than left"): an upper storey's floor runs out under the outer wall's foot, and seen from outside its edge showed as a band in the room's carpet at every storey (where the wall's own shape starts above the floor). The map draws the wall down to the floor and never a floor's edge, so now a floor's side at the tile's edge where a wall stands takes the wall's colour there (its lowest row: the art's own on the side it draws, the wall's paint on the side it never shows), in both renderers. The siding runs unbroken from storey to storey.

- **Live command feed** (the owner: "Live command feed - herein the iso mode is replaced with realistic combat dashboard", from police tactical units' helmet cameras streaming to a command post; then "only one larger selected window ... you could control the soldier in his larger selected window and drone feed and tac map would update in real time", "let it be performant!", and "Equipment windows should be just inventory! And always keep the small buttons on the top with soldier mini cam Cards"): a new experimental setting, the last under Advanced, NO by default. The battle as the top bar with the squad's buttons and their helmet camera cards, the selected soldier's inventory (the game's own, worked in its window), their 3D view in a window where they are played, the map as a drone's feed (black and white with static, or night vision; names and boxes over the soldiers) with the game's panel under it, and the tactical map beside it, all live. The 3D view is drawn at its window's size: uncapped, the feed ran at 55 frames a second where the full 3D view on the same spot ran at 41. The drone's feed is the game's own map in a window: the engine's camera now centres in a window it is given, scrolls at its edges and tells what is on screen by it (`Camera::setViewWindow`), and the game's panel is moved under it. The tactical map can stand in a window (its zoom of its own, no OK, following the selected soldier). (The same day it first had a row of every soldier's helmet camera, then a strip of cards and an equipment panel of its own.)

- *Voxel units*: **XPiratez's gals have a woman's figure** (the owner, of a gal in the Wench outfit: "lets just make her body leaner, human like and feminine (breasts...)", and then for "All oufits"). XPiratez draws its gals broad and muscular, and the bodies followed the drawings. A new unit-table word, `figure: feminine`, given by name to every outfit drawn on the gals' body (the 145 armours with `layersDefaultPrefix: UBERGAL`), makes the standard humanoid leaner whatever the drawing's build: the trunk narrower at the shoulders and the chest (to 62 and 85% of the drawn breadth), the waist as drawn and the lower trunk, where a belt sits above the legs, a tenth wider than drawn (the owner, of the first narrow waist: "Enlarge waist to resemble normal female body, now is too narrow!", and then: "keep chest 85 % but increase the waist - where chest meets the legs - where belt would be"), a little shallower; the shoulder joints in with the shoulders, the arms and legs thinner (62 and 72%); and a bust on the chest. Height and pose stay the drawing's, the colours its paint. Every other body unchanged: on the #216 house save only its three gals changed (the Lok'nar, the peasants, a cat girl and the enemies byte for byte), on the zoo save nothing. The other races (cat girls, gnomes, hybrids...) are drawn on bodies of their own and keep them.
- **Obliteration in UFO and TFTD** (the owner: "make a setting in advanced: obliteration in UEU and TFTD on/off. And replicate xpiratez and xcom files obliteration for og games also"): a new setting under Combat, NO by default. The original games' damage types all ignore overkill, so no unit there ever bursts from a hit (a blast destroying a body on the floor does). With it on, explosives, lasers and plasma (Gauss, Sonic) have overkill as the big mods give their explosives, plasma and lasers (XPiratez: 137 of its 200 explosives, 52 of 69 plasma, 19 of 83 lasers; The X-Com Files: 54 of 94, 33 of 38, 40 of 50; seldom a bullet, a blade or a stun): a unit they take below minus three quarters of its health leaves no corpse and bursts (first minus half, OXCE's own rule; the owner: "Make it 75%"). Only where no loaded mod has overkill of its own. A heavy plasma's shot at a Sectoid: with it off a corpse in green blood, with it on the burst.
- **Wall paint is always All rooms** (a player, GitHub issue #264: "backside of textures literally didn't exist in vanilla, we should just assume responsibility for that being on"; the owner: "lets have All rooms always and thats it"). Its row has left the tab; the setting and its other two choices stay in the code and the profile (see *Wall paint* among the settings without a row). And **the backs of the alien entertainment panels are a plain wall**: a wall whose picture animates (a display: those panels, a computer's screen) now lends its paint to no other wall (copies of one frame of the show had stood still on the walls round it), and its back takes the paint of the nearest plain wall made of the panel's own frame (the alien base's grey wall, not the cave's rock). Its paint holds in every frame of the animation: it had held in the first frame only, so the backs flickered between rock and the show.
- **Liquid screens** (a player's idea on GitHub issue #261; the owner: "Build it"): a new setting under Graphics, YES by default, with *Reflections*: the alien entertainment panels' screens ripple like still water standing on end and mirror the room round them. The panels are named in the mod's rules (`liquidScreens:`): X-COM's, and the same panels in XPiratez's and The X-Com Files' sets.
- **A lift is only where the game floats units** (a player, GitHub issue #265: "is there a navigable path directly up? ... directly down? If not, don't apply"; the owner: "build it"): a grav lift's floor with no other one over or under it is a floor like any other, with no beam and no see-through pad, wherever its record is a lift elsewhere on the map. X-COM's UFOs and Cydonia keep all their lifts (0 such floors on the test saves); XPiratez's house from GitHub issue #216 had 24 of them.
- *Obliteration*: **the blood is the body's own colour** (the owner: "the sectoid has green blood so the overkill should sample particles from the dead body sprite! This should work also in 3D!"): the drops and the mist take the colours the unit's corpse picture shows that its living sprites don't (a soldier in armour red, a Sectoid, a Snakeman or a Chryssalid green, a Floater purple; the game's red where nothing tells), with *Voxel units* or without.
- *Voxel units*: **XPiratez's tank is a tank** (a player on the forum: the voxel look "a lot more goofy"; the owner: "specifically for that tank try to build a good boxy tank model with boxy turret and barrel - lets optimise this", and of the first, plain boxes: "too boxy! Now try to match the sprites and adapt the boxes a bit!!", "Turret is too high!", "bottom should not be filled with voxels as it is empty space"). Its sheet's name begins with PIR, as the gals' do, so it was carved and painted as a gal: a lumpy purple loaf.
