The unit table has a word for the rule alone, `arms: own` (`Routine::arms` bit 4), and an entry giving it to units named `HUMAN`: the mod's human race, 74 kinds of unit. Checked on four of them from a battle (hooligan, a civilian, a tourist, a woman in a robe), before and after from six sides, and the hooligan in the game: the white is off the arms. Not made general: applied to every body drawn in parts it repaints cubes on 119 of 120 test bodies (a Snakeman's 1667 of 17143, a Sectoid's 591 of 6465), and units not named stay as they are (280 test bodies cube for cube the same).
- *Voxel units*: **what a unit holds is not hit; a person's arms are painted by their own drawing** (a player, GitHub issues #198 and #219). #198 ("Kinda odd to hit a man shotguns barrel and he bleeds out"; the owner: "Yesyes"): a voxel body's hit shape (`makeHits`, what a shot meets with *Full sprite hitboxes*) no longer holds the cubes of the items in its hands: a shot at the barrel goes on, into the body behind it or past. A soldier with a rifle: 4936 cells a shot can meet, now 4694; a body holding nothing: the same. Units drawn as sprites are hit on their picture as before, the drawn weapon with it. #219 ("Arms should probably pull colors from front and back only, to prevent interference from torso ... hands wrapped in shirt fabric"): on a person (a unit the table names `humanoid: standard`) an arm's cube takes its pixel of the arm's own front or back drawing, and where that has none, the nearest pixel that drawing has (`ownColour`, `drawnNear`); it took whatever part was drawn on top there, the torso's shirt or straps on a hand. Only named people: every other unit's arms are as they were (280 test bodies cube for cube the same).
- *Voxel units*: **a person's head: the face from the front drawing, all the rest hair** (the owner, of the female civilian: "bald", "Correct colours and keep only face from frontal. The rest of the head is hair!"). A body is painted by its front and back drawings, each cube by the one it lies nearer: on a head that gave the whole front half to the front drawing, so the face's skin ran back to the ears and up over the crown, on a head made fuller than drawn. Now, on the head of a person (a unit the table names `humanoid: standard`; the head begins over the neck the standard humanoid found), only the cubes turned to the front within 53 degrees are the face, painted by the front drawing as drawn, its fringe with it; every other cube of the head is the back drawing's: thrown straight through at its own height on the sides and the back, along the slant on the crown, and where the head is fuller than that drawing, the nearest pixel it has (`FaceTurn`, `HairReach`, `drawnNear` in makeModel). Counted on the female civilian: face-coloured cubes reached 3.5 voxels back from the front of the head, now 1.5; none on the back half. Looked at from eight sides, against experimental 1: both civilians, three soldiers (hair and helmets), the aquanaut, a TFTD civilian; in the game, both civilians. Units not named: cube for cube as before (280 test bodies). The sheets' viewer had two faults of its own, found here: far cubes drawn over near ones, and gaps between cubes at a large scale, through which the far side showed (some of the speckle on earlier sheets was the viewer's).
- **A house's floor is no glass** (a player, GitHub issue #216: "Floor of house is glass??", with a save of an XPiratez safehouse). The upper storey's floor there is a record with no voxels at all that the mod flags as a grav lift, laid over a whole room. A floor without voxels that lies flat is drawn solid (the pool's water of #142), but for a lift's pad, which stays see-through so that who is over or under it is seen: so the room's floor was a pane one looked through, onto the table downstairs from above and into the bedroom from below; and with *Parts without shape* off it was not there at all, a pit and the open sky. Now a grav lift's floor is a lift only where it is one: where, somewhere on the map, a tile it floors has another grav lift's floor directly over or under it, as the game floats a unit only from one to another (`FirstPersonView::liftInUse`, found once a record for the battle). One that never has is a floor like any other. And a floor lying flat that units stand on is drawn with *Parts without shape* off too (it is the tile's floor; never a hole standing in for missing drawing). Checked on the player's save: the floor solid from above and below, with the setting on and off; the suite's lift, cage and ghost steps (40, 104, 151, 152, 161) pass alone.
- *Voxel units*: **a shield's flash shows on a voxel body** (a player, GitHub issue #214: "shield flicker, visual tell for units with sheids (star gods in piratez) doesn't properly engage on voxel models"). The big mods flash a shielded unit's sprite by a script that recolours it as it is drawn (XPiratez, The X-Com Files: 4 frames in every 32 of the battle's animation, each unit at its own time, in its armor's flash colour); a hit's blink is another such script. A body is made once and showed none of it. Now, each frame of the animation, a unit's script is asked what it makes of every palette colour (`VoxelUnits::recolourTable`: a strip of the 255 colours drawn as the game draws the unit's torso), now and at the frame the body was made at; where the two differ the body's cubes are drawn in the colours of now, on both renderers (`FirstPersonView::bodyRecolour`, `recordVoxelUnit`; not what the unit holds, nor a body going down). And a body is made at a frame where that recolouring is at rest (`VoxelUnits::restFrame`: the first frame that is as 8 and 16 frames later): before, a unit whose flash was on at the animation's first frame had a body in the flash's colours for good (the test's shielded soldier was one). Nothing changes for a unit whose mod recolours nothing, or always the same (hair and skin). Checked in XPiratez: a shielded soldier's body flashes its armor's colour on frames 31, 0, 1 and 2 of each 32, five screenshots in thirty.
- **Fires burn as far as the view reaches** (a player, GitHub issue #212: "pretty fire effect turns off with over 22 tiles in distance. I wouldn't mind if a lower fidelity option was used ... currently they appear to be extinguished"). A burning tile's and a burning unit's flames (particles) stopped 20 tiles from the eye, across the map. Now they go on to the view's distance: past 20 tiles with fewer flames (20 tiles over the distance, three tenths at least), each a size bigger, and without the unit's sparks and smoke and the tile's wisps (`FlamesNear`, `FlamesFarLeast` in FirstPersonViewEffects.cpp). Checked: a burning soldier seen from 24 tiles has its flames.
- **Two settings for the tactical map** (a player, GitHub issues #215 and #217; the owner: "fix github issues #212-217"). *Tactical map from the isometric view* (View, NO by default): the game's map button and key open the tactical map from the isometric battlescape too (`FirstPersonMode::openTacticalMap`: the squad's list from `squadList`, no view cone of the eye, the same soldier line and camera). *Tactical map: wheel changes level* (View, NO by default): the wheel goes a level up or down and Ctrl with the wheel zooms, the other way round from what it does without (`FirstPersonMapState::handle`); the four buttons' tips name the wheel as the setting has it. Both off: the isometric game stays the game's own unless asked, and the owner had ruled the wheel zooms ("the mouse wheel should not scroll levels of the map up down but zoom the map in/out"). Checked in a battle: the map opened from the isometric view, the wheel from level 1 to 2, Ctrl and the wheel zoomed, Escape back to the isometric map.
- *Voxel units*: **people are made the standard humanoid; every other unit is as it was** (the owner, of the round before, which had made every body on two legs so: "For humanoids i ment only people!", "Leave old units as they were before!", "Sectoids leave as they were in previous release!", "Make fixes on per unit basis always", and of the bodies since the humanoid rounds: "something is wrong now with voxel models if you compare to experimental 1 release"). The unit table has a new word, `humanoid: standard`, and one entry with it: X-COM's soldiers in every armour (the soldiers' type `STR_SOLDIER`, a whole name: `STR_SECTOID_SOLDIER` is no person) and the games' civilians (`MALE_CIVILIAN`, `FEMALE_CIVILIAN`, TFTD's `SAILOR_CIVILIAN` and `DOCKER_CIVILIAN`). Only a unit named so is cut by its front, back and side drawings alone, holds no item if it is drawn on one frame, and is made the standard humanoid (the entries below, which had these for every body). Every other body is built as before those rounds: of 334 test bodies of UFO, TFTD, XPiratez and The X-Com Files, unnamed, every one is cube for cube what the builder made before them (Sectoid, Muton, Floater, Snakeman as in experimental 5; the zombie, the Ethereal and the other one-tile bodies on one frame with their colours as before experimental 5's blotches). The standard humanoid itself (`standardBody`): the body made from the drawings is made again as a humanoid is built, to that body's own measures. Every part is taken apart into its sections (a piece at a height, an oval as wide and as deep as the piece; a piece that is two joined by a thin bridge is two; a ragged piece stays as it is) and the sections are smoothed along each limb: a limb is a round tube, the trunk a little square. The head is a full egg: its widest section's oval, round above it to the crown and below it to the neck (ears, horns, a plume above the crown stay as drawn; what is far wider than high over the neck is no head, and such a body gets no neck either). There is a neck, where the body is thinnest between head and shoulders (no lower than where the arms begin; where a head runs straight into the shoulders, at its foot): 0.6 of the head thick at most, a voxel long, the shoulders sloping away from it. A waist a tenth in. An arm with bones is its bones: a shoulder joined to the trunk, an upper arm, a forearm tapering to the wrist, a hand. Each leg goes straight down from its ankle to a foot: a sole on the floor from heel to toe, 0.13 of the body's height long whatever the drawing's shoe, pointing ahead with the toes out 40 degrees at most (the owner, of the first, which were as long as drawn: "Civilian has large feet correct!"). Sizes, places and the pose stay the drawings'. A person is painted by its front and back drawings, as every body drawn in parts always was: painted by whichever of the eight drawings each cube was turned to (the round before), a chest was speckled with its sides' pixels and a woman's hair was a thin cap on a face (the owner: "bald because face colour is taken to the hair"). Of the named, not made so: a body on one frame that shows no two legs, one drawn leaning, a kneeling body's legs. Measured on the 35 people of the two games' test battles: outline in common with their drawings 0.749 to 0.744, their pixels outside the drawings 26.8% to 23.2%, the drawings' pixels they miss 5.5% to 8.5%, cubes 9236 to 8492 a body; the builder takes as long as before (0.2 s a body). The frog's rule has its own word now, `sculpt: sides` (it was `sculpt: frontal` for a big walker, which XPiratez's named big units have too: one of them had changed with it). Each body also knows whether it kneels (`Routine::kneeling`). Tried first for the head: a plain egg (bald, see above); for the form: every section an oval, smoothed a voxel along (heads ran into the shoulders, bent arms straightened, the feet were gone); for the paint: the side drawing on the flanks only (stripes of the arms' skin down the legs' edges, seen from the front).
- *Voxel units*: **a big frog is a frog** (the owner: "frog is awful/ we must revisit its generator! (It looks like a green blob)"). A big walking creature (two tiles by two) is what its eight drawings leave of a block, one of them forgiven: for The X-Com Files' Bullfrog that was a lump with no legs to tell and no gap under its belly. A big walker the unit table names `sculpt: sides` is now cut by its four drawings from front, back and sides alone, strictly, and rounded as it stands (at each height a cell stays where it lies within the oval the body's stretches through it span, across and along: a leg a round post, a trunk an oval; no quarter drawing is asked), and each of its cubes is painted by the drawing it is turned to, the front, back and sides first, as a humanoid's. The table names frogs and toads. From its front, back and sides the Bullfrog has its drawings' outline, legs and belly; seen from between it is rounder than drawn. Its outline in common with its eight drawings 0.365 to 0.374, its pixels outside them 55% to 48%. By name only: UFO's Sectopod and Reaper made so agree less with their drawings (0.51 to 0.45 and 0.47 to 0.43: fatter from the quarters), and are as they were. Tried: all eight drawings strictly (a frog from every side, a sixth too small: over half its drawings' pixels missed), and with the outlines grown (a lump again).
- *Voxel units*: **a humanoid is its front and side drawings; from its side it shows its side drawing; a unit on one frame holds nothing** (the owner, of a civilian: "From the side it looks awful. Just create a model from frontal and side and that is it. You can ignore the other positioning!", "use only frontal sprite and back look and the front and left for side depth / profile. Diagonal only for colouring and not for shape!!", "floating pistol: In some sprites means that pistol should be removed altogether!"). **Shape.** A body on one frame that is one tile big (a civilian, an Ethereal, a zombie, a Celatid; no animal and no thin thing) is its slices, each row of its front drawing as deep as its side drawing's row, cut by the four drawings from its front, its back and its two sides alone, strictly. What a quarter drawing has or hasn't changes nothing of its shape. Nothing lower than two voxels is cut by the back drawing: a toe is behind the leg from there. The torso of a body drawn in parts is cut the same way. **Limbs drawn as parts of their own are still cut by all eight drawings**, two forgiven: an arm laid across the chest on a weapon slants through the body, and its front and side drawings alone make a plate of it; cut by the four only, limbs came out a fifth fatter (a body's pixels outside its drawings 24.7% to 28.7% on 74 bodies), and cut by them strictly a soldier's left arm lost a third and a Skeleton its feet. **Colours.** Every cube of a humanoid takes the colour of the drawing its outward side is turned to: the front's, the back's or a side's first, a quarter drawing's where none of those has it there; thrown straight through onto it, where that drawing sees it. Painted by front and back alone, its flanks were those two drawings' edges run through it in streaks. With all eight drawings counting alike a head had a face half way round it (a quarter drawing shows the face too), and a woman's hair framed nothing from the front. A cell made for a pixel it doesn't stand behind (a toe laid on the floor, a shoe, a shin carried on) has that pixel's colour: read where they stand, a civilian's shoes were its shins' colour. Big bodies on one frame and animals are painted as before. **No held item on a body on one frame that is one tile big**: its drawing has no hands on an item, so the item hung in the air before it (the game's map draws it so too). An Ethereal shows no weapon either. Measured: a civilian's feet (the lowest three rows of its drawings) covered 65% to 83%, a woman's 64% to 87%, their drawings 97% and 98%; the 74 bodies as before (0.711 of outline in common, 24.7% outside). Seen in the game from six sides: both civilians, the Ethereal. A developer's sheet (`tools/voxel_units`, not in the public tree) shows each body's eight drawings and under each the one built body turned to that direction, on the sprite's own pixel grid. *(Since the entry above: all of this only for the people the unit table names, and the painting by the drawing each cube is turned to taken back.)*
- *Voxel units*: **a one-tile creature on one frame is painted by its front and back again** (the owner, of a civilian: "something is very wrong with this model and its colors"). The first round on GitHub issue #211 had every body drawn on one frame take each cube's colour from the drawing that faces it, for a big frog whose front drawing ran along its long sides in streaks. For a body one tile big that was wrong: a hand drawn creature's eight drawings don't shade it alike, nor lie on one solid, so a civilian's white shirt was blotched with its arms' skin and shadow, its shorts with its hands, its face a plain patch; a Celatid was striped, a Silacoid speckled, an Imp's eyes gone. Now only big bodies on one frame (two tiles by two: a Bullfrog, a Cacodemon) are painted cube by cube from all eight; a one-tile one is painted by its front and back drawings, as a human is and as it was before. Seen in the game from six sides: a civilian, an Ethereal, a Celatid, a Silacoid, the Baron of Hell, the Imp (its red eyes back). **A held item on a unit drawn on one frame** stands where the game's own map draws it: there is no drawing of such a unit's arms raised, so a civilian given a pistol (a test save's; the game's civilians carry nothing) has it at its shoulder with its arms down, on the map and in 3D alike.
- *Voxel units*: **feet, a head and horns for creatures on one frame; one strut a leg; a rat's tail** (the owner, of what the rounds before left: "Make better", and "Work on these and fix": feet that end high, the Baron's ragged outline and faint horns, the Chupacabra's struts, the dog's 5% of height, the rat's tail). **A foot goes on along the floor as its side drawing has it.** A body on one frame is made row by row from its front drawing, a row a height; a side drawing has the near foot lower than that and a shoe's length where the front has an ankle, so a civilian's legs ended as posts. Now each place across a body that stands on legs takes the side drawing that has it near; the drawn pixels joined to where its lowest cells land, within 2.5 voxels and no higher than 2, are its foot, and get cells there. The lowest three rows of the drawings covered: UFO's civilians 49% to 65%, XPiratez's Imp 42% to 64%, its Baron of Hell 37% to 50%; the 22 bodies on one frame 64% to 68%. **An upright creature the cut leaves headless is read level as well.** Some creatures are drawn as a thing standing is looked at, not as the views' slant would show it: a head held forward no lower from the front than from behind. Cut along the slant alone the Baron and the Imp, both drawn leaning, had no head from the front, the Baron's horns and half an arm gone. Where under three quarters of the top of the front drawing is left, the cut is made again with a drawing having a cell by either reading: the Baron's head shown from the front 39% to 77%, the Imp's 48% to 88%; the Baron has its horns and both hands. They agree less with the drawings along the slant (0.705 to 0.687 and 0.720 to 0.704 of outline in common) and more seen level (0.681 to 0.713, 0.718 to 0.736). **A limb doesn't go on along a sliver**: a hand held before a thigh went on down the thigh's edge to the floor, a stick; a stretch is carried on only where it is half or more of the drawing's own stretch there. **The skin's pits are filled** on a body cut from eight drawings: an empty cell with five of its six sides against the body. **A Skeleton has its toes again**: the round before dropped a body's smallest pieces as specks, and they were its toes, drawn a dot each (its feet 85% covered, 66% in that round, 85% now); the smallest pieces are joined as the rest are. **One strut a leg**: a stroke of a long body's side drawing stood on every bar its frontal drawing has at that height; now it keeps to one bar a side, the nearest the one it had a row above. The Chupacabra went from 11,145 cubes to 9,006 and from 1.56 of its sprite's pixels on screen to 1.45, its sprite's size across and up (1.05, 1.00). **A rat has its tail**: the belly is raised only between the first leg and the last; what lies low behind the hind legs went with it. **The dog's height**: no change. From 6 tiles it is 1.01 to 1.03 of its sprite's (1.05 from 2.4): a body has breadth and is looked down on, a sprite's card has none; it is 15.8 voxels tall, drawn 16 rows above the floor. Seen in the game: the Baron with horns, hands and hooves from six sides, the Imp on two feet, a civilian's shoes, the rat's tail, the Chupacabra on four legs. Measured on the 74 bodies: the drawings' pixels a body misses 12.1%, pixels outside them 24.7% (12.2% and 24.9%). **Tried and dropped**: the level reading for every upright body (a civilian a tenth fatter, nothing gained); a cut forgiving near the floor (every creature fatter there); feet from twice the zone and reach (plates twice a shoe's length); smoothing by a cell's 26 neighbours (strong enough to tell, it ate what is a pixel thick). **Still short**: feet of creatures on one frame, 68%; the Chupacabra's bulk; the Baron's colours, speckled as its drawings are.
- *Sky*: **the stars stay points overhead** (a player: looking up at a starry sky shows "warp speed"; the owner: "Can we fix this elegantly?"). The stars are cells of the sky, a few in a thousand lit. The cells were laid out by compass bearing and by height above the horizon, as many to the degree of bearing at every height: towards the zenith, where the bearings run together, they narrowed to slivers all pointing at it, and the stars there were streaks towards one point. Now the sky is in rings by height, and each ring has as many cells as go round it at its height: a cell is as near square straight overhead as at the horizon. The same on both renderers and for every sky with stars (night, Mars by night, space, orbit, the Moon): the zenith drawn by the CPU and by the card differs in 0 of 702,500 pixels. The stars stand in other places than before.
- *Voxel units*: **a long body is as long as its drawings have it** (the owner: "The dog and cobra should not be too large! Take care on the size and keep original sprite sizes approximately"). Measured in the game, each as a voxel body and as its own sprite from the same places (the developer's camera, the unit alone, then no unit: the pixels that change are its own): the cobra was its sprites' size (as wide on average, 0.96 as tall), the dog 7% wider than its sprite from the side and 13% from a quarter. A long body is its side drawing given width, so it was as long as that drawing is wide, and the drawings from the quarters, drawn by hand, have most animals shorter: the dog is 28 pixels long from the side, 19.8 voxels, and 18 to 21 wide from the quarters, where a body that long would be 22. Now the six drawings that show a body's length each have a say: the body is made at its side drawing's length, its width on each of the six is measured as the drawing's is, and it is made again at the length (three quarters of the side drawing's or more) that brings the six nearest. The dog comes out 18.2 voxels long (0.92): 0.99 as wide as its sprite from the side, 1.04 from a quarter, 1.05 as tall. The cobra, drawn as long from every side, stays 19.0; the Chupacabra is 20.5 (21.5 before). The Chupacabra is its sprite's size across and up (1.05, 0.96) but draws half as many pixels again: more struts than it has legs, as said below. The developer's camera takes a fourth number 2 (no unit drawn) and a fifth, the view's pitch in degrees (90: straight up, for the sky).
- *Voxel units*: **animals shaped by two drawings, no limb a hair off its body** (the owner, of the round before: "dog needs to be much more elegant", "Cobra missing body - disjoined body", and "re-check missing limb humanoids report again"). **A long body is round**: its side drawing's stretch through a pixel, up and down or along the body, whichever is the shorter, is a round thing's thickness there, and a round bar lying is drawn 1.22 times as thick as it is (the drawings look down on it). It was as far across as its outline was near, a slab with its edges cut. **An animal's thick parts are as broad as its frontal drawings have them** at that height (an animal: a long body a mod's rules name a beast): the front drawing for its front half, the back drawing for its hind half; a trunk as its broadest row there, a head on a neck row by row. A dog's head was as broad as its chest and is 4.2 voxels on a chest of 5.2, as drawn. A frontal drawing taller than the side one by the body's length is read slanted (a rat, drawn with its back rising behind its head). **Ears stand as two**: a thin part of the front half the front drawing has in two stretches stands on both. **Each leg is a bar where the frontal drawing has it at that height, on its own side**: the side drawing has a pair's two legs as two strokes, the near one ending lower; both stood on both sides, and each leg was twice as thick as drawn; kept to all the columns of the drawing's lowest rows, a Chupacabra's legs spread wide were walls. **An animal's face is its front drawing's**: what looks ahead with nothing of the body before it takes that drawing's pixel (the same at the back); the rest its side drawing's, the same on both flanks (the reporter, of the dog's and the frog's faces: "I would hope it would prioritize there ... to give a symetrical face"). **A long body with no legs has none**: one that is no beast by name, a snake, had its lowest rows taken for legs and kept to the columns of legs it hasn't got: the cobra lay in three. It is one piece, 19.0 voxels long (drawn 19.1), 3.2 across. **No limb stands a hair off its body.** Each part of a body is made on its own and stays a quarter of a voxel inside its drawn edge, so an arm drawn against a shoulder stood a cell off it all round: of the 100 bodies of the reporter's five saves and the UFO test zoo, 19 had a limb apart (a Skeleton's two arms and its toes, a soldier's arm raised to aim, a Sectoid's arms) and 55 more a speck. Now pieces two cells apart or less have the cells between filled, all along; one still apart gets a bridge three cells thick, if it is three voxels off or nearer; specks under eight cells go. None of the 100 has a piece apart; the Chupacabra, in eight pieces, is one. **The limbs, checked again**, on the 95 one-tile bodies: of the 73 drawn in parts every arm and leg's own drawings are covered 80% or more by the body (95% on average, half a pixel allowed), their feet's lowest three rows 93%. Seen in the game, each alone (the developer's camera takes a fourth number, 1: the other units are not drawn): the Skeleton with both arms and its toes, the Imp on two legs from six sides, the Baron with both arms and legs, a soldier's shoes out in front. **Still short**: creatures on one frame have the lowest three rows of their drawings covered 64% only (the Baron 37%, the Imp 42%, an Ethereal 48%): their frontal drawings stand lower than their side drawings allow, and a body can't be both; the Baron's outline is ragged and its horns faint; the Chupacabra shows more struts than it has legs (each stroke stands on every bar of its height). Measured as before on the 74 bodies: the drawings' pixels a body misses 12.2% (12.4% the round before), pixels outside them 24.9% (23.7%). The UFO test zoo's picture differs from the round before's in 0.28% of its pixels.
- *Voxel units*: **long bodies whole, both legs, big units' fine detail** (GitHub issue #211 again; the owner, of what the first round left: "Go and improve all these isues": the cobra more than half missing, the Chupacabra's legs partial, the dog thicker than drawn, horns and spikes, the Baron's arm and the Imp's leg not seen in the game). **A long body has its width from its own side drawing.** A body longer than wide on one frame (an animal the unit table names; and now any thin thing, a snake) is its right side's drawing, read level, and was cut to width by the other seven: which for a thing drawn by hand in eight poses took its every thin end. It is now as far across, each way, as its side drawing is deep inside its own outline there (a tail a pixel thin is a pixel thick, a barrel of a body as thick as it is high), no wider than its frontal drawing, and the other drawings cut nothing; each leg is a bar where a frontal drawing has it; what the side drawing has below the floor's row lies on the floor. The X-Com Files' dog was 12.5 voxels long and 12.2 high and is 19.8 and 15.8 (drawn 19.8 and 17): its snout, ears, tail and legs are there; its cobra was 6.5 long and 4.2 high and is 18.5 and 11.8 (drawn 19.1 and 16), a snake with its hood up; its Chupacabra was 9.8 long and is 21.5 (drawn 21.9), on four thin legs. **A limb that ends early goes on down** on a body drawn on one frame that is no long one: where the front drawing has pixels under the body's and nothing of the body lands on them, the body's cells behind the row above are moved a row down (XPiratez's Imp has its second shin; "Imps have 1 leg"). **A big unit keeps its fine detail** in its shape (the Cacodemon's horn tips): it was left out for a view's own pieces to show, which one body for every side hasn't got. Seen in the game with a developer's camera (`OPENXCOM3D_WATCH_UNIT=<file>`, the file holding a unit's id, degrees round from its front and tiles away): the cobra, the dog, the Chupacabra, the Imp with two legs, the Baron with both arms, the Cacodemon off the floor with its face on its front only. Measured as before on the reporter's 74 bodies: the drawings' pixels a body misses, 16.7% at first and 13.2% after the first round, are 12.4%; pixels outside the drawings 23.7% (16.8% at first). The long bodies agree less with all eight drawings than before (dog 0.68 to 0.62 of outline in common, Chupacabra 0.49 to 0.43): they follow one drawing whole, where they followed eight in part. **Tried and dropped**: a looser cut for the animals (a slab: over half their pixels outside the drawings); limbs going on up the drawing as well, for the tops of heads and for horns (a tower on every head: a row up the front drawing is as likely further back as higher); a slice a missing row, as deep as all above it (a brim round a head). Still rough: horns and spikes on creatures one tile big; a long body from its front is a narrow column, its face its side drawing's.
- *Voxel units*: **feet, thin limbs, what lies on the ground, hovering units, creatures' colours** (GitHub issue #211, "voxel drawings errors": "feet don't exist in voxel land, meaning we are all canonically peg leg piratez"; a cobra "slightly inside the ground"; frog and dog faces "as if the front decided to adopt textures from the left"; Chupacabras with "no legs"; a Cacodemon "right against the floor when hitbox (and doom behavior) should indicate it hovering slightly"; the owner: "fix and generalise to the whole game"). Five rules, for every unit, none by name. **Thin limbs are kept**: a limb's slices are cut by its eight drawings' outlines, and a limb a pixel or two thin (a skeleton's shin, a spider's leg) is drawn a pixel here or there from frame to frame, so the outlines as drawn cut it away: The X-Com Files' Skeleton ended in stumps 3.4 voxels above the floor, its Chupacabra 4.9. The outlines are now grown by half a sprite's pixel for limbs, and for a body on one frame whose drawings are thin (under 0.42 of their pixels have a neighbour on all four sides: the cobra, the Chupacabra, the bats). **What a front drawing has below the floor's row lies ahead on the ground**: the drawings look down a slant, and rows below the floor's were read as heights under it, where they sank into the floor or were cut off; they are now a plate on the floor, a row down 1.41 voxels further ahead: toes, a snake's coils. **Nothing of such a body is under the floor.** **A unit the game holds off the ground is lifted**: its float height is where its hit box starts; a body whose drawings reach lower is raised until its lowest cube is there (the Cacodemon), for the picture and for *Full sprite hitboxes* alike; one whose drawings hold it up already (a Floater) stays as drawn. **A creature on one frame is painted by the drawing that faces each cube**, as animals and turrets were: thrown straight through from the front and the back, a frog's skin ran along its sides in streaks and a Cacodemon's face showed on its flanks; a body drawn in parts (a human's) stays painted by its front and back. Measured on the 74 bodies of the reporter's five saves, built again offline and held against their drawings from all eight directions: the drawings' pixels a body misses fell from 16.7% to 13.2% (Skeleton 24% to 15%, cobra 78% to 57% and no cube under the floor where 156 were, bat 59% to 42%, Chupacabra 45% to 31%); the bodies' pixels outside their drawings rose from 16.8% to 21.2%, the limbs' half pixel. **Tried and dropped**: adding whatever all eight drawings agree is there and the body lacks. For thin things they don't agree: the cobra is another squiggle in every direction, and no place in space is inside six of its eight outlines; forgiving a pixel made it whole and twice too fat. Still rough: thin creatures (the cobra's length, the Chupacabra's legs) are partly missing, horns and spikes are too thin to become cubes, a dog's body is thicker than drawn.
- **Every animated tile part seen on the screen: the arena** (the owner: "really scrutinize all animated tiles animate"). The check of the round before read the models; this one looks at the picture. A developer's arena, started with `OPENXCOM3D_ANIMATION_ARENA=<path>`: where a battle is loaded, a patch of its map is cleared from the ground up, floored with one of the map's own still floors and put in daylight, and a camera is fixed above it. Every animated record of every tile set the game has (sliding doors apart) is laid on it twelve at a time, with a thirteenth spot left empty; a script asks for batch after batch, takes nine pictures of the screen over one whole cycle of the animation, and counts the pixels that change in the box round each part's tile, with the empty spot telling what else changes there. Parts that do not clearly change are laid again three at a time, far apart, out of each other's light. Run with the default settings on both renderers: UFO 51 parts, TFTD 156, XPiratez 1474, The X-Com Files 1046. Clearly changing on both renderers at the first pass: 49, 141, 1310 and 903; after the retest all but 1, 5, 48 and 66 (and 11 more on one renderer only, at the threshold). Of those 120: 60 are records whose eight frame numbers are one picture, nothing to animate; 45 change under one hundredth of their art, a pixel or two (the twinkles on lava and swamp floors are above the floor's shape and not shown; a sprite a tile across a lava field for two pixels is not worth it); 4 change by hundreds of pixels beside a bright light in their batch; the other 11 change little or nothing: a light on the bottom rim of a thing or on a floor slab's side, which lies inside the floor's slab as the foot of every object does (XPiratez's `STATIONBITS` 4, `ACHURCH` 3, UFO's `UFO1` 6), and a few single lights on consoles. **What it found, now fixed**: the parts lying in the floor (two entries below), which showed nothing at all; TFTD's sparks drifting in the pyramid, art far above a plate in the floor, now a sprite; XPiratez's and The X-Com Files' hologram globes, a ring on the floor with a turning globe over it, now the ring laid and the globe a sprite (a sprite is given to low art made from its art too, where animated art floats over it); The X-Com Files' colony pedestal standing still with *UFO lights* on. A lone part of a set UFOs are built from is no UFO, so the arena shows it playing its frames, as with *UFO lights* off; inside a UFO with the setting on it is a steady model whose lights pulse, as before. Two wrong turns on the way, both the arena's own: the first map chosen for XPiratez was under water, where the whole floor ripples; and after the arena built the UFO parts' steady models again the card still held the old ones (in play they are built once a battle). With a file `<path>.save` holding a page's number, the arena instead lays the parts all over the cleared map, every other tile, and writes a save to walk through (the owner: "Make me also an arena save i can revisit with my own eyes").
- **Doom IDDQD turns the stats silver in TFTD too** (the owner: "IDDQD cheat colors all stats silver in UFO:enemy unknown but not in TFTD. Please fix so also in TFTD they turn silver and indicate god mode"). An invincible soldier's four stats, numbers and bars, go silver. TFTD's own interface draws every stat's number in a pale grey already, so silver on them told nothing, and gives each bar a border colour of its own, which stayed. Where the game's own numbers are as pale as silver, the stats are now a darker steel, and a bar's border goes with it where the interface sets one. UFO's look is as it was.
- **What lies in the floor shows: rubble, tufts, ponds, stumps** (found by the arena above). A wall or an object whose shape in the game's data is only the two bottom rows of voxels lies inside the floor it is on: the same voxels, and a ray meets the floor's first. So it showed only where it had no floor under it, which is nearly nowhere: the remains of a shot fence, a fallen beam, a tuft of grass, a pond, a stump with its log, a mound of snow, a scatter of stones, the wreckage round a crashed plane, TFTD's grates glowing in a deck. The map draws each of them over its floor. UFO has 38 such records, TFTD 69, XPiratez 408, The X-Com Files 304. Laid on a floor, 24 of UFO's showed as nothing, every one. With *Parts without shape* on at all they are now made from their art, as a part with no shape at all is when its art is low: what lies inside the floor's diamond is laid on the slab, what rises from it stands up as the map draws it, solid; in the game they stop nothing more than before. If the art is nowhere near the ground (TFTD's sparks drifting in a pyramid's air, over a plate in the floor) there is nothing to lay: where such art animates it is drawn as a sprite, where it doesn't the game's shape is kept. With the setting off nothing changes.
- **T'leth's pedestals move as shapes** (the owner, told that the pedestals of XPiratez's T'leth base changed only their paint from frame to frame: "then fix 3"). The game gives a tile part one shape for all eight frames of its animation, and the view built every frame from it: the pedestal's gold collar rising round its rod and the drum sinking into the slab were one lump in other colours. A part the mod's rules name under `turned` (see "Things that turn" in chapter 6) is now turned from each frame's art as on a lathe, round its own upright axis: at each height a disc as wide as the art is there. The art draws a round top as an ellipse, so, read row by row, a flat slab came out as a dome: a height gets a disc of its own only where the art is wider than the far rims of the discs below it would show (a slab filling the tile shows a diamond, which is allowed for), and a height those rims account for takes what stands on the disc, the next one found above. Voxels of a top that the art hides behind what stands on it (the far half of the collar's top, behind the rod) take the colour of the top's near half, across the axis. Measured on a T'leth pedestal, in eight pictures a third of a second apart: its outline differed by 606 pixels before (the cross-fade's dots) and by 8236 now; each frame's profile, read back, is the art's (the slab 3 to 6 voxels high, the drum up to 9 to 12, the collar up to 14 to 22, the rod alone in one frame). Named: TFTD's colony pedestal in its two sets, the same one in XPiratez's three and The X-Com Files' two. Any other part keeps the game's shape: a single drawing can't tell every shape, and a wrong guess would stand on the map. In a set a UFO is built from (The X-Com Files' colony) such a part stood still with *UFO lights* on, as a steady model; a named part plays its frames. Both renderers.
- ***Reflections* cost every pixel; now only the pixels that mirror** (the owner, told that the setting alone cost a test street 9 ms a frame: "First check 2! Optimise"). Measured on an RTX 4060 laptop at 1280×800: a street with a few windows in sight took 5.2 ms a frame without *Reflections* and 14.2 with it, though hardly a pixel showed a mirror. The cause is in how the driver builds the rays' program: each further place in it that walks a ray through the map (the sea's mirror, a window pane's) makes the whole program slower, used or not. With either of the two taken out it took 8.2 ms, with both 5.2; walking all three from one loop made it worse (20.4). So a frame with mirrors is now drawn in two goes. The first is the program without mirrors, as fast as ever, which also marks the pixels that want a mirror's ray (a pane of glass looked through, the sea); the second is the program with the mirrors, over the marked pixels only. The street: 6.5 ms. A port in TFTD: 5.5 against 13.3. A window filling a quarter of the view: 7.7 against 13.9. With *Surface relief* Strong as well, the street: 6.8 against 15.4. A frame without glass or sea is as before (5.3). The picture is the same: on every pixel that stands still (all but drifting clouds), one go and two goes agree to the last bit in four scenes with windows, on the desktop's OpenGL and built as OpenGL ES. The sea was looked at, not compared (its waves never stand still). In one go as before: with *Supersampling* (its further rays are another program again), in a headset with *VR foveation*, and on a card that won't draw the fifth picture the marks need. `OPENXCOM3D_GPU_ONE_GO_MIRRORS` draws in one go, for comparing. What is left over the 5.2 is the second go passing over every pixel to find the marked ones. Not tested: Windows, macOS, a Quest headset.
- **Every animated tile part checked: the ones that stood still now move** (the owner: "Test all animated world tiles so they actually animate in 3D"). A developer's check now lists every animated record of every tile set a game has: started with `OPENXCOM3D_ANIMATION_CHECK=<file>`, the game loads all the mod's terrain, UFO and craft sets where a battle begins, builds each animated record's eight frames as the view would with the settings that are on, and writes a verdict a record. Run on UFO (66 animated records), TFTD (192), XPiratez (1820) and The X-Com Files (1303), with *UFO lights* on and every part without shape shown. Sliding doors apart (their frames are their opening: 15, 37, 390, 294), three kinds did not animate. **Standing still**: the art changes where the game's shape for the part has no voxels, so every frame gave the same model: a torch's flame above its holder, a candelabra's candles, a hologram globe turning over its base, the aerial rising from the T'leth base's mound (XPiratez 73 records, The X-Com Files 26, TFTD 3, UFO 1). **Frozen as a UFO's part**: with *UFO lights* a part of a set a UFO is built from is a steady model whose lights pulse, and one without a light among its voxels showed its first frame for ever (17 and 29). **Not drawn at all**: no voxels and no ghost either, its art being nowhere its kind of ghost stands: bubbles, sea spray drawn as a "wall", a planet turning drawn as a "floor" (17 and 9). Now: (1) for a part that stands still in the view, the pieces of its art outside its shape that change from frame to frame are drawn as a sprite standing on the shape (a piece whole: the flame with the candle under it), held where the middle of the shape's top lands, in the tile's light, or unshaded if the part is a light; frame flows into frame as a flame's does. A part whose model already shows its animation is left as it is (a first version drew a sprite for every part with changing art beyond its shape: 697 of XPiratez's records, every tile of TFTD's sea). (2) A part without voxels that no ghost shows is drawn as its whole art the same way, standing where its middle column meets the floor. (3) A UFO's part without lights whose frames give different models plays its frames, as anywhere else; one whose models are all alike stays steady (it keeps its hull's trim) and gets the sprite for what changes beside it. With *Parts without shape* on lifts and ladders only, the sprites of parts that are lights; off, none. After: UFO 32 animate by their voxels, 18 by their UFO lights, 1 by a sprite; TFTD 127, 14, and 8 by sprites (5 of them flames, as before), 6 in some of their steps only; XPiratez 988, 279, 111 by sprites, 34 in some steps only; The X-Com Files 652, 273, 54, 18. Still standing: lava and swamp floors whose art changes by a pixel or two at an edge (XPiratez 18, The X-Com Files 12: under four pixels over the eight frames isn't worth a sprite on every tile of a field). Seen in battles: the mound's aerial in XPiratez's T'leth base rising and sinking (dark, as that palette has its colours), a hideout's candle with its flame where there was a bare stand, a UFO's hull unchanged. Cost in the T'leth base (39 such mounds on the map): the card's time for units and effects 0.39 ms against 0.32, the rays unchanged; where such parts are on the map is looked up once in 64 frames. A base and a UFO's inside without such parts are the published build's picture pixel for pixel. Not seen in a battle: a part drawn wholly as a sprite (no save of mine has one; it is drawn by the same code), and most of the about 140 kinds that now have a sprite, one by one. Not tested: Windows, macOS, a Quest headset.
- **Animated parts in motion no longer stand in dots** (the owner, on XPiratez's T'leth base: "some animated parts look bad ingame (3d) while lifts and some parts are superb. Examine and fix if a certain Environment tile has animations."). Animated terrain steps a frame about three times a second and cross-fades over the whole step, the next frame on a growing share of the pixels in a dot pattern: made for lights, which pulse softly that way instead of blinking. A part in motion fared badly: the base's pedestals (a gold collar rising and sinking on a grey rod), its steps folding flat, a floor hatch opening. Most of such a part is another colour from frame to frame, so it stood in a coarse speckle of gold and grey nearly all the time and never showed a clean picture; up to 16% of a pedestal's pixels were lone dots. The game's shape for the part is one lump for all its frames (the art covers 94 to 99% of the pedestal's 2180 voxels in every frame), so the motion is paint moving over it, in the map's view too; that stays. Two changes. In both renderers, where a voxel's colours in the two frames are far apart (another hue, or more than three shades of one) that spot goes over whole at the step's middle; near colours cross-fade as before, the dots there the palette's ordinary shading. A part whose animation is a light always cross-fades, whatever its colours: two pictures taking turns (a blinking lamp, a pulsing lift pad, the pods, the brain), or frames that keep their pattern of shades while the colours change (the shades of the pixels that change rise and fall together from frame to frame: a correlation of 0.7 or more; a dance floor's tiles going through eight hues have 1.00, TFTD's pyramid floors 0.94, the pedestals 0.21, the folding steps 0.59, the hatch under 0). That is what the cross-fade is for, and a first version without this rule would have flipped such floors hard three times a second. At three steps a second nothing changes back and forth three times a second either way. And on the graphics card in full colour with *Smooth light*, the two frames are mixed smoothly, near or far (on faces showing the part's own colours or its side picture; a face that borrowed its colour from a craft's mirror image or a wall's paint keeps the dots): no dots at all on the pedestal, in eight pictures a third of a second apart. Ghosts (lift beams, sparks) and UFO parts with *UFO lights* on are as they were. Checked: a base and a UFO's inside without animated parts are the published build's picture pixel for pixel, also built as OpenGL ES; the card's time is the same (5.3 ms and 14.2 ms in the two test scenes). Counted over the tile sets' pictures: of the animated parts with a shape and more than two pictures, 6 of UFO's 31, 38 of TFTD's 119 and 275 of XPiratez's 1070 have more than a tenth of their pixels far apart between frames (the Lightning's pedestal, the alien pods' screens, TFTD's base machinery); the polar sea, the consoles and most lit panels change by near colours and look as before. Not looked at one by one in the 3D view: those other parts.
- **Surface relief** (the owner: "Could we automatically generate normal maps from sprites and show a bit of topogeqphy on walls ufos etc.?"). New setting *Surface relief* (Off / Subtle / Strong; off by default; *Renderer* GPU; in the SCREEN group, and Off at every *Graphics detail* level). The world's walls, floors and hulls are blocks of little cubes, each with the colour of one pixel of the tile's drawing, and a face was lit by the way it points alone: a drawn brick or panel was paint on a flat face. With the setting on, the card reads the drawing as a height. A colour's brightness is its height (a new row of the colour tables); the four neighbours of a cube on its face give the slope there (the outermost cube a step nearer, level or a step deeper; across tiles too; on faces the map's drawing never shows, by where their colour was borrowed from: the part's side picture, a craft's mirror image, a wall's paint); and the slope tilts the face towards or away from each light: the faces' own cosine shading (the sun's by day, the sky's under clouds; at least a shade and a half of it, so it shows indoors and at night too; less in the sun's shadow), the lamps, both flashlights, the flares and the coloured lights. The tilt goes smoothly across a cube's face, from the step at its one edge to the step at the other: a first version that tilted each cube as a whole only gave it another flat shade, and read as noise. Nothing is stored and nothing is made ahead: it is four to a dozen look-ups a pixel. It fades out 12 to 22 tiles away, and wherever a pixel covers more than a third of a cube's face (far ground looked along): measured against a picture of four rays a pixel, relief on a street's and a desert's ground 5 to 30 tiles ahead made the one-ray picture 30 to 50% more unlike it, which is shimmer as one walks; with that fade, 1 to 7%. Subtle tilts a face by half the step, Strong by all of it; Strong makes walls drawn as smooth ramps of shades (inside a UFO, in a base) rippled. Undersides stay flat (they are one colour), ghosts, lights and the sea have none, units and items are not touched, and a door has none while it swings. The program with the relief in it is built when the card opens, if the setting is on, so the first frame doesn't wait for it; switched on in a battle, the CPU draws until the driver has built it. Two calls that find a mirrored or a painted face's colour, made again for each neighbour, doubled the whole frame's time (5.5 to 12 ms): now they leave where the colour came from, and the neighbours are plain look-ups. Measured on an RTX 4060 laptop at 1280×800, Strong against Off: a base indoors 5.4 ms a frame against 5.35; a street with *Reflections* off 5.2 against 5.2, with *Reflections* on 15.4 against 14.1 (9%: the program with both in it is slower as a whole). With the setting off the picture is the same as before, pixel for pixel (a base, a UFO's inside). It works in the palette's picture, without *Smooth light*, and built as OpenGL ES (what the Quest runs: 32 pixels of a million differ from the desktop's). Looked at in UFO, TFTD, The X-Com Files and XPiratez. Not tested: Windows, macOS, a Quest headset.
- **The first 3D frame's waiting is done where a battle begins** (the owner: "Can we move shader loading the first time into battle load? (players are confused as game freezes for 2s upon first click on 3D icon in battlescape)"). With *Renderer* on GPU the first frame the 3D view drew in a battle was slow: a driver finishes each program the first time something is drawn with it, the pictures the card draws into are made, and the whole map and the units' bodies are sent to the card (measured with the shaders already kept by the driver: a quarter of a second on a fast laptop; seconds on slower drivers). The card was already opened on the side from the battle's start; now that frame is drawn too, unseen, on the battle's first frame, while the screen before it (the loading box, the next turn's screen) still shows: through the eyes of the soldier the view will open on, with the view's own settings (`FirstPersonMode::primeView`). It waits for a card still being opened, 8 seconds at most (`PrimeCardWaitMs`), and with *Voxel units* for the bodies, a second at most (`PrimeBodiesMs`). A card ready only later (the very first run on a machine, when a driver builds the shaders for half a minute; the renderer switched in a battle) has its frame drawn ahead when it is ready, if the map still shows. A battle that starts in 3D has its first frames on the card. Where the wait can be seconds (the owner: "Sometimes 4-5s"; the first time in a run of the game, or a card still being opened), the screen says PREPARING THE 3D VIEW meanwhile, and the window keeps answering its system while the card is waited for. What a click can still cost is the card waking from idling, where it does (measured: up to 170 ms on a laptop's card after ten seconds on the map, every time, not only the first).
- **The tactical map** (the owner: "a better battlescape map for 3d ... full screen with unit numbers by our unit dots and with enemy class abbreviation (like Sec. for Sectoid). Also every dot should have a small 2d cone (green for our, red for enemy, blue for current selected unit) for where it is viewing ... should not have the sprite border of the original but should have small styled button to keep all original map functionality"). A new setting, *Tactical map* (on): the map opened from the 3D view fills the screen (see "View and camera" in chapter 6). All the game's map does is kept: the level up and down, the click that centres, the drag, OK, and the battle's own view left looking where the map did. Added: zoom, the selected soldier's cone following the 3D view's own direction, a click on a soldier to select them (a second click: their 3D view), a live helmet camera of the soldier pointed at or selected (the owner: "one can click on dot and see the units 3d view also?"), short weapon names under the dots ("short weapon labels under the dots that can be turned on/off"), the selected soldier's reach in blue ("a reach based on remaining TUs of the unit ... based on exact current AVAILABLE TUs": the tiles the game's pathfinding says they can reach, outlined), everything scaled with the screen as the 3D view's overlays are, the next soldier, units on other levels as rings marked above or below, names that keep clear of each other, a line telling who the pointer is on, the arrow keys. On the isometric map the game's own map screen opens as ever (`FirstPersonMapState`, `FirstPerson::tacticalMap`).
- **The tactical map: the wheel zooms, a help button, the soldier along the bottom** (the owner: "the mouse wheel should not scroll levels of the map up down but zoom the map in/out ... besided ok to the left should be a help buton that toggles the help as is now at the bottom. Otherwose at the bottom is soldier name, health, TU remaining and his/hers weapon - all in colored text as in hud"). The wheel makes the map bigger or smaller about the tile under the pointer; with Ctrl it changes the level, as it did alone. A **?** button left of OK (and F1) switches the help on and off: the line that says how the map is worked, or what the button under the pointer does, in as many rows as the screen needs, broken between sentences; it starts off. The line along the bottom is now the selected soldier's, or the unit's the pointer is on: number and name, health, time units, the weapon in hand worded as the 3D view's panel words it, in that panel's own colours (handed to the map by the 3D view: `Setup::nameColour` and the rest, `weaponWords`).
- *Voxel units*: **a unit drawn the same from every side keeps its shape, and animated units move** (GitHub issues #203, "abominations, sometimes, end up flat looking puddles ... and do not animate", and #210, "bats still dont flap ... other animated units also dont move"). The game draws some units with one picture whichever way they face (the Silacoid's, the Celatid's and the Bio-Drone's routines), and a body carved from eight drawings that are all the same keeps only what the drawing has on both sides of its middle: enough of a Celatid, a puddle of The X-Com Files' Abomination, whose bubbles sit off to one side. Such a unit, when its drawing is lopsided, is now that drawing given volume (see "Units by name"). And a body for each frame of a sprite's own animation is made for any unit that has one, not only the bats named in the unit table: it took a named unit with empty hands, and a real bat holds its bite, a built-in weapon the game never draws, so in a battle it never flapped. A Celatid and a Silacoid pulse as their sprites do. Units alike share their bodies whatever frame of the animation each was first seen at.
- *Voxel units*: **no hold-ups while bodies are made** (GitHub issue #204, "Lag spikes on movement dimension x ... unloading, and then rebuilding 3d models mid fight"). Before a body is made, or shared with a unit drawn alike, the unit's frames are gathered: a hundred drawings by the game's own drawer, about 3 ms on the game's thread. Only a body actually made counted against the frame's allowance, so a battle of 110 units alike gathered them all in one frame: a third of a second at the battle's start, and again whenever many units needed a body at once. Now two drawings (front and back) tell a unit like one already seen, which takes that one's body without the rest; what is gathered is spread over frames by the time it takes (2 ms a frame); the threads that make bodies run in spare time, behind the frame's own; and a unit showing nothing in its hands has one body for standing and aiming, not a second made at its first shot. That battle's start: 13 ms of gathering instead of 360.
- *Voxel units*: **arms outside a robe, sleeves that end where they do, the gun out of the stomach** (GitHub issue #207, "arms and arm accessories": "texture should probably be applied along length of limb, rather than height based? the dagon disciples have longer, ragged looking sleeves ... larger units ... have the gun imbedded in their stomach ... their arms are clearly outside robes"). Two things for the units the unit table names (`arms:`, set for The X-Com Files' Church of Dagon): the arms of a body carved as wide as its robe are moved out to its sides and lie over it; and an arm posed on a weapon takes its colours along its length from the drawing that shows it longest. And for every unit: what it holds is moved ahead until no more than a third of its length lies inside the body, and the arms reach for it there.
- *Voxel units*: **a shield faces the front** (GitHub issue #208: the combat shield is "slanted as if trying to slice with blade, rather than smack with front face ... sheild is very clearly with flat face outward in handobs"). An item standing in the hand stood with its picture's edge ahead, as a sword's. One the hand drawings show broad and solid from the front and the back and thin from the sides is a plate now: its face ahead, the hand behind its middle.
- *Voxel units*: **melee weapons as their hand drawings hold them** (GitHub issue #199, "Idea for weapons orientation : pull from handobs?": "most melees are a bit janked, like axes held inside arms, knives being missing ... Works REALLY well for guns"). A melee weapon points the way its eight hand drawings draw it (the direction that fits them best), where that is not the way a gun is held. A blade standing in the hand is turned half way between front and side unless its side drawings are clearly the broader ones: edge on to the front, a knife was one cube thin there and as good as missing. A staff or a spear drawn from corner to corner in its inventory picture is laid straight along the way it is held. Guns are held as before.
- *Voxel units*: **a tank's turret has a dome and a barrel** (GitHub issue #201: "turrets could use better definition. Rocket tanks should roughly keep shape of rocket pods on sides ... tank barrel for cannon / laser ... should have a protruding barrel"). The turret was a plain block. It is now what its drawings' outlines leave, no higher over each place than its front and side drawings show there, so the dome rises above what stands beside it; and where the side drawings run out ahead of it thin, a round bar as long, as high up and as thick as drawn: the barrel. And a tank stands as high as the game's units do (the same issue: "height should, overall, reflect hitbox. Hull of main chasis should be, roughly kneel height, and turret maxing out on normal unit height. Tanks are often used as mobile cover"; the owner: "follow reporter instructions"): its hull is stretched so the deck is at a kneeling soldier's height (14 voxels in the games themselves, read from the first kind of soldier in the rules) and its turret so the top is 20 voxels up (the owner: "make tank only 20 voxels tall"; a standing soldier is 22), by whole layers of cubes, each with the colours it was painted with as drawn. By its drawings alone a tank was about 12 voxels high. It is the one body made higher than its sprite (see "in every pose a body is as its sprite is" below): hover tanks too, their hulls from where they float.
- **A unit's head stays under the floor above it** (GitHub issue #206, "head stuck in roof"). A unit's picture, and its voxel body, is often taller than a level: a hat, a helmet's crest, a unit on a raised floor. The map draws the floor above over it; in 3D its top stood out of the roof for anyone up there to see. A unit's picture and body are cut off at the underside of the floor above its tile, where there is one (both renderers).
- *Doom mode*, IDCLIP: **a burst fired from the air is a whole burst** (GitHub issue #206: "smg in hand fires only once if you fly high up and fire down"). The game ends a burst after its first round when the shooter has no floor under it and cannot fly. A soldier IDCLIP holds in the air fires on as one that flies does.
- *Doom mode*, IDCLIP: **inside a solid block the block is not in the way** (GitHub issue #209: "when inside a SOLID block, draw everything invisible until next opening to open air, drawn as ghost voxels for that wall / ceiling / floor, and rest beyond solid, ala gzdoom engine"). Only the parts the eye itself was in were left out of the picture; inside a hill or thick foliage the next tile's block filled it. Now the block is found (the tiles solid at their middles that are joined to the eye's, up to 10 tiles away): its inner tiles' objects are left out with the walls and floors between them, and its skin where it meets the open air (the outer tiles' objects, the walls on its sides, the floor above it) shows as ghosts, seen through as any part without shape. Beyond that everything is as ever.
- *Akimbo*: **a spray burst from two guns is two bursts** (GitHub issue #205: "Akimbo spray waypoints doesn't respect akimbo quantity ... fires often 6 or 7 shots instead of the intended 12"). In 3D the click that begins marking a spray's points set the pair on the game's shot and took it off again, so the click that fires the burst came without it and the chosen gun sprayed alone. And with the two guns taking turns, each gun's round took a point off the spray's list, which ran out after one gun's worth. Both guns now sweep the same points, each its full burst, at once or in turn; a spray begun on the map with akimbo marked there fires both guns too.
- *Voxel units*: **a bat flaps** (GitHub issue #195, "Bats are actually frisbys": the bat "looks exactly like that and never flaps"). X-Com Files' shadowbat and XPiratez's vampire bat are animated by a script of their mod: as they hover, their sprite goes through 8 drawings of the wings. The body was made from the one drawn at that moment, and stayed so. For the units the unit table names (`animation: sprite`, the bats), a unit whose standing drawing changes outline with the battle's animation frame has a body for each of the 8 frames, each made from that frame's own 8 drawings, shown by the frame's number as the sprite is; what a shot meets with it (`VoxelUnitModel::frames`, `UnitSprite::setAnimationFrame`).
- *Voxel units*: **a sword held upright is a sword, in a hand on an arm** (GitHub issue #196, "Amputations": "Picking up many melee weapons causes amputations"). A held item was as long as its hand drawings are wide, and one held in both hands lay across the waist as a rifle does. A blade drawn standing in the hand is narrow in every drawing: X-Com Files' greatsword came out five cubes at the chest, and both arms, reaching for it, lay folded inside the body. An item drawn taller than wide in every hand drawing (1.5 times) now stands up, as long as it is drawn high, one hand at its lower end; the other arm is as its own drawings have it (`placeItem`, `ItemPlace::upright`).
- *Voxel units*: **no gun metal on the clothes** (GitHub issue #197, "greasy guns stain clothes": "gun model merges into body, dying voxels of body grey"). A body's cube whose own part isn't drawn at its place in a drawing took the colour of the part on top there, and where a weapon is drawn over the body that was the weapon's. It takes a body part's colour only (`drawnColour`).
- *Voxel units*: **a tank is a hull with its turret on it** (GitHub issue #188: the tank "looks almost like a dopey pyramid with no protruding barrel ... turret, and body as seperate voxel models stacked"). Hull and turret were made from one set of drawings, and as those are all made from the same height above the tank, what they left of it was a low cone on a cone. The turret's drawings are now taken apart from the hull's (`modelFor`), and each is a body of its own: the hull as its drawings leave it, cut flat where it narrows to half its widest, above and below (`flattenHull`: a deck, sloping sides, the tracks); the turret a plain block with its barrel, as a turret on a vehicle is, standing on the deck; both painted from all eight drawings. For every big unit that moves and has a turret (the tanks and hover tanks of UFO, the mods' own); the Cyberdisc, the walkers and a turret on a vehicle are made as before.
- *Voxel units*: **human bodies sculpted from their frontal drawings** (the owner: the voxeliser "should maybe really look at the sprite frontals more (more than side positions) and carefully craft body waist arms head and legs ... as drawn in sprites"). A body was what its eight drawings agree on, a little forgiving of each: the side drawings left the gap between an arm and the waist filled and the legs joined below the hips, a heavier figure than the one drawn. For the units the unit table names (`sculpt: frontal`, the PIR units), nothing of the body stands outside what the front drawing or the back drawing shows; the side drawings give it its depth and no more. On XPiratez's gals what the body had beyond its front drawing went from 11-17% of the drawing to 6-8%.
- *Voxel units*: **in every pose a body is as its sprite is** (the owner: "voxel models should generally follow the sprites as not to change the game engine underneath and playstyle"). A kneeling body is as high as its kneeling drawing, as the sprite is, though the game has a kneeling unit lower than it is drawn (XPiratez's gals: drawn 21 pixels high, a kneeling height of 14); pressing it down to that height (tried for GitHub issue #192) is not done.
- *Voxel units*, *See-through corpses*: **a body lying on the floor stays solid when you look over it** (GitHub issue #190: "looking straight ahead over them, the units become invisible despite not looking at the voxel MODEL"). A lying body was see-through whenever the aim's line passed over it on the ground and ended beyond it, however high above it. Now only where it is in the way: the line passes within 6 voxels of its top, or ends on a unit standing behind it, whose legs it hides.
- **Water lying flat fills its tile** (GitHub issue #194: "Water tiles don't completely span across ... should cleanly tile, with 0 gaps in between"). A floor's picture is drawn a pixel row lower than a voxel's pixel is read, so a floor without shape, made from its picture, had no voxels along its north edge and at every other voxel of its west edge: what was under it showed in a line along every tile. Those voxels take the pixel below theirs (`buildGhost`).
- *Voxel units*: **XPiratez's gals are slim again** (GitHub issue #184, "Gals got fat": "more like a dad bod than an uber"). A torso is as deep as its side drawing, row by row, and the gals' torsos are drawn from the side with hips up to 10 pixels deep on a body 8 wide: that part of the drawing lies under the arm, hand on hip, and is never seen in the sprite; as a body's depth it was a belly out past the chest. In 100 of the 238 gal sheets the lower torso is drawn deeper than wide. For the units the unit table names (`torsoDepth: 80`: the armors and sheets named PIR), a torso below the shoulders is at most 80% as deep as it is wide, cut at the front (`headAndTorso`).
- *Voxel units*: **no bald spot** (GitHub issue #181, "unit seems to be given bald spot"). What faces up was painted along the slant of the front drawing, and at the front of a head's top that slant ends on the forehead: two or three cubes of skin on the crown, on XPiratez's humans, whose faces are drawn high on the head. For the units the unit table names (`top: back`, the same PIR units), what faces up is painted by the back drawing: from behind a head is hair all over.
- *Voxel units*: **dogs and cats stand on four legs** (GitHub issues #191, attack dogs "look like extremely adorable capybaras with stumpy legs", and #180, "cats aint got no legs"). A body drawn on one frame was read as one standing upright: row by row from its frontal drawing. A long animal's frontal drawing has its far end for its top, so each pair of legs came out one block, the gap under the belly was filled and the feet cut off; and the body was painted from its front and back drawings alone, which show nothing of its flanks: one tan barrel. A long body is now made from its side drawings (`profile`): their outline along the body, the other views trimming its width; the belly raised between the legs by what the drawings' slant adds to it (35% of the body's width), the legs going up to there; each pair of legs kept to the columns the front and back drawings have them in, so four legs stay four; painted by all eight drawings, each cube by the one that sees it best (a dog's dark flank on its light legs); and it walks at a trot, each leg about its own hip. Only the units the mod's rules name a beast are made so (see the next entry): the dogs, cats, rats, pigs, sheep, chupacabras, maggots, scorpions, spiders and beetles of XPiratez and The X-Com Files; every other unit is made as before, cube for cube.
- *Voxel units*: **units told by their names** (the owner: "this mod is heavily used with pirates and xcom files ... Lets have broad name detection for voxel units"). A new table in the mod's rules, `units:` (see [Units by name](#units-by-name)): words looked for in a unit's type, armor, sprite sheet and race, and what they say. Two things so far: **ghosts are seen through** (`seeThrough: 50`: XPiratez's ghost, The X-Com Files' ghosts, poltergeist and phantasmal tentacles; every other cube drawn), and **beasts** (`shape: beast`), the animals made as long bodies on four legs. What is done for one kind of unit is done for the units named and for no others (the owner: a fix for a ghost or a dog mustn't "break other voxel units that are good allready like sectoid, snakeman, mutton ... if we dont have unit name in table we will apply some general rules").
- *Voxel units*: **a unit drawn as a dither gets a body** (GitHub issue #185: XPiratez's ghosts "don't seem to make a model at all"). The stealth and spectre suits are drawn with every other pixel left out, so the map shows through them; a body made of those pictures lost nearly all its cells (a cell with too few neighbours goes) and only a scrap was left, the weapon floating beside it. The holes of such pictures are filled before the body is made (`fillDither`: a sheet counts as dithered from 33 holes for 100 pixels drawn; the suits have 69 to 87, any other sheet 9 at most), and the body is drawn half see-through, as the map shows it (`VoxelDitherFaint`).
- **A unit killed in the air plummets** (GitHub issue #187: "it does the old teleport to floor effect"). The fall was timed from the killing hit, but the game first turns the unit to the screen, a step for each eighth of a turn, and only then starts its collapse: a Floater facing the soldier had "landed" before it was drawn going down, and was on the ground at once. Sprites and voxel bodies alike. The fall is timed from when the collapse starts (`startAirFall`).
- **A Cyberdisc or hover tank straight overhead is drawn again.** Since a hull's picture lies back 50 degrees it turned its back on a soldier below it and wasn't drawn at all, though the crosshair still named it. A hull above the eye leans towards the eye as any other unit does: the lying back fades out over 30 degrees of height above the eye (`cardTilt`, `HullLayBackFade`).
- **Voxel units and the Meta Quest 3 build are in the main line now** (they were on a branch of their own): *Voxel units* is an experimental switch on the first Settings page, off by default, and with it off everything is as before.
- *Voxel units*: **a burning body's flames show on the GPU renderer too** (GitHub issue #186, "new fire effect only triggers on CPU"). The tongues of flame on a burning voxel body were drawn by the CPU renderer only; with *Renderer* GPU just the sparks and smoke showed.
- **The flashlight and the flares are always there: the settings *Flashlight* and *Unlimited tactical flares* are gone** (the owner: "Flashlight: assume on dynamic. The other light is personal lights, and can be toggled off. Also flashlight has a key binding"). The flashlight is the light on the weapon, a spot on the crosshair by day and night (the wide shoulder beam, the old *On*, is gone); **F** switches it and **T** throws a flare, and either key can be unbound.
- **Zoom is always there: the setting *Zoom* is gone** (the owner: "Its on a control, just don't hit it"). Hold **U**, or the right button in mouse look, as before.
- **Night vision for everyone: the setting *Night vision needs gear* is gone** (the owner: "Nobody uses the option night vision needs gear... Night vision is available period"). Every unit has the NIGHT VISION button and the N key in 3D, as on the isometric map; the mod's `nightVision:` list of armors and items is no longer read.
- **Night vision on the GPU renderer: no dark red band along the horizon.** With *True colour* and *Smooth light*, at night, the far ground and the horizon showed dark red through the goggles. The goggles show every colour by its shade alone, and much of a night scene is picked by shade only: the sky's and the haze's colours, and the fixed "black" a colour past its darkest shade falls back to, which in the palette's own colours is a dark red. The palette's picture hid that (the goggles' wash recolours it); the full-colour picture showed each one's own red. In full colour every colour is now taken as that shade of the goggles' own (`paletteRgb` in the rays' shader). The CPU renderer was right already.
- **Smoke through night vision has no neon rings** (GitHub issue #183: smoke "often gets neon green halos which weird out players a little, thinking it's an energy aura"). Smoke was mixed over the world by the see-through tables, which give the nearest colour of any group, and the goggles show a colour by its shade alone, which says nothing of how bright that colour was: dark smoke over a dark night glowed in bright green swirls and rings. Through night vision the smoke's shade (kept between a middle and a dark green: `SmokeNightBrightest`, `SmokeNightDarkest`) is now mixed with the shade of what is behind it, by the numbers, in the goggles' colours: a soft haze. CPU and GPU alike (measured on a night smoke cloud: half as many bright pixels on both).
- **A unit killed in the air no longer rises again.** It comes down while the game plays its fall frames up where it hovered; a short drop was over before those frames were, and for the rest of them it was drawn back up at its tile: a body that dropped, then rose, then lay on the ground. Now it stays where it landed (`airFallHeight`). Found while checking the voxel bodies of flyers; the picture did the same.
- **Zoom, 8 times** (GitHub issue #176: 4 times is "a bit anemic for large maps and sniper squad sight"; the owner: "Zoom + space at maxed zoom goes to 8x"). Zoomed in, holding the *Jump* key (Space) glides the view on to 8 times closer; the key doesn't jump while zoomed. `ZoomBoostFactor`, `updateZoom`.
- **Reflections on window glass** (GitHub issue #174, "Gpu option for reflections on glass panes"): with *Reflections* (and *True colour*, on the GPU renderer) a pane mirrors what is in front of it: one ray off the pane's face from where the eye's ray went in, what it meets in the game's light there or the sky, mixed in from 8% seen straight on to more than half along the pane. The reflections' program is used on maps with a sea or with window glass (`_anyGlass`). Not through night-vision goggles.
- **A warning on the Renderer setting** (GitHub issue #171, a long freeze on the first switch to GPU on Windows): the card builds its programs once, on the first frame; the description now says so in the second colour.
- **A melee blow at a wall no longer turns the soldier away from it** (GitHub issue #170, "melee turn whiplash against windows", "particularly an issue with north, turning someone southeast"). For a blow at a wall, door or object the soldier turned to the tile the part belongs to; a wall ahead is the north or west wall of some tile, which can lie beside the soldier (the window ahead and a little to the right is the north wall of the tile to the east: the soldier turned east). Now the soldier keeps the facing while the point struck lies ahead (within 67.5 degrees to either side), and else turns to the eighth the point itself lies in (`facingTo`). Found by reading the code against the report; the clip itself could not be watched.
- **A big hull's picture lies back** (a tank, a turret, a hover tank, a Cyberdisc, TFTD's Coelacanth and Displacer: the two-by-two vehicles, and any two-by-two unit that can't move; the owner's photo of XPiratez's truck, its turret standing on the roof like a lid turned to the soldier). Their pictures are drawn from above, the roof showing, and stood upright like every unit's, tipping with the view. Now a hull's card lies back 50 degrees whatever the view's pitch (further only while the view looks down further), so the roof lies as a roof and the hull stands low; the frame is still the one of the side it is seen from, so a turret keeps the way it was left facing as the soldier walks round it, and is turned as any unit is.
